Network Devices
Network Devices are the devices required for communication between computers on a computer network.


Enable communication by transmitting and receiving data between devices.
Allow devices to connect to networks efficiently and securely.
Improve network performance by reducing congestion and managing traffic.
Extend network coverage and solve signal loss

Layer 1 Devices: Physical Layer

These devices deal with raw electrical  signals (bits). These devices don't understand IP addresses or MAC addresses.

1. Hub

It is a central connection point for devices in a LAN.
It takes an incoming signal on one port and blindly broadcasts it to all other ports
High network traffic collisions and security risks (everyone sees everyone's data).

2. Repeater

It regenerates signals to extend the range of a network.
It receives a weak signal (due to attenuation over long cables), amplifies it, and retransmits it.
Extending WiFi range beyond 100 meters.


3. Modem (Modulator-Demodulator)

This bridges the gap between digital computer data and analog signals, allowing devices to connect to the internet via telephone, cable, or fiber lines.

Connects your home network to the ISP (Internet Service Provider).
Converts digital signals from your computer into analog signals for telephone/cable lines



Layer 2 Devices: Data Link Layer

These devices work with MAC Addresses (Physical Addresses). They are smarter than hubs.

4. Switch

A switch is a high-speed networking device that connects devices (computers, printers, servers) within a Local Area Network (LAN),
Unlike hub, switch learns the MAC address of every connected device.
It sends data only to the specific port
Reduces collisions and improves security and speed.

5. Bridge

This connects two separate LAN segments to make them appear as one.
Filters traffic based on MAC addresses to keep local traffic local, reducing congestion

6. Access Point (AP)

It is a networking hardware device that allows Wi-Fi-enabled devices, such as laptops and smartphones, to connect to a wired network.
Acts as a bridge between wired Ethernet and wireless Wi-Fi devices.
An AP only provides Wi-Fi signal and it does not route traffic or assign IP addresses.

Layer 3 Devices: Network Layer
These devices work with IP Addresses (Logical Addresses) and connect different networks together.

7. Router

It is a networking device that connects multiple networks and directs data packets between them using IP addresses.
It uses IP Addresses to determine the best path for data packets to travel
Routers stop broadcast traffic, isolating networks from each other.

8. Brouter (Bridging Router)

It is a networking device combining bridge and router functions, operating at both the Data Link and Network layers of the OSI model.
It can route known protocols (like TCP/IP).
It bridge unknown protocols and rarely used nowadays.

Layer 4-7 Devices: Advanced Processing

9. Gateway

It is a hardware device or software node that acts as an entry/exit point between two distinct networks using different protocols,
It can convert protocols, data formats,
An Internet Gateway translates your private LAN requests into public internet requests.

10. Firewall

It is a network security system, comprising hardware, software, or both that monitors and controls incoming and outgoing network traffic based on predetermined security rules

Hub vs. Switch vs. Router

Feature	          Hub	                     Switch	              Router
Layer	      Layer 1 (Physical)	          Layer 2 (Data Link)	        Layer 3 (Network)
Address Type	None (Bits)	MAC Address	IP Address
Data Flow	Broadcast (to all)	Unicast (to destination)	Route (best path)
Intelligence	      Dumb	             Smart	                       Smarter
Used For	Obsolete	Connecting Devices (LAN)	Connecting Networks (WAN)


Types of Computer Networks


Computer networks can be classified based on three factors:

Geographical Area (How large the network is)
Transmission Medium (How data is transmitted)
Ownership and Access Control (Who owns and can access the network)

1. Classification Based on Geographical Area

This classification depends on how much physical area the network covers.

A) Personal Area Network (PAN)
A Personal Area Network (PAN) is the smallest type of network. It connects personal devices within a very short distance around a single person.
Coverage: 1–10 meters

Technologies Used:

Bluetooth
NFC (Near Field Communication)
Infrared

Example
Smartphone connected to wireless earbuds
Smartwatch connected to a phone

When Used
Whenever personal devices need to communicate over a short distance.

B) Local Area Network (LAN)
A Local Area Network (LAN) connects computers and devices within a small area such as a home, office, school, or building.
Coverage: One room, building, or small campus

Technologies Used:
Ethernet
Wi-Fi

Example
Home Wi-Fi
School computer lab
Office network

When Used
To share:
Internet
Files
Printers
Software

within a local location.

C) Campus Area Network (CAN)
Definition

A Campus Area Network (CAN) connects multiple LANs within the same campus or organization.

Coverage: University, hospital, or corporate campus

Technologies Used:

Ethernet
Fiber Optics
Example

A university where:

Library LAN
Hostel LAN
Administration LAN

are connected together.

When Used

To connect different buildings belonging to the same organization.

D) Metropolitan Area Network (MAN)
Definition

A Metropolitan Area Network (MAN) connects networks across a city or metropolitan area.

Coverage: Entire city

Technologies Used

Fiber Optics
Metro Ethernet
Microwave

Example
City-wide Internet Service Provider (ISP)
Cable TV network
When Used

To provide high-speed networking across a city.

E) Wide Area Network (WAN)
Definition

A Wide Area Network (WAN) connects networks across countries or continents.

Coverage: Country, continent, or worldwide

Technologies Used

Internet
Satellite
Leased Lines

Example

The Internet
Bank networks connecting branches across India
Railway reservation system

When Used

Whenever long-distance communication is required.

2. Classification Based on Transmission Medium

This classification depends on how data travels between devices.

There are two types.

A) Broadcast Network
Definition

A Broadcast Network uses one shared communication channel for all devices.

When one device sends data:

Every device receives it.
Only the intended receiver accepts and processes it.
Others ignore it.

Characteristics
One shared communication medium
One-to-many communication
No dedicated path

Examples
Bus Ethernet
Wi-Fi

B) Point-to-Point Network
Definition

A Point-to-Point Network provides a dedicated path between sender and receiver

Characteristics
One-to-one communication
Dedicated links
Routing algorithms decide the best path
More secure and efficient

Examples
Internet
Telephone network

3. Classification Based on Ownership and Access Control

This classification depends on who owns the network and who can use it.

A) Private Network
Definition

A Private Network is owned and managed by a person or organization.

Only authorized users can access it.

Features
High security
Requires login
Internal communication
Controlled by one organization

Examples
Company LAN
Corporate intranet
Private cloud

B) Public Network
Definition

A Public Network is available for everyone.

Usually owned by:

Internet Service Providers (ISPs)
Government
Telecom companies
Features
Open access
Large number of users
Less secure
Can be wired or wireless

Examples
Internet
Public Wi-Fi
Mobile network

C) Hybrid Network
Definition

A Hybrid Network combines private and public networks.

Some resources are private while others are accessible over the public Internet.

Features
Combines security and accessibility
Supports external users securely
Common in modern organizations

Examples
Company network connected to the Internet
Online banking systems

Intranet
Definition

An Intranet is a private network used only inside an organization.

Only employees or authorized users can access it.

Features
Private
Login required
Internal communication
Secure

Uses
Employee portal

Examples
University portal

Extranet
Definition

An Extranet is an extension of an intranet that allows trusted external users (such as partners, suppliers, vendors, or customers) to access specific resources securely.

Features
Partially private
Secure login required
Uses VPN or secure web access
Supports collaboration with external organizations

Uses
Customer portals
Supplier management
Partner collaboration
Online banking

Examples
Vendor access portal
banking portal

Network Models

Computer networks rely on these foundational frameworks to standardize communication. It explains the layered structures of the OSI and TCP/IP models and highlights how they organize network functions for efficient data flow.
OSI MODEL
Layers of OSI Model

The OSI (Open Systems Interconnection) Model is a 7-layer conceptual model that explains how data travels from one computer to another over a network. Each layer has a specific responsibility, making network communication organized

7. Application
6. Presentation
5. Session
4. Transport
3. Network
2. Data Link
1. Physical

Layer 1: Physical Layer

This is the lowest layer of the OSI model. It is responsible for transmitting raw bits (0s and 1s) over a physical medium such as cables, fiber optics, or wireless signals. It defines how devices are physically connected and the speed of data transmission. Devices like hubs, repeaters, modems, and cables work at this layer.

Example
When you plug an Ethernet cable into your laptop, the Physical Layer sends electrical signals through the cable.

Layer 2: Data Link Layer (DLL)

The Data Link Layer provides reliable communication between two directly connected devices. It adds MAC (physical) addresses, detects transmission errors, controls the flow of data, Devices such as switches and bridges operate at this layer.

Example

When your laptop sends data to your Wi-Fi router, the Data Link Layer ensures the packets of data reaches the correct device using MAC addresses.

Layer 3: Network Layer

The Network Layer is responsible for moving data between different networks. It assigns IP addresses, determines the best route for data, and forwards packets through routers. This is the layer that makes communication across the Internet possible.

Example

When you open www.google.com, the Network Layer routes your data through multiple routers until it reaches Google's server.

Device: Router

PDU: Packets

Layer 4: Transport Layer

The Transport Layer provides end-to-end communication between applications. It breaks large messages into smaller segments, reassembles them at the destination, ensures reliable delivery with TCP, or provides faster but less reliable delivery with UDP

Example

When watching YouTube, video streaming often uses UDP for speed, while downloading a file uses TCP to ensure every part arrives correctly.

Layer 5: Session Layer

The Session Layer manages the communication session between two applications. It establishes, maintains, synchronizes, and terminates sessions, and can insert checkpoints so interrupted transfers can resume instead of restarting.

Example

During a video call on Zoom or Google Meet, this layer helps maintain the communication session until the call ends.

PDU: Data

Layer 6: Presentation Layer

The Presentation Layer is often called the Translation Layer. It formats data performs encryption/decryption, and compresses/decompresses data so that different systems can understand each other.

Example

When you access a secure website using HTTPS, encryption (TLS/SSL) happens at this layer.

PDU: Data

Layer 7: Application Layer

The Application Layer is the topmost layer and is the one users interact with directly. It provides network services to applications such as web browsers, email clients, and file transfer software. Common protocols include HTTP, HTTPS, SMTP, FTP, DNS, and DHCP.

Example

When you open Chrome and visit a website

How Data Flows Through the OSI Model

Suppose you send an email to a friend.

Application Layer: You type the email and click Send.
Presentation Layer: The email is formatted and encrypted.
Session Layer: A communication session with the mail server is established.
Transport Layer: The email is divided into smaller segments.
Network Layer: IP addresses are added, and the best route is chosen.
Data Link Layer: Packets are converted into frames, and MAC addresses are added.
Physical Layer: Frames are converted into electrical, optical, or wireless signals and transmitted.

At the receiver's end, the process happens in reverse until the email is displayed in the recipient's inbox

TCP/IP Model

The TCP/IP (Transmission Control Protocol/Internet Protocol) Model is the networking model used by the Internet. It explains how data is sent from one device to another using 4 layers.
Unlike the OSI model (7 layers), TCP/IP has only 4 layers, making it simpler and more practical for real-world networking.

Why is it called TCP/IP?

It is named after its two most important protocols:

TCP (Transmission Control Protocol) → Ensures reliable communication.
IP (Internet Protocol) → Finds the destination and routes the data

Layers of TCP/IP Model
+---------------------------+
| Application Layer         |
+---------------------------+
| Transport Layer           |
+---------------------------+
| Internet Layer            |
+---------------------------+
| Network Access Layer      |
+---------------------------+

1. Application Layer

This is the top layer.
It is the layer where users interact with the network.

Examples:
Chrome
Firefox
Gmail
Outlook
WhatsApp

Responsibilities
Creates data
Provides services to applications
Handles formatting
Manages sessions
Provides encryption

Protocols
HTTP
HTTPS
FTP
SMTP
DNS

Example

You open Chrome and search:

www.google.com

Chrome sends an HTTP request using the Application Layer.

1. HTTP (HyperText Transfer Protocol)
Definition

HTTP is the protocol used to transfer web pages between a web browser and a web server.

It allows your browser to request a webpage and the server to send it back.

How HTTP Works
You type:
www.google.com

        │
        ▼
Browser sends HTTP Request
        │
        ▼
Google Web Server
        │
        ▼
Server sends HTTP Response
        │
        ▼
Browser displays webpage

Characteristics
Used for websites
Works on a request-response model

Advantages
Fast
Simple
Lightweight

Disadvantages
No encryption
Passwords and data can be intercepted

2. HTTPS (HyperText Transfer Protocol Secure)
Definition

HTTPS is the secure version of HTTP.

It encrypts all communication between the browser and the server.

How HTTPS Works
Browser
     │
Encrypted Request 🔒
     │
     ▼
Secure Web Server
     │
Encrypted Response 🔒
     ▼
Browser

Characteristics
Uses SSL/TLS encryption
Secure communication
Protects passwords and banking information
Default Port: 443

Example
https://www.amazon.com
https://www.google.com
https://bankofbaroda.in

Notice the lock icon (🔒) in the browser.

Uses
Online banking
Shopping websites
Email services
Login pages

What is SSL/TLS Encryption?

SSL (Secure Sockets Layer) and TLS (Transport Layer Security) are security protocols that encrypt data sent between a client (browser) and a server (website).
TLS is the newer and more secure version of SSL. Although SSL has been replaced by TLS, people still commonly say "SSL certificate" or "SSL encryption."
The main purpose of SSL/TLS is to make sure that no one can read, modify, or impersonate the communication between you and a website.
Encryption converts readable information (plaintext) into unreadable information (ciphertext) using a mathematical algorithm and a key.

Public Key and Private Key

SSL/TLS uses asymmetric encryption initially.

There are two keys:

Public Key
Can be shared with everyone.
Used to encrypt data.
Private Key
Secret and stored securely on the server.
Used to decrypt data encrypted with the matching public key.

Example:

Public Key
      │
Encrypt Data
      │
      ▼
Encrypted Data
      │
      ▼
Private Key
      │
Decrypt Data
      ▼
Original Data

Why Doesn't TLS Use Public-Key Encryption for Everything?

Public-key encryption is secure but slow.
After the browser and server securely exchange a shared secret, they switch to symmetric encryption, where both sides use the same secret key. Symmetric encryption is much faster and is used for the rest of the session.

3. FTP (File Transfer Protocol)
Definition

FTP is used to transfer files between computers over a network.

You can:

Upload files
Download files
Rename files
Delete files
How FTP Works

Your Computer
      │
 Upload/Download Files
      │
      ▼
FTP Server

Example

A web developer uploads a website to a hosting server using FTP.

Characteristics
Transfers files
Can upload and download
Username and password may be required

Default Ports:
21 (control connection)
20 (data transfer)

Uses
Website hosting
File backup
Sharing large files

Disadvantages
Normal FTP is not encrypted, so secure alternatives like SFTP or FTPS are often preferred.

4. SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol)
Definition

SMTP is the protocol used to send emails.

It transfers emails from:

Sender → Mail server
Mail server → Mail server
How SMTP Works

You
 │
 ▼
Gmail
 │
SMTP
 │
 ▼
Receiver's Mail Server
 │
 ▼
Receiver

Example

When you send an email:

To: friend@gmail.com
Subject: Hello

SMTP sends the email to Gmail's mail server, which then forwards it to the recipient's mail server.

Characteristics
Used only for sending emails
Reliable
Uses mail servers

Common Ports:
25
587
465 (secure)

Important

SMTP does not retrieve emails.

Receiving emails is done using:

POP3
IMAP

IMAP (Internet Message Access Protocol)
Definition

IMAP (Internet Message Access Protocol) is an email protocol used to access and manage emails directly on the mail server.

Unlike POP3, IMAP does not download and remove emails by default. Instead, it keeps the emails on the server and synchronizes all your devices.

How IMAP Works
               Mail Server
              /     |      \
             /      |       \
        Laptop   Phone   Tablet

All devices connect to the same mailbox.

Characteristics
Emails remain on the server.
Synchronizes emails across multiple devices.
Read, unread, deleted, and folder changes are reflected everywhere.
Requires an Internet connection to view and sync emails

Advantages
Supports multiple devices.
Keeps emails backed up on the server.

Disadvantages
Requires more server storage.
Usually needs an Internet connection for full functionality.
Slightly more complex than POP3.

POP3 (Post Office Protocol Version 3)
Definition

POP3 (Post Office Protocol Version 3) is an email protocol used to receive (download) emails from a mail server to your device.

Characteristics
Downloads emails from the server.
Mainly stores emails locally on your device.
Can delete emails from the server after download.
Best suited for one device.
Requires an Internet connection only while downloading.

Default Ports
110 → POP3
995 → Secure POP3 (POP3S using SSL/TLS)
Example

Suppose you receive an email.

Friend
   │
SMTP
   ▼
Gmail Server
   │
POP3
   ▼
Your Laptop

The email is downloaded to your laptop. If you later check your phone, the email may not be there if it was removed from the server.

Advantages
Fast and simple.
Emails are available offline after downloading.
Uses less server storage.

Disadvantages
Poor support for multiple devices.
Emails may be removed from the server.
Folders and read/unread status are not synchronized across devices.

5. DNS (Domain Name System)
Definition
DNS is known as the Internet's Phonebook.
It converts domain names into IP addresses.

Why is DNS Needed?
Humans remember names:
www.google.com
Computers communicate using IP addresses:
142.250.xxx.xxx
DNS converts the name into the corresponding IP address.

How DNS Works
You type:

www.google.com

        │
        ▼
DNS Server

        │
Converts Name
        │
        ▼

142.250.xxx.xxx

        │
        ▼
Browser connects to Google Server

Characteristics
Converts domain names into IP addresses
Makes websites easy to access
Essential for web browsing

Default Port: 53
Example

Instead of remembering:
142.250.195.78
you simply type:
www.google.com
DNS finds the correct IP address automatically.

2. Transport Layer

This layer ensures that data reaches the destination correctly.

Its responsibilities are:

Break data into segments
Reassemble data
Error checking

It uses two protocols:

TCP
UDP

TCP (Transmission Control Protocol)

TCP is used when accuracy is more important than speed.
Imagine sending an important assignment to your professor.

You want:

Nothing missing
Correct order
Error-free delivery

TCP provides this.

Examples
Website loading
File download
Email
Internet banking

UDP (User Datagram Protocol)

UDP is used when speed is more important than perfect accuracy.
Imagine watching a live cricket match.

If one video frame is lost,
you don't want the stream to pause waiting for it.
It simply continues.

Features
✔ Fast
✔ Connectionless
✔ No retransmission

Examples
Online games
Video calls
Voice calls
Live streaming

3. Internet Layer

This layer is responsible for delivering packets to the correct destination.

Its responsibilities are:

IP addressing
Routing
Packet forwarding
Finding the best path

Protocols
IP
ICMP
ARP

Example

Suppose:

Laptop
↓
Wi-Fi Router
↓
ISP
↓
Google Server

The Internet Layer decides the best route.
Think of it like Google Maps.

1. IP (Internet Protocol)
Definition

IP (Internet Protocol) is the protocol responsible for addressing and routing data packets from the sender to the correct destination across one or more networks.

Think of IP as the GPS of the Internet.

Why is IP Needed?

Every device connected to a network has an IP address.

Example:

Laptop          : 192.168.1.10
Router          : 192.168.1.1
Google Server   : 142.250.xxx.xxx

When you visit Google:

Your Laptop
      │
      ▼
Router
      │
      ▼
Internet
      │
      ▼
Google Server

IP ensures the packet reaches the correct destination.

Functions of IP
Assigns IP addresses
Identifies sender and receiver
Routes packets
Delivers packets across networks

2. ICMP (Internet Control Message Protocol)
Definition

ICMP (Internet Control Message Protocol) is used for error reporting
Unlike IP, ICMP does not carry user data (such as webpages or emails). Instead, it carries control and error messages about network conditions.

Why is ICMP Needed?

Suppose you try to visit a website.

Laptop
   │
   ▼
Router
   │
❌ Destination Unreachable

The router sends an ICMP message back saying:

Destination Unreachable

This helps identify network problems.

Example

When you run:

ping google.com

Your computer sends:

Echo Request

Google replies:

Echo Reply

If replies are received, the network connection is working.

Characteristics
Error reporting
Network diagnostics

3. ARP (Address Resolution Protocol)
Definition

ARP (Address Resolution Protocol) is used to find the MAC address of a device when its IP address is already known.

Remember:

IP Address → Identifies a device on the network.
MAC Address → Identifies the network interface on the local LAN.
Why is ARP Needed?

Suppose your computer wants to send data to the router.

It knows the router's IP:

192.168.1.1

But to send the frame on the local network, it needs the router's MAC address.

How ARP Works
Step 1

Computer knows:

Router IP = 192.168.1.1

It doesn't know:

Router MAC = ?
Step 2

The computer broadcasts an ARP Request:

Who has IP address 192.168.1.1?
Tell 192.168.1.20

Every device on the LAN receives this request.

Step 3

Only the router responds:

I have IP 192.168.1.1

My MAC Address is

AA:BB:CC:DD:EE:FF
Step 4

The computer stores the MAC address in its ARP cache and uses it to send frames.

Laptop
   │
Destination MAC:
AA:BB:CC:DD:EE:FF
   │
Router

Characteristics
Converts IP Address → MAC Address
Works only on the local network (LAN)
Uses broadcasts for ARP Requests

4. Network Access Layer (Link Layer)

This is the lowest layer.

It sends data over:

Ethernet cable
Wi-Fi
Fiber
Wireless

Responsibilities:

Converts packets into frames
Uses MAC addresses
Detects transmission errors

Working of TCP/IP

Suppose you open www.youtube.com.

Step 1 — Application Layer

You type:

www.youtube.com

The browser creates an HTTP request.

↓

Step 2 — Transport Layer

TCP breaks the request into smaller segments.

It adds:

Port number

Step 3 — Internet Layer

IP adds:

Source IP
Destination IP

It chooses the best route.

↓

Step 4 — Network Access Layer

Packets become frames.

MAC addresses are added.

The data is sent through:

Wi-Fi
Ethernet
Fiber

↓

Internet

↓

Server

At the Server

The process happens in reverse.

Network Access
↓

Internet

↓

Transport

↓

Application

Finally,

YouTube sends the webpage back

Advantages
1. Widely Used
The entire Internet uses TCP/IP.
2. Platform Independent

Works on:
Windows
Linux
macOS
Android
iPhone

3. Reliable
TCP ensures:
No missing data
Correct order
Error detection
Error correction

4. Scalable
Can work for:
Home Wi-Fi

Disadvantages

1. Complex
There are many protocols to learn.

2. Layer Boundaries
Unlike the OSI model, some responsibilities overlap between layers.

4. Security
Basic TCP/IP doesn't include strong built-in security.
Extra protocols are needed:

HTTPS
TLS
SSL

Why is TCP/IP Preferred Over OSI?
Simpler: Only 4 layers instead of 7.
Practical: Built around protocols that are actually used on the Internet.
Flexible: Works across different hardware and network technologies.
Open Standard: Free and universally adopted.
Real-World Use: The Internet and most modern networks are based on TCP/IP

Physical Layer (Layer 1) of the OSI Model

The Physical Layer is the first (lowest) layer of the OSI model. It is responsible for transmitting raw bits (0s and 1s) from one device to another through a physical medium such as cables, fiber optics, or wireless signals.
It does not understand data like files, emails, or webpages. It only deals with electrical signals.

Functions of the Physical Layer
1. Transmits Raw Bits
The Physical Layer transfers only bits (0s and 1s) between devices.

Example:

101101001011010
It does not know whether these bits represent a photo, email, or video.

2. Encoding and Decoding
Encoding

The sender converts binary data into signals.

Example:

Binary

10110010

↓
Electrical Signals

Decoding

The receiver converts the received signals back into binary.

Electrical Signals

↓

10110010

Without encoding and decoding, computers could not understand each other's signals.

3. Modulation and Demodulation

These are mainly used in wireless communication and modems.
Modulation
Converts digital data into signals suitable for transmission.

Example:

Computer

↓

Wi-Fi Signal

↓

Router

Demodulation
Converts the received signal back into digital data.

Router

↓

Computer

4. Controls Data Transmission
The Physical Layer determines:
Data transmission speed (bit rate)

Protocols Used in the Physical Layer

The Physical Layer uses standards that define how signals are transmitted.

1. Ethernet (IEEE 802.3)
Used for wired communication.

Example:

Computer
   │
Ethernet Cable
   │
Router

Characteristics:

High speed
Reliable
Uses RJ-45 connectors

2. Wi-Fi (IEEE 802.11)
Used for wireless communication.

Example:

Laptop )))))) Router

Characteristics:

No cables
Uses radio waves
Common in homes and offices

3. Bluetooth (IEEE 802.15.1)
Short-range wireless communication.

Examples:

Phone ↔ Earbuds
Laptop ↔ Mouse
Keyboard ↔ Computer

Range:

Approximately 10 meters.

4. USB (Universal Serial Bus)

Used to connect devices over short distances.

Examples:

Pendrive
Keyboard

Need of security in Physical Layer
Many security attacks happen at the Physical Layer because attackers target the actual hardware or transmission medium.

1. Cable Tapping
An attacker connects to a network cable and secretly copies transmitted data.

Example:

Computer
     │
     │
Attacker
     │
Router

The attacker may capture sensitive information if the data is not encrypted.

2. Physical Access

If someone gains access to a server room, they can:

Steal servers
Disconnect cables
Damage equipment
Install malicious devices

This is why organizations use:

Locked server rooms
CCTV

3. Wireless Signal Interception
Attackers may capture Wi-Fi signals from outside a building.
Using strong Wi-Fi encryption (such as WPA2 or WPA3) helps protect against this.

4. Hardware Manipulation
An attacker may tamper with devices like:

Routers
Switches
USB ports

Example:
Plugging an infected USB drive into a computer can install malware.

WPA2 and WPA3 Explained

WPA2 and WPA3 are Wi-Fi security protocols used to protect wireless networks from unauthorized access. They encrypt the data transmitted between your device and the Wi-Fi router, making it difficult for attackers to read or steal the information.

WPA2 = Wi-Fi Protected Access 2 (introduced in 2004)
WPA3 = Wi-Fi Protected Access 3 (introduced in 2018)
WPA3 is the newer, more secure version of WPA2.

What is WPA2?
Definition

WPA2 (Wi-Fi Protected Access 2) is a Wi-Fi security standard that uses AES (Advanced Encryption Standard) to encrypt data between devices and the Wi-Fi router.

How WPA2 Works
You select your Wi-Fi network.
You enter the Wi-Fi password.
The router verifies the password.
If the password is correct, an encrypted connection is established.

Laptop
   │ Password
   ▼
Wi-Fi Router
   │
AES Encryption 🔒
   │
Internet

Features of WPA2
Uses AES encryption.
Requires a Wi-Fi password.

Advantages
Strong encryption.
Supported by most routers and devices.
Easy to configure.

Limitations
Uses a shared password (Pre-Shared Key) in home networks. If someone learns the password, they can join the network.
Susceptible to offline password-guessing attacks if users choose weak passwords.

What is WPA3?
Definition

WPA3 (Wi-Fi Protected Access 3) is the latest Wi-Fi security standard. It improves on WPA2 by providing stronger authentication, better encryption, and increased protection against password-guessing attacks.

How WPA3 Works

The process is similar:

Connect to the Wi-Fi network.
Enter the password.
WPA3 uses a stronger authentication method called SAE (Simultaneous Authentication of Equals).
A secure encrypted connection is established.

Features of WPA3
Uses stronger authentication (SAE).
Better protection against brute-force password attacks.
Improves privacy on public Wi-Fi networks.
Provides stronger encryption than WPA2.

Advantages
More secure than WPA2.
Better resistance to password guessing.
Improved protection even if users choose weaker passwords.
More secure public Wi-Fi connections.

Limitations
Requires newer routers and devices.
Older hardware may not support WPA3.

Network Topology
Imagine you have several computers that need to communicate.
There are different ways to connect them:
The way these computers are connected is called the network topology.

Types of Network Topology

There are six major physical topologies:

Point-to-Point
Bus
Star
Ring
Mesh
Tree
Hybrid

1. Point-to-Point Topology
Definition

A Point-to-Point topology directly connects two devices using a dedicated communication link.

Diagram
Computer A  ───────────  Computer B

Only these two devices use the connection.

Example
Computer connected to a printer
Two routers connected by a leased line

Advantages
Simple to set up
High speed (dedicated link)
Secure communication
No data collisions

Disadvantages
Only connects two devices
Expensive for large networks
If the link breaks, communication stops

2. Bus Topology
Definition

In a Bus topology, all devices share one main cable, called the backbone cable.

Diagram
PC1 ---- PC2 ---- PC3 ---- PC4
        (Single Backbone Cable)

All devices communicate over the same cable.

How It Works
If PC1 sends data, it travels along the backbone cable. Every device receives the signal, but only the intended recipient accepts it.

Advantages
Low installation cost
Uses less cable
Easy to install for small networks

Disadvantages
If the backbone cable fails, the entire network fails.
Performance decreases with heavy traffic.
Low security.

Example

Small Ethernet LANs (older designs) and cable TV networks.

3. Star Topology
Definition

In a Star topology, every device connects to a central hub or switch.

Diagram
          PC1
           |
PC2 --- Switch --- PC3
           |
          PC4
How It Works

If PC1 wants to send data to PC3:

PC1
  │
  ▼
Switch
  │
  ▼
PC3

The switch forwards the data to the correct device.

Advantages
Easy to install and manage.
Easy to identify faults.
If one cable fails, only that device is affected.
High performance.

Disadvantages
If the central switch/hub fails, the entire network stops.
Installation cost is higher.

Example
Home Wi-Fi networks
Office LANs

4. Ring Topology
Definition

In a Ring topology, every device is connected to exactly two neighboring devices, forming a closed loop (ring).

Diagram
PC1 ---- PC2
 |         |
PC4 ---- PC3
How It Works

Data travels around the ring until it reaches the destination.
Many ring networks use a token, and only the device holding the token can transmit data.

Advantages
High-speed data transmission.
Very few collisions.
Easy to expand.

Disadvantages
If one device or connection fails, the entire ring may stop working.

Example

Older IBM Token Ring networks.

5. Mesh Topology
Definition

In a Mesh topology, every device is connected to every other device through dedicated links.

Diagram
      PC1
     / | \
    /  |  \
 PC2---PC3
    \  |  /
     \ | /
      PC4
How It Works

Each pair of devices has its own dedicated connection.
If one link fails, data can travel through another link.

Formula

If there are N devices:

Links = N × (N − 1) / 2
Ports per device = N − 1

Example:

For 6 devices:

Links = 6 × 5 / 2 = 15

Advantages
Very reliable.
High security.
Easy fault detection.
Fast communication.

Disadvantages
Expensive.
Requires a lot of cabling.
Difficult to install.

Example
Internet backbone
Military communication

6. Tree Topology
Definition

A Tree topology is a hierarchical structure that combines multiple Star topologies.

Diagram
          Main Switch
         /           \
   Switch A       Switch B
    /    \          /    \
  PC1   PC2      PC3   PC4
How It Works

The central hub connects to secondary hubs, which then connect to end devices.

Advantages
Easy to expand.
Suitable for large organizations.
Better management through hierarchy.

Disadvantages
If the main hub fails, the whole network is affected.
Expensive because of extra cabling.

Example
Universities
Large companies
Schools

7. Hybrid Topology
Definition

A Hybrid topology combines two or more different network topologies into a single network.

Diagram
Star Network
      |
      |
Bus Backbone
      |
      |
Ring Network

Advantages
Highly flexible.
Easy to expand.
Can combine the strengths of different topologies.

Disadvantages
Complex to design.
Expensive.
Requires more networking devices.

Example

A university campus where:

Buildings are connected in a star topology.
Individual labs use bus or ring topology.

Physical vs Logical Topology

Physical Topology	                                Logical Topology
Describes the actual arrangement of cables and devices	Describes how data flows through the network
Concerned with hardware layout	 			Concerned with communication rules
Example: Star, Bus, Mesh				Example: Ethernet using CSMA/CD, Token Passing in Ring

What is Ethernet?
A Local Area Network (LAN) is a high-speed data communication system interconnecting devices within a limited scope such as offices or campuses. While LANs may use wired or wireless media, Ethernet (IEEE 802.3) dominates as the primary wired LAN standard.

Types of Ethernet

1. Fast Ethernet
Speed: 100 Mbps

2. Gigabit Ethernet
Speed: 1 Gbps (1000 Mbps)

3. 10 - Gigabit Ethernet
Speed: 10 Gbps

4. Switch Ethernet
Uses network switches for dedicated connections
Each device gets a separate collision domain
Supports speeds from 10 Mbps to 10 Gbps

Key Features of Ethernet
Speed: Supports from 10 Mbps to 400 Gbps.
Reliability: Error detection ensures accurate data transfer.
Cost - effectiveness: Inexpensive and widely available.
Interoperability: Standardized under IEEE 802.3, ensuring compatibility.
Security: Supports encryption and authentication mechanisms.

Carrier Sense Multiple Access (CSMA)
Definition

Carrier Sense Multiple Access (CSMA) is a Media Access Control (MAC) protocol used to control how multiple devices share the same communication channel.
Before sending data, a device first listens (carrier sensing) to check whether the communication channel is free.

If the channel is idle (free) → the device sends data.
If the channel is busy → the device waits until it becomes free.

This reduces the chances of collisions.
CSMA works at the Data Link Layer (Layer 2) of the OSI Model.

Why is CSMA Needed?
Imagine 10 computers connected to the same network.
If all computers send data at the same time:

PC1 ──►
PC2 ──►
PC3 ──►
PC4 ──►

All signals overlap.

This is called a collision.
The transmitted data gets corrupted and has to be sent again.
CSMA helps prevent this by making every device listen before transmitting.

How CSMA Works

Suppose four computers share one cable.

PC1 ---- PC2 ---- PC3 ---- PC4
Step 1

PC1 wants to send data.
It first checks the cable.
Cable Busy?
NO

Step 2
Since the cable is free,
PC1 starts transmitting.

PC1 ==========> PC4

Step 3
Now PC2 also wants to send.
It checks the cable.
Cable Busy?
YES
So PC2 waits.

Step 4
After PC1 finishes,
PC2 checks again.
Cable Busy?
NO
Now PC2 transmits.

Collision
Sometimes two computers check the cable at almost exactly the same time.

Example:

PC1

Checks → Free

PC2

Checks → Free

Both start sending simultaneously.

PC1 =========>

<========= PC2

Their signals collide.
This is called a collision.

Vulnerable Time
Even after sensing the channel, a collision can still happen.
This is because signals take time to travel.
This time is called the Propagation Time (Tp).

Example
Suppose:

PC1 ---------------------- PC2
Signal travel time = 5 μs
PC1 starts sending.
Before PC2 receives PC1's signal,
PC2 also thinks the channel is free.
Both transmit simultaneously.
Collision occurs.
The short period where this can happen is called the Vulnerable Time

Types of CSMA

There are two major protocols:

CSMA/CD
CSMA/CA

1. CSMA/CD (Collision Detection)

Full Form:
Carrier Sense Multiple Access with Collision Detection

Used in:
Wired Ethernet (IEEE 802.3)
Working

Step 1
Listen.
If channel is free,
Send data.

Step 2
While sending,
keep listening.

Transmit

↓

Listen simultaneously

Step 3
If collision occurs,
Stop transmitting immediately.

Step 4
Wait for a random time.

Step 5
Transmit again.

Example
PC1 =========>

<========= PC2

Collision detected.
Both stop.
Both wait random time.
Then retransmit.

Why only in Wired Networks?
In wired Ethernet,
a device can
Send
Listen
at the same time.
Therefore,
it can detect collisions.

2. CSMA/CA (Collision Avoidance)
Full Form:
Carrier Sense Multiple Access with Collision Avoidance

Used in:
Wi-Fi (IEEE 802.11)

Why Not Collision Detection?
Suppose your laptop is transmitting Wi-Fi signals.
Laptop ))))))))))
The transmitted signal is much stronger than any incoming signal.
While transmitting,
the laptop cannot accurately hear another device's transmission.
So it cannot reliably detect a collision.
Therefore,
Wi-Fi avoids collisions instead of detecting them.

Working of CSMA/CA
Step 1
Device wants to transmit.

Step 2
Listen to the channel.
If busy,
wait.

Step 3
When free,
wait for a short time called
IFS (Interframe Space).

Step 4
Check again.
Still free?
Proceed.

Step 5
Choose a random waiting time.
This is called the Backoff Time.
Random Wait

Step 6
Transmit data.

Step 7
Receiver sends
ACK (Acknowledgement).
If ACK received:
Success.
If not,
assume collision.
Retry.

Why Random Backoff?

Suppose
PC1 and PC2 both want to transmit.
Instead of sending immediately,
they choose random waiting times.

Example:

PC1 waits 2 slots

PC2 waits 8 slots

PC1 sends first.

PC2 waits.

No collision occurs.

RTS/CTS Mechanism

RTS = Request To Send
CTS = Clear To Send
Used to solve the Hidden Terminal Problem.

Step 1
Sender:
RTS
"I want to send."

Step 2
Receiver:
CTS
"You may send now."

Step 3
Other nearby devices hear the CTS and remain silent until the transmission is complete.

Hidden Terminal Problem
Imagine:

Laptop A      Router      Laptop C
A and C are both within range of the router, but A and C cannot hear each other.
Both think the channel is free.
Both send data.
Collision occurs at the router.

Using RTS/CTS helps avoid this situation.

Four Access Modes of CSMA

1. 1-Persistent CSMA
Listen continuously.
If channel becomes free,
send immediately.
Very aggressive.
Higher collision chance.

2. Non-Persistent CSMA
Listen once.
If busy,
wait a random time.
Then check again.
Fewer collisions.
More delay.

3. P-Persistent CSMA
Used in slotted systems.
When channel becomes free:
Transmit with probability P.
Wait with probability 1 − P.
Balances delay and collisions.

4. O-Persistent CSMA
A central controller assigns each device a transmission order or time slot.
Devices transmit only during their allocated turn.
This greatly reduces collisions but requires coordination.

Advantages of CSMA
Reduces collisions by sensing the channel first.
Improves network efficiency.
Simple and inexpensive to implement.
Works in both wired and wireless networks (with different variants).
Supports multiple devices sharing the same medium.

Disadvantages of CSMA
Performance decreases as the number of devices increases.
Devices may experience delays while waiting for the channel to become free.

Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP)
Definition
DHCP (Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol) is an Application Layer protocol that automatically assigns IP addresses and other network settings to devices when they join a network.
Without DHCP, every device would have to be configured manually.

DHCP automatically provides:

IP Address
Subnet Mask
Default Gateway
DNS Server

Why is DHCP Needed?
Imagine an office with 500 computers.
Without DHCP:
The network administrator must manually configure each computer.

Computer 1 → 192.168.1.2

Computer 2 → 192.168.1.3

Computer 3 → 192.168.1.4
This is time-consuming and can lead to duplicate IP addresses.

With DHCP:
Simply connect the device to the network.
Computer
↓

Router (DHCP Server)

↓

Automatically gets:
IP Address
Subnet Mask
Gateway
DNS
Everything is configured automatically.

Information Provided by DHCP
1. IP Address
A unique address for the device.

Example:
192.168.1.15

2. Subnet Mask
Defines which devices belong to the same network.
Example:
255.255.255.0

3. Default Gateway
The router's IP address used to reach other networks (like the Internet).
Example:
192.168.1.1

4. DNS Server
Converts domain names into IP addresses.
Example:
8.8.8.8

Components of DHCP
1. DHCP Server
The device that assigns IP addresses.

Usually:
Router
Windows Server
Linux Server

Example:
Router

↓

Assigns IP addresses

2. DHCP Client
Any device requesting an IP.

Examples:
Laptop
Mobile phone
Printer
Smart TV

3. DHCP Relay
Used when the client and DHCP server are in different networks (subnets).
It forwards DHCP messages between them.

Example:
Client

↓

Router (Relay)

↓

DHCP Server

4. IP Address Pool
A range of IP addresses the server can assign.

Example:
192.168.1.100

↓

192.168.1.200
The DHCP server assigns one available IP from this range.

5. Lease
A lease is the time for which a client is allowed to use an assigned IP address.

Example:
Lease Time
24 Hours
Before the lease expires, the client requests a renewal.

7. Default Gateway
Allows devices to communicate outside the local network.
Usually, it is the router's IP.

DHCP Packet Fields (Important Ones)
A DHCP packet contains information exchanged between the client and server.

Important fields include:
Transaction ID: Matches requests and replies.
Client Hardware Address: The client's MAC address.
Your IP Address (yiaddr): The IP assigned to the client.
Server IP Address: The DHCP server's address.
Gateway IP Address:

How DHCP Works (DORA Process)
DORA = Discover → Offer → Request → Acknowledge

Step 1 – DHCP Discover
The client has no IP address.
It broadcasts:
"Is there any DHCP server available?"

Client

↓

Broadcast
DHCP Discover

Step 2 – DHCP Offer
The DHCP server replies:
"I can give you this IP address."
The offer includes:
IP Address
Subnet Mask
Gateway
DNS
Lease Time
DHCP Server

↓

DHCP Offer

192.168.1.25

Step 3 – DHCP Request
The client accepts one offer and requests that IP address.
It also tells any other DHCP servers that their offers are not being accepted.
Client

↓

DHCP Request

I want 192.168.1.25

Step 4 – DHCP ACK (Acknowledgment)
The server confirms the lease.
DHCP Server

↓

DHCP ACK
IP Assigned
The client can now use the network.

Other DHCP Messages
DHCP NAK (Negative Acknowledgment)

The server rejects the client's request.
Reasons:

Requested IP is already in use.
Requested IP is outside the valid range.

DHCP Decline
The client detects that the offered IP address is already being used (usually using ARP).
It informs the server:
"This IP address is already in use."
The server marks that IP as unavailable.

DHCP Release
When a device disconnects or shuts down, it can return its IP address to the DHCP server.
The server places it back into the available pool

DHCP Security Problems

1. DHCP Starvation Attack
An attacker sends thousands of fake DHCP requests using spoofed MAC addresses.

Fake Device 1

Fake Device 2

Fake Device 3
The DHCP server assigns all available IP addresses.

Result:

No IP addresses remain for legitimate users.

2. Rogue DHCP Server
An attacker installs a fake DHCP server on the network.
Instead of receiving the correct gateway and DNS, clients receive malicious settings.
This can redirect users to fake websites or route all traffic through the attacker.

3. Man-in-the-Middle (MITM) Attack
Using a rogue DHCP server, the attacker provides:
Fake gateway
Fake DNS
All network traffic passes through the attacker before reaching its destination.

4. DNS Misuse
The attacker provides a malicious DNS server.
When the user types:
google.com
the fake DNS may redirect them to a phishing website.

Protection Against DHCP Attacks

1. DHCP Snooping
Switches allow DHCP responses only from trusted ports connected to legitimate DHCP servers.
This blocks rogue DHCP servers.

2. Port Security
Limits the number of MAC addresses allowed on a switch port, helping prevent DHCP starvation attacks.

3. Rate Limiting
Restricts the number of DHCP requests a device can send in a given time.

4. Monitoring and Logging
Administrators monitor DHCP lease logs to detect suspicious activity such as abnormal numbers of requests or unauthorized servers.

Advantages of DHCP
Automatically assigns IP addresses.
Prevents duplicate IP addresses.
Reduces manual configuration.
Simplifies network management.

Disadvantages of DHCP
If the DHCP server fails, new devices may not receive IP addresses.
Lacks built-in authentication, making it vulnerable to attacks like rogue DHCP servers.

TRANSIMISSION MODES
Transmission Modes define how data travels between two devices

They determine:
Who can send data
Who can receive data
Whether communication is one-way or two-way
Whether both devices can communicate simultaneously

There are three transmission modes:

1. Simplex Mode (One-Way Communication)
In Simplex Mode, data flows only in one direction.
One device is always the sender (transmitter).
The other device is always the receiver.
The receiver cannot send any response back.

Diagram
Sender  ───────────► Receiver
Only one direction of communication is possible.

Example
Keyboard → Computer
Keyboard sends data.
Computer receives it.

Advantages
Very simple to implement
Uses the full bandwidth for sending
Low cost

Disadvantages
No feedback
Receiver cannot acknowledge messages
Errors cannot be reported

2. Half-Duplex Mode (Two-Way, But One at a Time)
In Half-Duplex Mode, both devices can send and receive data, but not at the same time.
Only one device transmits while the other waits.

Diagram
A ─────► B
(wait)

A ◄───── B
Communication happens alternately.

Example
Walkie-talkie

Person A:
"Hello!"
Then releases the button.
Person B:
"Yes, I'm listening."
Only one person can talk at a time.

Advantages
Supports two-way communication
Cheaper than full duplex
Uses only one communication channel

Disadvantages
Cannot transmit simultaneously
Waiting time exists
Delay increases with heavy traffic

3. Full-Duplex Mode (Two-Way Simultaneously)
In Full-Duplex Mode, both devices send and receive data at the same time.
Nobody has to wait.

Diagram
A ─────────► B
A ◄───────── B

Both arrows are active simultaneously.
Example
Telephone call

While talking:
You can speak.
You can hear the other person.
Both happen simultaneously.

Other examples:
Video calls

Advantages
Fast communication
No waiting time
Higher efficiency

Disadvantages
More expensive
Requires complex hardware
Needs more bandwidth

System Bus Design
System Bus Design (Easy Explanation)
A System Bus is a set of communication pathways (wires or traces) that connects the CPU, Memory (RAM), and Input/Output (I/O) devices. It allows these components to exchange information.

What is a System Bus?
A System Bus is a collection of parallel wires.

Each wire carries 1 bit.
If there are 8 wires, it is an 8-bit bus.
If there are 16 wires, it is a 16-bit bus.
If there are 64 wires, it is a 64-bit bus.
More wires mean more data can be transferred at once.

Why is it called a "Shared Bus"?
Only one device can use the bus at a time.
Imagine a single-lane bridge.
CPU is sending data → Others must wait.
Hard disk is sending data → CPU waits.
This prevents two devices from sending data simultaneously and causing collisions.

Three Types of System Bus
A system bus consists of three types of lines:
Address Bus
Data Bus
Control Bus

1. Address Bus
Purpose
The Address Bus tells where the data should go.
It carries the memory address from the CPU.
Direction
Unidirectional
CPU  ─────────► Memory

Only the CPU sends addresses.
Memory never sends addresses back.

Example
Suppose the CPU wants data stored at memory location 500.
The CPU sends:
Address = 500
using the Address Bus.
Now RAM knows which location the CPU wants.

Why is it Important?
The number of address lines determines how much memory can be accessed.

Formula:
Maximum Memory = 2^(Number of Address Lines)

Example
16 address lines
2^16 = 65,536 addresses

Data Bus
The Data Bus carries the actual data.
This is the information being transferred.
Direction
Bidirectional
CPU ◄────────► Memory
Both CPU and memory can send data.

Example 1 (Reading)
CPU asks RAM:
Give me the value at address 100.
RAM sends
25
through the Data Bus.

Why is Data Bus Width Important?
The width determines how much data can be transferred at one time.

Example
8-bit Data Bus
Transfers 8 bits at once
A wider data bus generally means higher performance because more data moves in each transfer.

3. Control Bus
The Control Bus carries control and timing signals.
It tells every component what operation to perform and when.
Think of it as the traffic police of the system bus.

Example
Suppose CPU wants to read data.
The CPU sends:
Memory Read
through the Control Bus.
RAM understands:
"The CPU wants to read data."

If CPU wants to store data:
Memory Write
RAM understands:
"Store the incoming data."

Types of Control Signals
Memory Read
CPU → RAM
"Send me data."

Memory Write
CPU → RAM
"Store this data."

I/O Read
CPU → Keyboard
"Send your data."

I/O Write
CPU → Printer
"Print this."

Interrupt Request (IRQ)
Sometimes a device needs immediate attention.

Example:
You press a key.
Keyboard sends
Interrupt Request
The CPU pauses its current task and processes the keyboard input.

Clock Signal
The clock synchronizes all operations.

Bus Request / Bus Grant
If multiple devices want to use the bus:
A device sends a Bus Request.
The CPU or bus controller sends a Bus Grant.
Only then can that device use the bus.
This avoids conflicts.

Why is the Control Bus Important?
Without the Control Bus:
RAM wouldn't know whether to read or write.
Devices would act at the wrong time.
Multiple devices could try to use the bus simultaneously.

Complete Example
Suppose the CPU wants to read the value stored at memory location 200.

Step 1: Address Bus
CPU sends:
Address = 200

↓

RAM selects location 200.

Step 2: Control Bus
CPU sends:
Memory Read

↓

RAM understands it should send the data.

Step 3: Data Bus
RAM sends:

45

↓

CPU receives the value 45.

Transmission Media in Computer Networks
Transmission Media is the path through which data travels from one device to another in a computer network. It can be a physical cable (wired) or wireless signals (air).

Types of Transmission Media
There are two main types:

Guided Media (Wired)
Unguided Media (Wireless)

1. Guided Media (Wired Transmission)
Guided media uses physical cables to carry data.
The signal is guided through a fixed path.

Examples
Ethernet cable
Telephone cable
Optical fiber

Advantages
Faster
More secure
Less interference
More reliable

Disadvantages
Installation cost
Limited mobility

Types of Guided Media

A. Twisted Pair Cable
A twisted pair cable contains two insulated copper wires twisted together.
The twisting helps reduce electromagnetic interference (EMI)


Why are the wires twisted?
Imagine two straight wires acting like antennas—they easily pick up electrical noise.
Twisting the wires causes the interference picked up on one twist to cancel with the interference on the next, resulting in a cleaner signal.

Types of Twisted Pair

(i) UTP (Unshielded Twisted Pair)
No extra metal shielding
Cheapest
Easy to install

Advantages
Low cost
Flexible
Easy installation
Good speed for short distances

Disadvantages
More affected by noise
Shorter transmission distance

Examples:
Ethernet cable (Cat5e, Cat6)
Telephone cable

(ii) STP (Shielded Twisted Pair)
STP has an additional metal shield around the wires.
This shield blocks outside interference.

Advantages
Better performance
Less noise

Disadvantages
More expensive
Harder to install
Thicker cable

B. Coaxial Cable

A coaxial cable has several layers:

Outer Jacket
Metal Shield
Insulator
Copper Wire (Center)

The metal shielding protects the signal from noise.

Uses
Cable TV

Advantages
Higher bandwidth than twisted pair
Better protection from interference

Disadvantages
More expensive than twisted pair
Bulkier
Less flexible

C. Optical Fiber Cable
Optical fiber sends light instead of electricity.
It uses the principle of Total Internal Reflection (TIR) to keep light inside the fiber.

Advantages
Extremely high speed
Very high bandwidth
Long-distance communication
Immune to EMI

Disadvantages
High installation cost
Fragile

Uses
Undersea internet cables
Hospitals
Military communication

2. Unguided Media (Wireless)
Unguided media sends data through the air using electromagnetic waves.
No physical cable is needed.

Examples:
Wi-Fi
Bluetooth
Mobile networks

Types of Unguided Media

A. Radio Waves
Radio waves travel in all directions and can pass through buildings.

Uses
AM/FM radio
Television broadcasting

Advantages
No line of sight needed
Long-distance communication

Disadvantages
Lower security
Can experience interference

B. Microwaves
Microwaves require Line of Sight (LOS).
The transmitting and receiving antennas must directly face each other.

Uses
Mobile towers
Satellite communication

Advantages
High speed

Disadvantages
Affected by rain and fog
Obstacles block the signal

C. Infrared
Infrared waves are used for very short distances.
They cannot pass through walls, which reduces interference and improves privacy over short ranges.

Uses
TV remote controls
Wireless keyboards
Wireless mouse

Advantages
Low cost
Minimal interference between nearby rooms

Disadvantages
Very short range
Cannot penetrate obstacles

Transmission Impairments
Sometimes signals become weak or distorted during transmission.

1. Attenuation
Loss of signal strength over distance.

Solution:
Analog: Amplifier
Digital: Repeater

2. Distortion
The shape of the signal changes because different frequency components arrive at different times.

3. Noise
Unwanted electrical or electromagnetic signals mix with the original signal and can corrupt data.

What is UTP (Unshielded Twisted Pair)?
UTP (Unshielded Twisted Pair) is the most commonly used networking cable. It consists of pairs of insulated copper wires twisted together without any additional metal shielding.

Why are the wires twisted?
Suppose there is a nearby power cable.
A straight wire would pick up electrical noise.

When two wires are twisted:

Both wires experience nearly the same interference.
The receiver compares the signals.
The common interference cancels out.
This results in a cleaner, more reliable signal.

Categories (Types) of UTP Cable

Different categories support different speeds and frequencies.

Category	Bandwidth	Maximum Speed					Common Use
Cat5e	        100 MHz			1 Gbps						Home and office LANs
Cat6		250 MHz			Up to 10 Gbps (short distances)			Modern Ethernet
Cat6a		500 MHz			10 Gbps (longer distances)			Enterprise networks
Cat7		600 MHz			10 Gbps						High-performance networking (includes extra shielding)
Cat8		2000 MHz		25–40 Gbps						Data centers

Applications of UTP

1. Local Area Networks (LAN)
Most computers in homes, schools, and offices are connected using UTP Ethernet cables.

2. Telephone Networks
Telephone systems have used twisted pair cables for many years because they carry voice signals efficiently.

Difference between WDM and DWDM
Both WDM (Wavelength Division Multiplexing) and DWDM (Dense Wavelength Division Multiplexing) are technologies used in optical fiber communication.
Their main purpose is to increase the amount of data that can be transmitted through a single optical fiber by sending multiple light signals of different wavelengths simultaneously.

What is Multiplexing?
Multiplexing means combining multiple signals into one communication channel.

Example
Imagine a highway.
Without multiplexing → Only one car can use the road.
With multiplexing → Many cars travel together on the same highway

What is WDM?
WDM (Wavelength Division Multiplexing) is a technology that combines multiple light signals of different wavelengths (colors) and sends them through one optical fiber.
Each wavelength carries its own independent data.

Why use WDM?
Without WDM:

Fiber 1 → Signal A
Fiber 2 → Signal B
Fiber 3 → Signal C
Need three separate fibers.

With WDM:
Signal A
Signal B
Signal C
      │
      ▼
One Optical Fiber
Only one fiber is needed.
This reduces installation cost.

Advantages of WDM
1. Increased Capacity
Many signals travel simultaneously.
More data.

2. Cost Effective
No need to install multiple optical fibers.

Disadvantages of WDM
Difficult wavelength tuning
Mostly suitable for smaller networks

What is DWDM?
DWDM (Dense Wavelength Division Multiplexing) is an advanced version of WDM.

The word Dense means:
More wavelengths are packed very closely together.
Instead of using only a few wavelengths,
DWDM uses dozens or even hundreds of wavelengths on the same optical fiber.

Diagram

WDM
🔴        🔵        🟢
Large spacing between wavelengths.

DWDM
🔴🔵🟢🟡🟣⚪🟠🔴🔵🟢🟡
Very closely packed wavelengths.

Advantages of DWDM
1. Very High Capacity
Hundreds of channels can be transmitted.

3. Long Distance Communication
Signals travel very long distances.

Suitable for:
Internet backbone
Telecom companies
Undersea cables

4. Efficient Bandwidth Usage
Almost the entire fiber capacity is utilized.

Disadvantages of DWDM
Expensive
Complex technology
Requires more power

Applications

WDM
Campus networks

DWDM
Internet backbone
Telecom networks
Long-distance communication

Data Link Layer in OSI Model
The Data Link Layer is the second layer of the OSI (Open Systems Interconnection) Model.
It is responsible for transferring data between two directly connected devices (node-to-node communication) on the same local network (LAN).

Main Responsibilities of the Data Link Layer
The Data Link Layer receives packets from the Network Layer, converts them into frames, and sends them to the Physical Layer for transmission.

It performs the following tasks:
Node-to-node delivery
Framing
Physical (MAC) addressing
Error detection and correction

2. Physical Addressing (MAC Address)
Every network device has a unique MAC Address.

Example:
00:1A:2B:3C:4D:5E
The Data Link Layer uses MAC addresses to deliver frames within the same LAN.

3. Error Detection
Sometimes data becomes corrupted during transmission.
The Data Link Layer detects errors using techniques like:
CRC (Cyclic Redundancy Check)
Checksum
The receiver detects that the data has changed.

Sub-layers of Data Link Layer
The Data Link Layer has two sublayers.

Data Link Layer
│
├── Logical Link Control (LLC)
│
└── Media Access Control (MAC)

1. Logical Link Control (LLC)

LLC is responsible for:
Multiplexing
Flow control
Error reporting

Example
Computer A sends data.
Computer B replies:
ACK
This acknowledgment is handled by LLC.

2. Media Access Control (MAC)

The MAC sublayer is responsible for:
MAC addressing
Frame creation

It decides:
"Which device can send data now?"

Communication Process
Suppose Computer A sends data to Computer B.

Step 1
Application creates data.

↓

Step 2
Network Layer creates a packet.
↓

Step 3
Data Link Layer creates a frame.
Destination MAC
Source MAC
Data
CRC
↓

Step 4
Physical Layer converts it into bits.
↓

Step 5
Receiver performs the reverse process.

Devices Operating at Data Link Layer

1. Switch
A switch is the most common
It forwards frames using MAC addresses.

Example
PC1 ----\
          \
PC2 ---- Switch ---- PC3
          /
PC4 ----/

The switch learns MAC addresses and sends the frame only to the correct destination.

2. Bridge
A bridge connects two LANs.
It reduces unnecessary traffic by forwarding only required frames.

3. Network Interface Card (NIC)
Every computer has a NIC.

Functions:
Stores the MAC address.
Sends and receives frames.
Connects the computer to the network.

4. Wireless Access Point (WAP)
Allows wireless devices to communicate with a wired network.
Protocols Used at Data Link Layer

Some common protocols include:
HDLC (High-Level Data Link Control) – Reliable communication over point-to-point links.
PPP (Point-to-Point Protocol) – Commonly used for direct connections (e.g., older dial-up and some WAN links).
SLIP (Serial Line Internet Protocol) – Older protocol for serial connections.
SDLC (Synchronous Data Link Control) – IBM-developed data link protocol.
LCP (Link Control Protocol) – Establishes and manages PPP links.
NCP (Network Control Protocol) – Configures network-layer protocols over PPP.

What is SDLC?
SDLC (Synchronous Data Link Control) is a Data Link Layer (Layer 2) protocol developed by IBM
Its main purpose is to provide reliable communication between devices by organizing data into frames and detecting transmission errors.

It supports:
Point-to-Point communication
Multipoint communication

Basic SDLC Frame Format
An SDLC frame looks like this:

+------+---------+---------+-------------+------+------+
|Flag  | Address | Control | Information | FCS  | Flag |
+------+---------+---------+-------------+------+------+

Every field has a specific purpose.

1. Flag Field
Purpose
The Flag Field marks the beginning and end of every frame.

Flag Pattern
The flag is always:
01111110
This special bit pattern helps the receiver recognize where a frame begins and ends.
Without the flag, the receiver wouldn't know where one frame ends and the next begins.

2. Address Field
Purpose
The Address Field tells which device should receive the frame.

Example
Suppose three computers exist:
Computer A
Computer B
Computer C
Address field:
Address = Computer B
Only Computer B processes the frame.
The others ignore it.

Types of Addressing

Individual Address
Sent to one device.

Example:
Address = PC2

Group Address
Sent to multiple devices.

Example:
Address = Lab Computers

Broadcast Address
Sent to everyone.

Example:
Address = All Devices

3. Control Field
This is one of the most important fields.
It tells the receiver:
"What should I do with this frame?"

Types of SDLC Frames

There are three frame types.

A. Information Frame (I-Frame)

Used to carry actual user data.
The Control field identifies it as an I-Frame.

Uses
Data transfer

B. Supervisory Frame (S-Frame)

Used for:

Acknowledgement
Error reporting

It does not carry user data.

Uses
ACK
NAK

C. Unnumbered Frame (U-Frame)

Used for connection management.

Examples

Establish connection
Disconnect connection
Reset communication

It is mainly used for control purposes

Uses
Link setup
Link termination

4. Information Field
Purpose
This field contains the actual data.

5. Frame Check Sequence (FCS)
Purpose
The FCS detects transmission errors.

Before sending:
The sender calculates an error-checking value using a method such as CRC (Cyclic Redundancy Check).
The receiver performs the same calculation.
If both values match:
✅ Data is correct.
If not:
❌ Error detected.

6. Ending Flag
The ending flag is exactly the same as the starting flag.
01111110
It tells the receiver:
"The frame has finished."

Complete Example
Suppose Computer A sends:
Hello
The SDLC frame becomes:

+--------+---------+-----------+-------------+------+--------+
| Flag   | Address | Control   | Information | FCS  | Flag   |
+--------+---------+-----------+-------------+------+--------+
|01111110| PC2     | I-Frame   | Hello       | CRC  |01111110|
+--------+---------+-----------+-------------+------+--------+

How SDLC Communication Works
Step 1

Sender creates data.

↓

Step 2

SDLC adds:

Flag
Address
Control
Information
FCS
Ending Flag

↓

Step 3

Frame is transmitted.

↓

Step 4

Receiver checks:

Starting Flag ✔
Address ✔
Control ✔
FCS ✔

↓

Step 5

If no errors exist:

Receiver sends an acknowledgement (ACK).

Advantages of SDLC
1. Reliable Communication
Detects transmission errors.

2. Error Detection
Uses Frame Check Sequence (FCS) to identify corrupted frames.

4. Multiple Network Types
Works in:

Point-to-point networks
Multipoint networks

What is HDLC?
HDLC (High-Level Data Link Control) is a bit-oriented Data Link Layer (Layer 2) protocol used for reliable communication between two or more network devices.

Features
Operates at Data Link Layer (Layer 2)
Uses frames for data transmission
Supports point-to-point and multipoint communication

HDLC Frame Structure
+------+---------+---------+-------------+------+------+
|Flag  | Address | Control | Information | FCS  | Flag |
+------+---------+---------+-------------+------+------+

Purpose of Each Field
Field	Purpose
Flag	Marks the beginning and end of the frame
Address	Identifies the destination station
Control	Specifies the frame type and communication control
Information	Carries user data (optional)
FCS	Detects errors using CRC
Ending Flag	Marks the end of the frame

How HDLC Communication Works
Step 1
Application creates data.

↓

Step 2
HDLC creates a frame.
Flag
Address
Control
Information
FCS
Flag

↓

Step 3
Frame is transmitted.

↓

Step 4
Receiver checks:
Flag ✔
Address ✔
Control ✔
CRC ✔

↓

Step 5
If everything is correct,
Receiver sends an ACK (usually via an S-Frame).

Serial Line Internet Protocol (SLIP)
SLIP (Serial Line Internet Protocol) is one of the earliest protocols used to send IP packets over a serial connection.

Why was SLIP created?
In the early days of the Internet, computers were connected using serial communication (telephone lines, RS-232 serial ports, dial-up modems).
However, the IP protocol could not directly send packets

It simply says:
"Take an IP packet, add start/end markers, send it over the serial cable."

SLIP was mainly used in:
Serial ports (RS-232)
Router-to-router communication
Host-to-host communication
Early UNIX systems

Today, it has almost completely been replaced by PPP (Point-to-Point Protocol)

How does SLIP work?
Suppose the IP layer creates an IP packet.
SLIP simply adds a special byte at the end.
When the receiver sees the END byte, it knows:
"The packet is finished."

A major job of SLIP is framing.
Framing means:
Determining where one packet starts and where it ends.
SLIP uses two special control characters.

1. END Character
Purpose:
Marks the end of every packet.

2. ESC Character
Purpose:
Used when the actual data itself contains an END or ESC byte.
Otherwise, the receiver would mistake it for a control character.

Instead of sending

END

it sends

ESC ESC_END

Similarly,

Instead of sending

ESC

it sends

ESC ESC_ESC

Addressing
SLIP cannot automatically assign IP addresses.
Both devices must already know each other's IP addresses.

Data Transmission
SLIP sends
IP Packet
+
END

Nothing more.
A SLIP frame looks like:

+--------------------+
|     IP Packet      |
+--------------------+
|      END Byte      |
+--------------------+

Very simple.

Advantages of SLIP

1. Very Simple
Easy to implement.
Very little code is required.

2. Low Overhead
Adds only a few extra bytes.

3. Lightweight
Suitable for devices with limited memory and processing power.

Disadvantages of SLIP

1. No Authentication
Anyone connected to the serial line can communicate.

2. Supports Only IP

4. No Dynamic IP Address Assignment
IP addresses must be configured manually.

3. No Error Detection
If bits are corrupted during transmission,
SLIP does not detect or correct errors.

Why was PPP introduced?

SLIP had many limitations.
PPP (Point-to-Point Protocol) improved on SLIP by adding:

Authentication (PAP, CHAP)
Dynamic IP address assignment
Error detection
Support for multiple network protocols

The two most common authentication methods are:

PAP (Password Authentication Protocol)
CHAP (Challenge Handshake Authentication Protocol)

Think of them as two different ways of proving your identity.
Why is Authentication Needed?
Suppose you dial into an Internet Service Provider (ISP).
Before allowing access, the ISP wants to verify your identity.

1. PAP (Password Authentication Protocol)
PAP is the simplest authentication protocol.
It works like logging into a website using a username and password.

How PAP Works
Client connects to the server.
Client sends:
Username
Password
Server checks the credentials.
If correct → Access granted.
If incorrect → Access denied.

Problem with PAP
The password is sent in plain text.
If an attacker intercepts the communication, they can directly read the password.

Advantages of PAP
Very simple
Easy to implement
Fast authentication

Disadvantages of PAP
Password sent in plain text
Vulnerable to eavesdropping

2. CHAP (Challenge Handshake Authentication Protocol)
CHAP is much more secure.
Instead of sending the password, it proves the client knows the password without actually transmitting it.

How CHAP Works
Step 1: Connection Established
Client ------------- Server
Step 2: Server Sends a Challenge
The server generates a random number.

Example:
Challenge = 56789
and sends it to the client.

Step 3: Client Creates a Hash
The client combines:
Challenge
Password

Example:
Password = abc123
Hash(56789 + abc123)
Suppose the result is:
8A92BC41
The client sends only:
8A92BC41
The password itself is never sent.

Step 4: Server Verifies
The server already knows the user's password.
It performs the same calculation:
Hash(56789 + abc123)
If the computed hash matches the client's hash:
Authentication Successful
Otherwise:
Authentication Failed

What is LAP?
Link Access Procedure (LAP) is a family of Data Link Layer (DLL) protocols

It is based on HDLC (High-Level Data Link Control) and is designed to provide reliable communication over different types of networks.

Think of HDLC as the parent protocol, and LAP protocols as specialized versions created for different communication technologies.

LAP works at the Data Link Layer (Layer 2) of the OSI model.

Its main responsibilities are:
Framing

Error detection

Error correction (in some versions)

Why are there different LAP protocols?

Different communication technologies have different requirements.

Types of LAP Protocols
1. MLP (Multilink Procedure)

Purpose

MLP combines multiple physical communication links into one logical link.

Example

Suppose one connection provides:

64 Kbps

Using four links:

64 + 64 + 64 + 64

= 256 Kbps

MLP treats them as one large connection.

Advantages

Higher bandwidth

Better utilization

2. LAPM (Link Access Procedure for Modems)

Purpose

LAPM is used in modems, especially V.32 and V.42 modems.

Its main goal is error-free communication over telephone lines.

Why is LAPM needed?

Telephone lines are noisy.

Errors may occur during transmission.

LAPM detects and corrects these errors.

Features
1. CRC (Cyclic Redundancy Check)

Checks whether data has been corrupted.

Example:

Original Data

HELLO

Receiver computes CRC.

If CRC matches:

No Error

Otherwise:

Error Detected

2. ARQ (Automatic Repeat Request)

If an error is found,

Receiver sends:

Please send again.

Sender retransmits the frame.

3. Selective Reject (SREJ)

Suppose frames are

1

2

3

4

5

Only frame 3 is damaged.

Without SREJ:

Resend everything.

With SREJ:

Resend only Frame 3.

This improves efficiency.

Advantages

Reliable transmission

Error detection

Error correction through retransmission

Better modem performance

3. LAPF (Link Access Procedure for Frame Relay)

Purpose

LAPF is used in Frame Relay networks.

Frame Relay is designed for fast communication.

To achieve speed, LAPF removes unnecessary features.

Key Idea

Instead of correcting errors itself,

LAPF simply checks the frame and forwards it.

If an error occurs,

Higher layers (such as TCP) handle retransmission.

Features

Very lightweight

Fast

4. LAPX (Link Access Procedure for Half-Duplex)

Purpose

LAPX is designed for half-duplex communication.

What is Half-Duplex?

Only one side can transmit at a time.

Example:

Walkie-talkies.

Where is it used?

Examples include:

Ship-to-shore communication

Radio communication

Advantage

Reliable communication where only one direction is active at a time.

Key Features of LAP Protocols

1. Based on HDLC

2. Reliable Communication

Most LAP protocols use:

CRC

ARQ

3. Technology-Specific

Each version is designed for a specific technology.

Network Control Protocol (NCP)
The term NCP (Network Control Protocol) can refer to two different things, which often causes confusion:

Original NCP – Used in ARPANET before TCP/IP (historical protocol).

PPP Network Control Protocols – A family of protocols used with PPP to configure different network-layer protocols (such as IP and IPv6).

1. Original NCP (ARPANET)
What is NCP?

Network Control Protocol (NCP) was the first host-to-host communication protocol used in ARPANET, the predecessor of the Internet.

Before TCP/IP existed, computers communicated using NCP.

ARPANET → NCP → TCP/IP

ARPANET (Advanced Research Projects Agency Network) was the world's first computer network and the direct precursor to the modern Internet.

How it worked

Instead of sending data as one continuous stream, ARPANET broke information into small packets. Each packet could travel independently across the network and be reassembled at its destination. This made the network more efficient

Why was NCP created?

It allowed computers on ARPANET to:

Communicate with each other

Transfer files

Without NCP, computers could not exchange information over the network.

Features of Original NCP

1. Host-to-Host Communication

It enabled two computers to communicate.

Computer A <------NCP------> Computer B

2. Remote Login

Users could log into another computer over the network.

Example:

You are sitting at Computer A but use Computer B remotely.

3. File Transfer

Allowed users to send files between computers.

Example:

Computer A

│

│ file.txt

▼

Computer B

4. Simplex Communication

NCP mainly supported simplex (one-way) communication.

Example:

Sender -----------> Receiver

Limitations of Original NCP

It lacked many features that TCP/IP later introduced.

No proper routing between multiple networks

Could not efficiently connect different kinds of networks

Why was TCP/IP better?

TCP/IP introduced:

Reliable communication

Routing across multiple interconnected networks

2. PPP Network Control Protocols
The term NCP (Network Control Protocol) can refer to two different things, which often causes confusion:

Original NCP – Used in ARPANET before TCP/IP (historical protocol).

PPP Network Control Protocols – A family of protocols used with PPP to configure different network-layer protocols (such as IP and IPv6).

What does PPP NCP do?

After the PPP link is established, NCP configures the network-layer protocol.

Different NCP Protocols
1. IPCP (Internet Protocol Control Protocol)

Purpose

Configures IPv4 over PPP.

Functions

Assigns IP addresses

Enables IPv4 communication

Example:

Client

IP = 192.168.1.10

↓

Server

IP = 192.168.1.1

2. IPv6CP (IPv6 Control Protocol)

Purpose

Configures IPv6 over PPP.

Functions

enables IPv6 communication

3. IPXCP (Internetwork Packet Exchange Control Protocol)

4. OSINLCP (OSI Network Layer Control Protocol)

Purpose

Configures OSI Network Layer protocols.

Functions:

Enable OSI modules

Disable OSI modules

Configure OSI networking

5. NBFCP (NetBIOS Frames Control Protocol)

Purpose

Configures NetBIOS Frames (NBF) over PPP.

Used mainly in older Microsoft networking environments.

6. CCP (Compression Control Protocol)

Purpose

Compresses data before transmission.

Benefits:

Less bandwidth usage

Faster data transfer

Reduced congestion

7. ECP (Encryption Control Protocol)

Purpose

Provides encryption for data sent over PPP.

Benefits:

Improved security

Protects sensitive information

8. BCP (Bridging Control Protocol)

Purpose

Allows PPP links to carry bridged network traffic.

Advantages of NCP (PPP)

1. Network Configuration

Automatically configures network-layer protocols.

2. Compatibility

Supports multiple protocols:

IPv4

IPv6

IPX

OSI

NetBIOS

5. Compression

Can reduce the amount of transmitted data (using CCP).

Applications

1. WAN (Wide Area Networks)

Used for long-distance point-to-point links.

2. VPNs

PPP with NCP can help configure secure VPN connections.

Link Control Protocol (LCP)
Link Control Protocol (LCP) is a protocol used by PPP (Point-to-Point Protocol) to establish, configure, test, maintain, and terminate a communication link between two devices.

Think of LCP as the manager of a PPP connection.

Before any data can be exchanged, LCP first ensures that the communication link is ready.

Where does LCP work?

LCP operates at the Data Link Layer (Layer 2) of the OSI model.

Functions of LCP

LCP performs five major functions.

1. Link Establishment

LCP first establishes the PPP connection.

Process

Computer A Computer B

|------ Configure-Request ----->|

|<----- Configure-Ack ----------|

Once both devices agree on the settings, the PPP link is established.

2. Link Configuration

LCP negotiates various communication parameters.

Examples include:

Maximum Receive Unit (MRU)

Authentication protocol (PAP or CHAP)

3. Link Testing and Monitoring

LCP continuously checks whether the link is still functioning.

If the connection fails, LCP detects it.
