Common mechanism:

Echo Request

↓

Echo Reply

If no reply is received, the link is considered down.

4. Authentication Negotiation

LCP decides which authentication protocol will be used.

For example:

Use PAP

or

Use CHAP

The actual authentication is performed later by PAP or CHAP.

LCP only negotiates which one to use.

5. Link Termination

When communication ends, LCP gracefully closes the connection.

Terminate Request

↓

Terminate Acknowledge

This frees network resources.

Advantages of LCP

Establishes PPP connections reliably.

Negotiates communication parameters automatically.

Supports authentication methods such as PAP and CHAP.

Detects link failures using Echo Requests and Echo Replies.

Terminates connections gracefully.

Limitations

LCP itself does not authenticate users; it only negotiates the authentication protocol.

It does not configure IP addresses; this is done by NCP

Services Offered by the Data Link Layer (DLL)
Its main job is to ensure reliable communication between two directly connected devices by organizing data into frames, detecting/correcting errors, controlling data flow, and using physical (MAC) addresses.

Services Offered by the Data Link Layer

The main services are:

Framing

Error Detection

Error Correction

Flow Control

Addressing

1. Framing

The Physical Layer sends only bits (0s and 1s).

Example:

101101011010100101001

These bits have no clear beginning or end.

The Data Link Layer groups these bits into frames.

A frame is a structured unit of data.

Example

Without framing:

101101011010100101011010101001

Receiver cannot determine where one message ends and another begins.

With framing:

+-----------------------------+

| Header | Data | Trailer |

+-----------------------------+

The header and trailer mark the start and end of the frame.

Why is framing needed?

Identifies the beginning and end of data.

Makes transmission reliable.

Types of Framing

A. Fixed-size Framing

Every frame has the same size.

Example:

Frame 1 → 100 bytes

Frame 2 → 100 bytes

Frame 3 → 100 bytes

Easy to process but may waste space.

B. Variable-size Framing

Frames can have different sizes.

Example:

Frame 1 → 80 bytes

Frame 2 → 150 bytes

Frame 3 → 45 bytes

Uses bandwidth more efficiently.

2. Error Detection

Sometimes bits change during transmission because of noise.

Example:

Sender sends:

10110110

Receiver gets:

10100110

An error has occurred.

The Data Link Layer detects such errors.

Methods of Error Detection

A. Parity Check

A parity bit is added to the data.

Even Parity

The total number of 1s should be even.

If the receiver counts an odd number of 1s, it detects an error.

B. Checksum

The sender calculates the sum of the data and appends a checksum.

3. Error Correction

After detecting an error, it must either be corrected or the data must be retransmitted.

There are two main methods.

A. Automatic Repeat Request (ARQ)

If the receiver detects an error, it asks the sender to retransmit the frame.

Types of ARQ

Stop-and-Wait ARQ

Send Frame 1

↓

Wait for ACK

↓

Send Frame 2

Simple but slow.

Go-Back-N ARQ

Suppose the sender transmits:

1 2 3 4 5

If Frame 3 is lost:

The sender retransmits:

3 4 5

Selective Repeat ARQ

If only Frame 3 is lost:

The sender retransmits:

Only Frame 3

This is more efficient than Go-Back-N.

B. Forward Error Correction (FEC)

Instead of asking for retransmission, extra redundant bits are added.

The receiver uses these bits to detect and correct errors on its own.

Examples:

Hamming Code

Reed-Solomon Code

This is useful where retransmission is difficult, such as satellite communication.

4. Flow Control

What is Flow Control?

Suppose:

Sender can send 1000 frames/second

Receiver can process only 100 frames/second

Without flow control, the receiver's buffer overflows and frames are lost.

Flow control regulates the sender's speed.

Methods of Flow Control

A. Stop-and-Wait

Sender

↓

Frame 1

↓

Receiver

↓

ACK

↓

Frame 2

Only one frame is sent at a time.

Simple but inefficient.

B. Sliding Window

The sender can transmit multiple frames before waiting for acknowledgments.

Example:

Window Size = 4

Sender

1 2 3 4

↓

Receiver sends ACKs later

↓

Sender sends 5 6 7 8

Advantages:

Better bandwidth utilization

Faster communication

5. Addressing

The Data Link Layer uses MAC (Media Access Control) addresses.

A MAC address uniquely identifies a network interface within a local network.

Example:

00:1A:2B:3C:4D:5E

Every network card (NIC) has a unique MAC address.

Example

Suppose three computers are connected to a switch.

Computer A

MAC:

AA-AA

|

Switch

/ \

Computer B Computer C

MAC:

BB-BB

MAC:

CC-CC

If Computer A wants to send data to Computer C:

The frame contains:

Destination MAC = CC-CC

The switch forwards the frame only to Computer C.

Limitations of the Data Link Layer

1. No Encryption

It does not encrypt data.

2. No Authentication

It generally does not verify whether a device is genuine.

This makes attacks such as MAC address spoofing possible.

3. Limited to Local Communication

It works only between directly connected devices or within the same local network.

Switching in Computer Networks
Switching is the process of transferring data from one device to another through a network.

A switch receives data from one device and forwards it only to the intended destination device.

Instead of sending data to everyone, a switch sends it only to the correct device, making communication faster and more efficient.

Switching mainly occurs at the Data Link Layer (Layer 2) of the OSI model.

What Does a Switch Do?

Connects multiple devices in a LAN.

Learns the MAC address of connected devices.

Sends frames only to the intended device.

Reduces unnecessary traffic.

Working of a Network Switch

Step 1: Frame Reception

Computer A sends a frame.

PC A

↓

Frame

↓

Switch

Step 2: MAC Address Extraction

The switch reads the destination MAC address from the frame.

Example:

Destination MAC

00:11:22:33:44:55

Step 3: Lookup

The switch checks its MAC Address Table.

Example:

MAC Address Port

AA-AA 1

BB-BB 2

CC-CC 3

If the destination MAC is BB-BB, the switch knows it is connected to Port 2.

Step 4: Forwarding

The switch sends the frame only through the correct port.

Port 1

↓

Switch

↓

Port 2

No other device receives the frame.

Step 6: Frame Transmission

The frame reaches the destination device.

PC A

↓

Switch

↓

PC B

Communication is complete.

What if the Destination MAC Address is Unknown?
Suppose the switch has never seen the destination device before.

So it performs Flooding.

Flooding

The switch sends the frame to every port except the incoming port.

The correct device receives the frame and replies.

The switch learns its MAC address and stores it for future communication.

Types of Switching
There are three switching techniques.

Message Switching

Circuit Switching

Packet Switching

Message Switching
Message Switching is a switching technique in which the entire message is transmitted as one unit from the sender to the receiver.

There is no dedicated path between the sender and receiver.

Instead, each intermediate node:

Receives the complete message.

Stores it temporarily.

Forwards it to the next node when the communication link becomes available.

This process is called Store-and-Forward Switching.

How Message Switching Works

Step 1

Computer A sends the entire message to Node B.

A

|

| Entire Message

|

▼

B

Step 2

Node B stores the complete message in its memory.

Node B

[Stored Message]

If the next link is busy,

the message waits.

Step 3

When the communication channel becomes free,

Node B forwards the complete message to Node C.

Node B

↓

Node C

Step 4

Node C again stores the complete message.

Node C

[Stored Message]

Step 5

Node C forwards it to the destination.

Computer A

↓

Node B

↓

Node C

↓

Computer D

The message has now reached the destination.

Message Format

Each message contains:

+---------------------------------------+

| Header | Complete Message |

+---------------------------------------+

The Header contains:

Source Address

Destination Address

Components of a Message Switching Network

1. End Stations

These are the sender and receiver.

Example:

Computer

Server

Terminal

2. Switching Nodes

Intermediate devices that store and forward messages.

Example:

Sender

↓

Node A

↓

Node B

↓

Receiver

3. Transmission Links

Communication channels connecting the nodes.

Examples:

Fiber optic cable

Characteristics of Message Switching

1. No Dedicated Path

2. Entire Message is Sent

The whole message travels together.

It is not divided into packets.

3. Hop-by-Hop Communication

The message moves from one node to another.

Advantages of Message Switching

1. Better Traffic Management

Messages can be assigned priorities.

3. No Dedicated Circuit Required

Communication is possible even if no direct path exists.

4. Flexible Message Length

Messages can be of any practical size because they are transmitted as complete units.

5. Reduces Congestion

If a communication channel is busy,

messages are stored until the channel becomes available.

This helps manage network traffic.

Disadvantages of Message Switching

1. High Delay

2. Large Storage Requirement

3. Not Suitable for Real-Time Applications

4. Delivery Uncertainty

Applications of Message Switching

1. Email

Emails are stored on mail servers before being delivered.

4. Store-and-Forward Messaging

Used in systems where direct communication is not always available.

Circuit Switching
Circuit Switching is a switching technique in which a dedicated communication path (circuit) is established between the sender and the receiver before any data is transmitted.

Once the path is established:

The entire bandwidth of the path is reserved.

No other user can use that path.

Data flows continuously until the communication ends.

How Circuit Switching Works
Suppose Computer A wants to communicate with Computer D.

Computer A → Switch 1 → Switch 2 → Computer D

Step 1: Circuit Establishment

A dedicated path is created.

A ===== Switch1 ===== Switch2 ===== D

The ===== represents the reserved communication circuit.

No data is sent yet.

Step 2: Data Transfer

Once the circuit is established, data flows continuously.

A =============================== D

Since the path is already reserved, there is no routing delay for each piece of data.

Step 3: Circuit Disconnection

When communication finishes:

Disconnect

↓

Reserved path removed

↓

Resources become available

The dedicated circuit is released.

Phases of Circuit Switching
There are three phases.

1. Circuit Establishment

Before communication starts:

Sender requests a connection.

Intermediate switches create a dedicated path.

Receiver accepts the request.

2. Data Transfer

After the path is ready:

Data flows continuously through the same path.

3. Circuit Disconnection

When either side ends communication:

The reserved bandwidth becomes available for other users.

Multiplexing in Circuit Switching
A physical communication link can be shared by multiple users using multiplexing.

The two main techniques are:

Frequency Division Multiplexing (FDM)

Time Division Multiplexing (TDM)

1. Frequency Division Multiplexing (FDM)

What is FDM?

The total available bandwidth is divided into multiple frequency bands.

Each user gets a separate frequency band.

All users transmit simultaneously.

Applications

Radio broadcasting

Television broadcasting

2. Time Division Multiplexing (TDM)

What is TDM?

Instead of dividing frequency,

TDM divides time into slots.

Each user transmits only during their assigned time slot.

Applications

Digital telephone systems

Long-distance communication

Characteristics of Circuit Switching

Dedicated communication path.

Fixed bandwidth.

Constant bit delay.

Formulas Used in Circuit Switching

1. Transmission Rate

If:

Link rate = R bps

Number of slots = h

Then:

Transmission Rate=rbps/h

Applications of Circuit Switching

1. Telephone Networks

Advantages of Circuit Switching

1. Guaranteed Data Rate

The reserved path ensures a constant transmission speed.

2. No Delay During Data Transfer

Once the circuit is established, data flows continuously without per-packet routing delays.

3. High Reliability

Disadvantages of Circuit Switching

1. Wastes Bandwidth

2. Expensive

Maintaining dedicated circuits increases cost.

4. Failure of Path

If any link in the dedicated path fails, communication stops until a new circuit is established.

Packet Switching
Packet Switching is a switching technique in which data is divided into small units called packets before transmission.

Each packet is sent independently through the network, and all packets are reassembled at the destination to form the original message.

Unlike Circuit Switching, no dedicated communication path is established.

How Packet Switching Works
Suppose Computer A wants to send data to Computer D.

Computer A → Router → Router → Computer D

Step 1: Divide the Message

Original Message

HELLO WORLD

Becomes:

Packet 1

Packet 2

Packet 3

Packet 4

Step 2: Add a Header

Each packet receives a header containing important information.

+-------------------------------------+

| Header | Data |

+-------------------------------------+

The header usually contains:

Source Address

Destination Address

Packet Number (Sequence Number)

Error-checking information

Step 3: Independent Transmission

Each packet can travel through a different path.

Packet 1 → Route A

Packet 2 → Route B

Packet 3 → Route C

Packet 4 → Route A

This makes packet switching very flexible.

Step 4: Reassembly

At the destination:

Packet 1

Packet 2

Packet 3

Packet 4

↓

HELLO WORLD

The receiver uses the sequence numbers to arrange the packets in the correct order.

Characteristics of Packet Switching

Data is divided into packets.

No dedicated path.

Packets may travel through different routes.

Packets are reassembled at the destination.

Advantages of Packet Switching

1. Efficient Bandwidth Utilization

2. High Reliability

If one route fails,

packets can be sent through another route.

3. Flexibility

Supports different types of data:

Text

Audio

Video

Disadvantages of Packet Switching

1. Packet Loss

Some packets may be lost due to:

Network congestion

Hardware failures

2. Out-of-Order Delivery

Because packets may take different paths, they may arrive in a different order.

3. Variable Delay (Latency)

Different paths have different transmission times.

Applications of Packet Switching

Packet Switching is used in almost every modern network.

Examples:

Internet

Email

Web Browsing

MAC (Media Access Control)
A MAC Address (Media Access Control Address) is a unique physical address assigned to every Network Interface Card (NIC) by the manufacturer. It is used for communication within a Local Area Network (LAN)

Why Do We Need a MAC Address?

Suppose four computers are connected to a switch.

If PC A wants to send data to PC C, the switch must know which device should receive the frame.

The MAC address uniquely identifies each device so the frame reaches the correct destination.

What is a NIC?

NIC (Network Interface Card) is the hardware that connects a device to a network.

It acts as an interface (bridge) between the device and the network by sending and receiving data.

Without a NIC, a device cannot communicate with other devices on a network.

Other Names of NIC

A NIC is also called:

Network Card

Network Adapter

Ethernet Card (for wired networks)

Layers Where NIC Operates
A NIC works at two OSI layers.

1. Physical Layer (Layer 1)

Responsible for:

Sending signals

Receiving signals

Examples:

Electrical signals (Ethernet)

Radio signals (Wi-Fi)

2. Data Link Layer (Layer 2)

Responsible for:

Framing

MAC Addressing

What Does a NIC Do?

Connects the device to a network.

Sends data to the network.

Receives data from the network.

Converts data into network signals.

Uses the MAC address to identify the device.

Working of a NIC
Suppose Computer A sends a file to Computer B.

Step 1

The operating system sends the data to the NIC.

Step 2

The NIC converts the data into signals.

Wired NIC → Electrical signals

Wireless NIC → Radio signals

Step 3

The signals travel through the network.

Step 4

The NIC of Computer B receives the signals and converts them back into data.

Communication is complete.

Characteristics of NIC

1. Physical Network Address (MAC Address)

Every NIC has a unique MAC address.

3. Status Indicator Lights (LEDs)

NICs usually have two LEDs.

Link Light

Shows whether the network cable is connected.

Activity Light

Blinks whenever data is transmitted or received.

Types of NIC

There are two main types.

Wired NIC

Wireless NIC

1. Wired NIC (Ethernet NIC)

A Wired NIC uses Ethernet cables to connect to the network.

Features

Uses physical cables.

High speed (1 Gbps or more).

Stable connection.

More secure.

Used In

Desktop PCs

Servers

Offices

2. Wireless NIC

A Wireless NIC connects using Wi-Fi (radio signals).

Features

No cables required.

Portable and convenient.

Used In

Laptops

Smartphones

Advantages of NIC

1. High-Speed Communication

2. Reliable Connection

3. Device Identification

4. Supports Data Sharing

Disadvantages of NIC

1. Wired NIC Needs Cables

Ethernet cables reduce mobility.

2. Configuration Required

Drivers and network settings must be properly configured.

3. Security Risks

If proper security is not used, attackers may intercept network traffic.

Format of a MAC Address
A MAC address is:

48 bits long

Written as 12 hexadecimal digits

Usually displayed in pairs separated by colons.

Structure of a MAC Address
Example:

00:40:96 : AB:CD:EF

First 3 Bytes (24 bits)

00:40:96

This is called the OUI (Organizationally Unique Identifier).

It identifies the manufacturer.

Examples:

Cisco

Google

HP

Huawei

Last 3 Bytes (24 bits)

AB:CD:EF

Assigned by the manufacturer to uniquely identify the specific NIC.

Types of MAC Addresses
There are three types.

1. Unicast MAC Address

A frame is sent to one specific device.

2. Multicast MAC Address

Only the devices in the multicast group receive the frame.

A frame is sent to a group of devices.

3. Broadcast MAC Address

All connected devices receive the frame.

A frame is sent to every device on the LAN.

Why Do We Need Both IP Address and MAC Address

MAC Address

Used inside a LAN

Provides hop-to-hop delivery

Operates at the Data Link Layer

IP Address

Used between different networks

Provides end-to-end delivery

Operates at the Network Layer

Suppose you send data from Delhi to Mumbai.

The IP address tells the network where the data should finally go.

At each step along the journey, the MAC address is used to deliver the frame to the next device (next hop).

Characteristics of MAC Address

Unique for every NIC.

48-bit binary address (12 hexadecimal digits).

Assigned by the manufacturer.

Used only in LAN communication.

Advantages of MAC Address

Every device has a unique identifier.

Simple to configure and use.

Supported by almost all networking technologies.

Disadvantages of MAC Address

Limited to the local network (LAN).

MAC addresses can be spoofed (forged).

IP Address
An IP Address (Internet Protocol Address) is a unique numerical address assigned to every device connected to a network that uses the Internet Protocol. It helps identify a device and locate it so that data can be sent to the correct destination.

Functions of an IP Address

An IP address performs two main functions:

1. Identification

It uniquely identifies a device connected to a network.

2. Location

It tells the network where the device is located so routers can deliver data correctly.

Components of an IP Address

An IP address has two parts:

1. Network Portion

Identifies the network.

2. Host Portion

Identifies the specific device on that network.

Types of IP Address
There are several ways to classify IP addresses.

1. Public IP Address
A Public IP Address is a unique IP address assigned by an Internet Service Provider (ISP) to a device It allows the device to communicate with other devices over the Internet.

Why Do We Need a Public IP Address?

Suppose you want to open www.google.com.

Your computer sends a request through your router.

Your ISP assigns your network a public IP address, which identifies your network on the Internet.

Without a public IP address, websites on the Internet would not know where to send the requested data.

How Does a Public IP Address Work?
Step 1

Your laptop sends a request to the router.

Step 2

The router sends the request to the ISP.

Step 3

The ISP attaches your public IP address to the request.

Step 4

Google receives the request.

Step 5

Google sends the response back to your public IP address.

Step 6

The router forwards the response to your laptop.

Characteristics of a Public IP Address

1. Globally Unique

Every public IP address is unique across the Internet.

2. Assigned by ISP

Public IP addresses are assigned by your:

JioFiber

3. Internet Accessible

Devices with a public IP can communicate with other devices anywhere on the Internet.

Public IP Address Ranges
1. Class A

Range:

1 – 126

Format:

N.H.H.H

First octet → Network ID

Last three octets → Host ID

Example:

41.167.11.21

Features

Used for large networks.

2. Class B

Range:

128 – 191

Format:

N.N.H.H

Example:

172.25.110.51

Features

Used for medium-sized networks.

3. Class C

Range:

192 – 223

Format:

N.N.N.H

Example:

200.120.50.11

Features

Used for small networks.

Public IP vs External IP Address
According to the source:

A Public IP Address is assigned by an ISP and allows devices or networks to be accessible from anywhere on the Internet.

An External IP Address is generally similar, but in some environments (such as cloud computing), it may specifically refer to the address used to access a service from outside its private network.

Are Public IP Addresses Traceable?

Yes, but only to a certain extent.

A public IP address can reveal:

Approximate country

Region

Sometimes the city

Internet Service Provider (ISP)

However, it does not directly reveal your personal information such as your name or exact home address.

Advantages of Public IP Address

Enables communication over the Internet.

Allows hosting of websites and online applications.

Makes remote access possible.

Supports communication between networks worldwide.

Disadvantages of Public IP Address

Devices with public IP addresses are more exposed to Internet-based attacks if not properly secured.

Public IP addresses can reveal approximate location and ISP information

Port Number
A Port Number is a 16-bit logical number that identifies a specific process, service, or application running on a computer.

While an IP address identifies the device, the port number identifies the application or service on that device.

Why Do We Need Port Numbers?

Suppose your computer is running:

Google Chrome

Gmail

WhatsApp

Zoom

All these applications use the same IP address.

If data arrives at your computer, how does the operating system know which application should receive it?

The answer is Port Numbers.

How Does a Port Number Work?
Suppose you open a website.

The request reaches the server.

The server checks the destination port.

Since HTTP listens on Port 80, the request is sent to the web server.

The web server processes the request and sends the webpage back.

there are 65,536 possible port numbers.

Types of Port Numbers
Port numbers are divided into three categories.

1. Well-Known Ports
Range:

0 – 1023

These ports are reserved for standard Internet services.

Common Well-Known Ports

Port Service

20, 21 FTP

22 SSH

23 Telnet

25 SMTP

53 DNS

67, 68 DHCP

80 HTTP

2. Registered Ports
Range:

1024 – 49151

These ports are registered for specific software or applications.

Examples:

Database servers

Gaming applications

3. Dynamic (Ephemeral) Ports
Range:

49152 – 65535

These ports are used temporarily.

The operating system automatically assigns them when a client connects to a server.

Example:

Your browser may temporarily use Port 52345 to connect to a website.

After communication ends, the port is released.

Importance of Port Numbers

1. Routes Data to the Correct Service

The IP address brings the packet to the correct computer.

The port number delivers it to the correct application.

2. Allows Multiple Services on One Computer

A server can run:

Web Server

Mail Server

FTP Server

All on the same IP address.

Different port numbers keep them separate.

3. Supports Temporary Connections

How IP Address and Port Number Work Together
Suppose you visit:

https://www.google.com

Your browser sends a request like:

Destination IP:

142.250.xxx.xxx

Destination Port:

443

IP Address → Finds Google's server.

Port 443 → Sends the request to Google's HTTPS service.

Both are required for successful communication.

Cyber Attack
A Cyber Attack is an attempt by hackers or cybercriminals to steal, damage, destroy, or gain unauthorized access to a computer system, network, or data using digital methods.

The main goal of a cyber attack may be to:

Steal sensitive information

Damage systems

Disrupt services

How Does a Cyber Attack Work?
A typical cyber attack follows these steps:

Attacker

│

│ Sends fake email/link or malware

▼

Victim

│

│ Clicks malicious link or opens attachment

▼

Malware Installed / Credentials Stolen

│

▼

Attacker Gains Access

│

▼

Data Theft / System Damage / Ransom

Categories of Cyber Attacks
Cyber attacks are mainly divided into two categories.

1. Untargeted Cyber Attack

In an untargeted attack, hackers do not target a specific person.

Instead, they attack many users or devices hoping that some victims will fall for the attack.

Examples

A. Phishing

Hackers send fake emails to thousands of people.

Victims enter their login details on a fake website.

B. Ransomware

Hackers lock your files and demand money.

2. Targeted Cyber Attack

A targeted attack focuses on a specific individual, company, or organization.

These attacks are carefully planned.

Examples

A. Spear-Malware Emails

A specially crafted email is sent to one employee.

The attachment contains malware.

B. Focused Credential Theft

Hackers create customized fake websites or messages to steal passwords from a particular victim.

Types of Cyber Attacks

1. Phishing Attack

A phishing attack uses fake emails or messages pretending to be from trusted organizations.

2. Whale Phishing Attack

Also called Whaling.

It targets high-ranking executives.

Targets

CEO

Founder

Director

Company President

The emails appear highly professional.

3. Spear Phishing Attack

A phishing attack aimed at one specific person or organization.

Unlike normal phishing, the message is personalized.

5. Malware Attack

Malware means Malicious Software.

Examples:

Virus

Worm

Trojan Horse

Malware can:

Delete files

Corrupt data

Spy on users

Steal passwords

It often enters through:

Email attachments

Fake software downloads

Infected USB drives

6. Web Attack

Targets websites and web servers.

Goal:

Steal customer information

Attack online payment systems

Exploit website vulnerabilities

7. DoS (Denial of Service) Attack

A DoS attack floods a server with huge numbers of requests.

9. Brute Force Attack

Hackers use automated software to try every possible password.

Prevention of Cyber Attacks
1. Use Strong Passwords

A strong password should include:

Uppercase letters

Lowercase letters

Numbers

2. Do Not Trust Unknown Emails

Always:

Verify the sender.

Avoid clicking suspicious links.

3. Secure Your Devices

Protect your computer by:

Installing antivirus software

Enabling the firewall

Advantages of Cyber Security (Against Cyber Attacks)

By preventing cyber attacks, organizations can:

Protect confidential data.

Ensure uninterrupted services.

Prevent financial losses.

Maintain customer trust.

Reduce the risk of malware and ransomware.

Private IP Address
A Private IP Address is an IP address that is used only inside a local network (LAN), such as a home, school, or office network. These addresses are not accessible or routable on the Internet.

Private IP addresses are assigned by a router or network administrator, and they allow devices on the same network to communicate with each other.

Why Do We Need Private IP Addresses?
Imagine a company has:

500 computers

100 printers

50 servers

300 smartphones

Giving each device a public IP address would be expensive and would quickly exhaust the limited supply of public IPs.

Instead:

Every device gets a private IP address.

The entire network uses one public IP address to access the Internet through NAT (Network Address Translation).

This saves public IP addresses and improves security.

Characteristics of Private IP Address

Used only within a local network (LAN).

Cannot be accessed directly from the Internet.

Assigned by a router or network administrator.

Can be reused in different networks.

Where Are Private IP Addresses Used?
1. Home Networks

Home routers assign private IP addresses to:

Computers

Smartphones

Smart TVs

Printers

IoT devices

All these devices communicate with each other using private IPs.

2. Business Networks

Companies use private IP addresses for:

Employee computers

Servers

Printers

Databases

Employees can share files and resources securely within the organization.

3. Virtual Private Networks (VPNs)

VPNs allow remote users to securely access a private network over the Internet.

Private IP addresses are used inside the VPN for secure communication.

4. Cloud Computing

Cloud providers create Virtual Private Clouds (VPCs) where:

Virtual Machines

Databases

Containers

communicate using private IP addresses while remaining isolated from other customers.

How to Find Your Private IP Address?
Windows

Open Command Prompt and type:

ipconfig

The IPv4 Address displayed is your private IP address.

Private IPv4 Address Ranges
The Internet Assigned Numbers Authority (IANA) reserves three IPv4 ranges for private networks.

Class Private IP Range Used For

Class A 10.0.0.0 – 10.255.255.255 Large organizations

Class B 172.16.0.0 – 172.31.255.255 Medium-sized networks

Class C 192.168.0.0 – 192.168.255.255 Home and small office networks

IPv6 Private Addresses
In IPv6, private addresses are called Unique Local Addresses (ULA).

Benefits of Private IP Addresses
1. Security

Private IP addresses cannot be accessed directly from the Internet, reducing the risk of unauthorized access and cyberattacks.

2. Scalability

Private IP ranges provide millions of addresses, allowing organizations to expand their networks

3. Flexibility

Network administrators can freely assign and manage private IP addresses

Limitations of Private IP Addresses

1. Limited Accessibility

Devices with private IPs cannot be reached directly from the Internet.

2. NAT Overhead

To communicate with the Internet, private IP addresses must use Network Address Translation (NAT), which adds processing overhead.

4. Interoperability Issues

Some external services require public IP addresses

How Private IP Works with NAT
Devices in Home Network

PC → 192.168.1.2

Laptop → 192.168.1.3

Phone → 192.168.1.4

│

▼

Router (NAT)

Private IPs ⇄ Public IP

│

▼

Internet

Devices communicate internally using private IP addresses.

The router translates private IPs into a public IP when accessing the Internet.

Advantages of Private IP Addresses

Conserves public IP addresses.

Improves network security.

Supports communication within local networks.

Allows address reuse across different networks.

What is a Router?
A Router is a networking device that connects two or more different networks and forwards data packets from one network to another using IP addresses.

It decides the best path for data packets to reach their destination

Without a router, devices on different networks cannot communicate.

How Does a Router Work?
When you access a website:

Your Computer

│

▼

Router

│

Reads Destination IP Address

│

Checks Routing Table

│

Chooses Best Path

│

Forwards Packet

▼

Next Router

▼

Destination Server

The router performs three main steps:

Receives the packet.

Checks the destination IP address.

Sends the packet through the best available route.

Routing Table
A Routing Table is a database inside the router.

It contains information about:

Destination networks

Available paths

Next hop

Cost/metric

The router consults this table before forwarding packets.

Types of Routing
1. Static Routing

Configured manually by a network administrator.

Does not change automatically.

Advantages

Simple

Secure

Low overhead

Disadvantages

Time-consuming to configure

Not suitable for large networks

2. Dynamic Routing

Routes are updated automatically.

Uses routing protocols.

Advantages

Automatically adapts to network changes.

Better scalability.

Disadvantages

More complex

Functions of a Router
1. Forwarding

The router receives packets and sends them to the correct outgoing interface based on the destination IP address.

2. Routing

The router determines the best path using routing tables and routing algorithms.

3. Network Address Translation (NAT)

Routers translate:

Private IP Address → Public IP Address

This allows multiple devices to share a single Internet connection.

Router Architecture
A router mainly consists of four components.

1. Input Port

Receives incoming packets.

Determines where the packet should go.

2. Switching Fabric

The switching fabric is the internal connection system that transfers packets from input ports to output ports.

Types of Switching Fabric
A. Memory Switching

CPU copies packets into memory.

Packets are then sent to the output port.

Slower.

B. Bus Switching

Uses a shared system bus.

Only one packet can use the bus at a time.

Faster than memory switching but limited by bus speed.

C. Interconnection Network

Uses dedicated hardware to connect multiple input and output ports.

Very fast.

3. Output Port

Stores outgoing packets in a queue if necessary.

Sends packets onto the next network.

4. Routing Processor

The routing processor is the brain of the router.

It:

Runs routing protocols.

Updates routing tables.

Makes routing decisions.

Common Routing Protocols
1. OSPF (Open Shortest Path First)

Finds the shortest path between networks.

Commonly used inside organizations.

2. BGP (Border Gateway Protocol)

Used between Internet Service Providers (ISPs).

Exchanges routing information between large networks on the Internet.

3. IGRP (Interior Gateway Routing Protocol)

Exchanges routing info within autonomous networks.

4. EIGRP (Enhanced IGRP)

Improved version of IGRP.

Learns routes from neighboring routers.

5. EGP (Exterior Gateway Protocol)

Used for sharing routing information between different networks.

Types of Routers
1. Broadband Router

Connects home or office devices to the Internet.

Shares one broadband connection.

Example: Home Wi-Fi router.

2. Wireless Router

Creates a Wi-Fi network.

Used in:

Homes

Offices

Schools

3. Wired Router

Uses Ethernet cables instead of Wi-Fi.

Advantages:

Faster

More secure

Lower latency

4. Edge Router

Located at the boundary of a network.

Connects an organization's network to an ISP or another external network.

5. Core Router

Used inside large networks.

Handles heavy traffic between different parts of the same network.

Found in:

ISPs

Data centers

6. Virtual Router

A software-based router running on a virtual machine instead of dedicated hardware.

7. Portable Router

A small, mobile router that creates a private Wi-Fi network.

Commonly used while traveling.

Applications of Routers
Routers are used to:

Connect different networks.

Share Internet connections.

Connect homes, schools, and offices.

Support wired and wireless communication.

Enable VPN access.

Security Challenges of Routers
1. Firmware Vulnerabilities

Outdated router firmware may contain security flaws that attackers can exploit.

2. DDoS Attacks

Attackers may flood the router with excessive traffic, making network services unavailable.

3. Default Administrator Credentials

Many routers use default usernames and passwords (e.g., admin/admin).

If not changed, attackers can gain unauthorized access.

Virtual Private Network (VPN)
A Virtual Private Network (VPN) is a technology that creates a secure, encrypted connection between your device and the internet. It protects your online privacy by encrypting your data and hiding your real IP address.

Without VPN

Your Device ───────────► Website

│

└── ISP can see your activity and IP address

With VPN

Your Device

│

▼

[Encrypted VPN Tunnel]

│

▼

VPN Server

│

▼

Website

Website sees VPN Server's IP, not yours.

Why Do We Need a VPN?

Normally, when you browse the internet:

Your ISP (Internet Service Provider) can see which websites you visit.

Hackers on public Wi-Fi may intercept your data.

Websites can identify your approximate location through your IP address.

A VPN solves these problems by encrypting your internet traffic.

Advantages of VPN

1. Privacy Protection

A VPN hides your real IP address.

2. Security on Public Wi-Fi

Suppose you're using free Wi-Fi at:

Airport

Railway Station

Café

Hotel

Without VPN:

You → Public Wi-Fi → Internet

Hackers may intercept passwords.

With VPN:

You

│

Encrypted Tunnel

│

Public Wi-Fi

│

Internet

Even if someone captures your traffic, it appears as unreadable encrypted data.

3. Bypass Geo-Restrictions

Some websites are available only in certain countries

4. Reduce ISP Throttling

Some ISPs intentionally slow down:

Streaming

Gaming

Torrent downloads

Since VPN encrypts your traffic:

ISP cannot easily determine what you're doing.

Therefore, selective throttling becomes harder.

How VPN Works
Step 1: Connection Establishment

You open the VPN app.

Step 2: Data Encryption

Suppose you type:

Password = hello123

Before leaving your computer:

Anyone intercepting it sees only meaningless encrypted text.

Step 3: Tunnelling

Your encrypted data travels through a secure tunnel.

No one can easily read the data while it is in transit.

Step 4: Decryption

The VPN server decrypts your data.

The server forwards it to the destination website.

Step 5: Website Responds

The website sends data back to the VPN server.

The VPN encrypts the response before sending it back.

Types of VPN (Based on Usage)
1. Remote Access VPN

Used by individual users to connect securely to a private network.

Common use:

Work from home

Company employees

2. Site-to-Site VPN

Connects two entire networks.

Example:

Head Office

│

VPN

│

Branch Office

Employees at both locations communicate securely.

3. Mobile VPN

Designed for smartphones and tablets.

It keeps the VPN connected even if you switch between:

Wi-Fi

↓

4G

↓

5G

The connection remains active.

4. MPLS VPN

Used mainly by large organizations.

Office A

│

MPLS Network

│

Office B

Features:

High performance

Traffic prioritization

VPN Protocols
A VPN protocol defines how the secure tunnel is created and how data is protected.

1. OpenVPN

One of the most popular VPN protocols.

Advantages:

Very secure

Open-source

Cross-platform

Reliable

Can use:

UDP → Faster

TCP → More reliable

2. WireGuard

A modern VPN protocol.

Advantages:

Extremely fast

Lightweight

3. IKEv2/IPsec

Uses:

IKEv2 for tunnel setup

IPsec for encryption

Best feature:

Automatically reconnects if the network changes.

Example:

Wi-Fi

↓

Mobile Data

VPN reconnects instantly.

Widely used in enterprise environments.

4. L2TP/IPsec

L2TP creates the tunnel.

IPsec encrypts the data.

Advantages:

More secure than PPTP

Supported by many operating systems

5. PPTP

One of the oldest VPN protocols.

Advantages:

Very fast

Simple setup

Disadvantages:

Weak encryption

Vulnerable to attacks

6. SSTP

Developed by Microsoft.

Uses:

HTTPS (TCP Port 443)

Advantages:

Strong security

Works well on Windows

Can bypass many firewalls because it resembles normal HTTPS traffic

Disadvantage:

Limited support on non-Windows platforms.

Drawbacks of VPN
1. Slower Internet

2. Not All VPNs Are Trustworthy

Some providers:

Keep activity logs

Use weak encryption

May sell user data

Choose a reputable VPN provider.

3. Some Websites Block VPNs

Certain services (such as some streaming platforms or websites) detect and block VPN traffic, preventing access.

4. Configuration Can Be Complex

5. Premium VPNs Cost Money

Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC)
Amazon VPC is AWS's networking service that lets you create a private, isolated virtual network inside the AWS cloud, where you control IP addresses, subnets, routing, and security for your cloud resources.

What is Amazon VPC?

Imagine AWS is a huge city with millions of buildings.

When you create an AWS account, you don't want your servers to be mixed with everyone else's.

AWS gives you your own private area inside its cloud.

That private area is called a Virtual Private Cloud (VPC).

Every customer has their own isolated network.

VPC vs Traditional Private Cloud
The file compares AWS VPC with a traditional private cloud:

AWS VPC Traditional Private Cloud

Easily scalable Limited by hardware

Pay only for what you use Expensive to build and maintain

Easy connection with AWS services Limited connectivity

Uses Security Groups, NACLs, IAM Relies on firewalls and VPNs

Components of Amazon VPC
A VPC is made up of several networking components that work together to create a secure cloud environment.

1. VPC
A VPC is your private network inside AWS.

Everything inside belongs to you.

The VPC is assigned a private IP address range using a CIDR block.

2. Subnets
Subnetting is the process of dividing one large network into multiple smaller networks, called subnets.

Why Do We Need Subnetting?
Suppose a company owns the network:

192.168.1.0/24

A /24 network has:

256 IP addresses

Suppose the company has:

Department Devices

Sales 20

HR 10

IT 50

Without subnetting:

256 IPs

Only 80 used

176 wasted

Everyone shares one large network.

After subnetting:

Department Devices Subnet

Sales 20 192.168.1.0/27

HR 10 192.168.1.32/28

IT 50 192.168.1.64/26

Each department gets only the IP addresses it actually needs.

Benefits of Subnetting

1. Efficient IP Address Usage

Instead of wasting addresses:

Whole Network

256 IPs

Used = 80

Unused = 176

After subnetting:

Each department receives only the required IPs.

Less wastage.

2. Better Performance

3. Better Security

Network Portion

Identifies which network the device belongs to.

Example

192.168.1.xxx

All devices having this network portion belong to the same network.

Host Portion

Identifies the individual device.

Example

192.168.1.10

192.168.1.20

192.168.1.35

All are different hosts inside the same network.

What is a Subnet Mask?
A Subnet Mask tells the computer:

Which bits belong to the network

Which bits belong to the host

Example

IP Address

192.168.1.10

Subnet Mask

255.255.255.0

CIDR Notation
Instead of writing

255.255.255.0

We simply write

/24

Meaning

24 bits = Network

8 bits = Host

Number of subnets = 2ⁿ, where n = number of host bits borrowed

Usable hosts per subnet = 2ʰ − 2, where h = remaining host bits

Advantages of Subnetting

1. Improved Security

2. Better Performance

3. Easier Maintenance

Disadvantages of Subnetting

1. Wasted Addresses

2. Higher Cost

3. More Complexity

Role of Subnet Mask
A subnet mask is a 32-bit number that works together with an IP address.

Its main job is to divide an IP address into two parts:

Network ID

Host ID

Example

IP Address

192.168.1.25

Subnet Mask

255.255.255.0

This means:

192.168.1 | 25

-----------|----

Network Host

Network ID = 192.168.1

Host ID = 25

Meaning of Binary 1s and 0s
In a subnet mask:

1 → Network Portion

0 → Host Portion

Example:

11111111.11111111.11111111.00000000

This means:

Network = First 24 bits

Host = Last 8 bits

That is exactly what /24 means.

Main Role of a Subnet Mask

The subnet mask helps a computer answer this question:

"Is the destination computer on my network or on another network?"

If Yes:

Computer A

│

Switch

│

Computer B

The computers communicate directly.

If No:

Computer A

│

Router

│

Computer B

The packet is sent to the router, which forwards it to the correct network.

Functions of a Subnet Mask
1. Separates Network ID and Host ID

2. Creates Smaller Networks (Subnetting)

Suppose you have one large network:

192.168.1.0/24

It contains 256 IP addresses.

You divide it into smaller networks:

Sales

192.168.1.0/26

HR

192.168.1.64/26

IT

192.168.1.128/26

Finance

192.168.1.192/26

Now each department has its own subnet.

3. Improves Network Performance

Without subnetting:

Broadcast

↓

Entire Network

Every computer receives the broadcast.

With subnetting:

Broadcast

↓

Only Sales Subnet

4. Helps Routers Forward Packets

Routers compare:

Destination IP

Subnet Mask

Then decide where to send the packet.

With Subnetting

The computer identifies:

1. Network

↓

2. Subnet

↓

3. Host

↓

4. Process

The extra Subnet level allows the network to be organized into smaller sections.

Example: Divide a Class C Network into 4 Subnets

Given network:

200.1.2.0/24

A /24 network has:

24 Network Bits

8 Host Bits

To create 4 subnets, borrow 2 host bits.

Why?

2² = 4

New Subnet Mask

Original mask:

255.255.255.0

Binary

11111111.11111111.11111111.00000000

3. Route Tables
A route table decides where network traffic should go.

A routing table is a table stored in the router's RAM that contains information about known networks and the best path to reach them.

when a router receives a packet, it looks at its routing table and decides which interface (port) the packet should leave from.

What is the Default Route?
Sometimes a router doesn't know where a destination is.

Instead of dropping the packet immediately, it sends it to a default gateway.

The default route is always:

Destination : 0.0.0.0

Subnet Mask : 0.0.0.0

Entries in a Routing Table
Each row (entry) contains important information.

1. Network ID (Destination)

This is the destination network.

2. Subnet Mask

The subnet mask tells the router how much of the IP address is the network portion.

The router performs a bitwise AND between:

Destination IP

Subnet Mask

3. Next Hop

Sometimes the destination is not directly connected.

The router forwards the packet to another router.

Types of Routes Stored in a Routing Table
1. Directly Attached Networks

These are networks directly connected to the router.

The router automatically knows these networks.

No manual configuration is needed.

2. Remote Networks

These are networks connected through another router.

3. Host Route

A route to one specific device.

Only that single computer is matched.

4. Default Route

Used when no other route matches.

How Are Routing Tables Created?
There are three ways.

1. Directly Connected Networks

These are added automatically.

Example:

Router

↓

Network Connected

↓

Added Automatically

2. Static Routing

The administrator manually enters routes.

Example:

Network Admin

↓

Configure Route

↓

Saved in Routing Table

Advantages:

Simple

Secure

No routing protocol overhead

Disadvantages:

Not suitable for large networks

Must be updated manually

3. Dynamic Routing

Routers automatically exchange routing information using routing protocols (such as RIP, OSPF, or EIGRP).

Example:

Router A

⇄

Router B

⇄

Router C

Advantages:

Automatic updates

Suitable for large networks

4. Network ACL (NACL)
A Network ACL is a firewall for an entire subnet.

Internet

↓

NACL

↓

Subnet

It checks every packet entering or leaving the subnet.

Characteristics:

Works at subnet level

5. Internet Gateway (IGW)
A Gateway is a network device that connects two different networks and translates data so that devices using different protocols can communicate.

It is often called a Protocol Converter because it converts one network protocol into another when necessary.

Why Do We Need a Gateway?

Suppose your home computer wants to access a website.

Without the gateway:

Your computer cannot reach the Internet.

Different networks cannot exchange data.

The gateway acts as the door connecting your local network to other networks.

Features of Gateways
1. Located at the Edge of the Network

A gateway sits at the boundary of a network.

2. Works at Any OSI Layer

Unlike devices such as switches or routers that mainly operate at specific layers, a gateway can operate at any layer of the OSI model, depending on the type of conversion it performs.

3. Packet Handling

Instead of sending large amounts of data at once, a gateway can process and forward data as smaller packets.

4. Provides Security

Since all traffic passes through the gateway, it can inspect and filter data.

Types of Gateways (Based on Data Flow)
1. Unidirectional Gateway

Data flows in only one direction.

2. Bidirectional Gateway

Data flows in both directions.

Types of Gateways (Based on Function)
1. Network Gateway

This is the most common type.

It connects two different networks using different protocols.

2. Cloud Storage Gateway

It connects local storage with cloud storage services.

It translates storage requests between local applications and cloud services.

3. Internet-to-Orbit (I2O) Gateway

Used to communicate between:

Internet

Satellites

4. IoT Gateway

Used in the Internet of Things (IoT).

5. VoIP Trunk Gateway: By using a VoIP (voice over Internet Protocol) network, it makes data transmission between POTS (plain old telephone service) devices like landlines and fax machines easier.

How Does a Gateway Work?
Step 1: Receive Data

A device sends data.

Step 2: Analyze the Packet

The gateway examines:

Destination address

Source address

Packet header

Step 3: Determine the Destination

The gateway decides where the packet should go.

Step 4: Convert the Data (if needed)

If the destination network uses a different protocol, the gateway converts the packet.

Step 5: Forward the Packet

Finally, the gateway sends the packet to its destination.

Limitations of Gateways

1. Time Delay

Protocol conversion and packet inspection take time.

2. Single Point of Failure

If the gateway fails:

The network may lose connectivity with other networks, such as the Internet.

3. Complex and Expensive

Gateways are more complex to configure than basic networking devices and may increase implementation costs.

SOAP (Simple Object Access Protocol)
SOAP and REST are two popular ways for applications to communicate over a network.

Think of them as two different ways of sending messages between a client and a server.

What is SOAP?

SOAP (Simple Object Access Protocol) is an XML-based messaging protocol used to exchange structured information between a client and a server.

Features of SOAP
1. XML-Based

SOAP messages are always written in XML.

Example

1001

2. Supports Multiple Transport Protocols

SOAP can work over:

HTTP

HTTPS

SMTP

3. Built-in Security

SOAP supports WS-Security, making it suitable for:

Banking

Online payments

4. Reliable Messaging

SOAP ensures that important messages are delivered correctly.

5. Uses WSDL

SOAP uses WSDL (Web Services Description Language).

WSDL acts like a contract that tells clients:

What operations are available

What data to send

What data will be received

Which URL to use

Components of SOAP Architecture
1. SOAP Client

The application that sends the request.

Example:

Mobile app

Website

2. SOAP Message

The XML document exchanged between client and server.

It contains:

Request

Response

3. SOAP Server (Web Service)

Receives the request.

Processes it.

Returns the response.

4. Transport Protocol

Carries SOAP messages.

Usually:

HTTP

But SOAP can also use:

HTTPS

SMTP

TCP

Working of SOAP
The working is very simple.

Step 1

The client creates a SOAP request.

Step 3

The server processes the request.

Step 4

The server creates a SOAP response.

Step 5

The response is returned to the client.

Structure of a SOAP Message
Every SOAP message has four parts.

SOAP Envelope

│

├── Header (Optional)

├── Body

└── Fault (Optional)

1. SOAP Envelope

The Envelope is the root element.

It tells the receiver:

"This document is a SOAP message."

Without the Envelope, the message is not recognized as a SOAP message.

2. SOAP Header (Optional)

Contains extra information.

Examples:

Authentication

Security

Routing

3. SOAP Body

Contains the actual request or response.

4. SOAP Fault (Optional)

Used if an error occurs.

Example:

Invalid Customer ID

OR

Authentication Failed

SOAP returns a Fault message.

WSDL (Web Services Description Language)
WSDL is an XML document describing the SOAP service.

Think of it as an instruction manual or contract.

It tells the client:

Service name

Available operations

Input format

Output format

Without WSDL, the client would not know how to call the web service correctly.

Advantages of SOAP

1. High Security

2. Reliable Messaging

3. Transaction Support

Limitations of SOAP

1. Large XML Messages

2. Complex

3. Slower

4. Not Suitable for Mobile Apps

REST API (Representational State Transfer)
A REST API allows a client and a server to communicate over HTTP, usually exchanging data in JSON format.

REST Architecture

Client

↓

HTTP Request

↓

Server

↓

JSON Response

↓

Client

HTTP Methods Used in REST
1. GET

Used to retrieve data.

2. POST

Used to create a new resource.

Meaning:

Create a new user.

3. PUT

Used to replace the entire resource

Entire user data must be sent.

If the user doesn't exist, PUT can create it.

4. PATCH

Used to update only selected fields.

example

Only the email changes.

Everything else remains the same.

Difference Between PUT and PATCH

PUT PATCH

Updates the entire resource Updates only selected fields

Sends complete data Sends only changed fields

5. DELETE

Used to remove a resource.

Features of REST APIs

Stateless: Each request contains all the information needed; the server does not store client session state.

Client-Server Architecture: Separates the client and server, improving scalability.

Cacheable Responses: Responses can be cached to improve performance.

Real-World Applications of REST APIs

REST APIs are commonly used in:

Social media platforms

E-commerce websites

Weather applications

GPS and geolocation services

limitations of REST APIs

Each request must include all required information, which can increase request size.

Not ideal for real-time communication because it follows a request-response model.

Clients may receive more or less data than needed in some cases.

REST does not enforce strict standards like SOAP, so implementations can vary.

API versioning and backward compatibility can become challenging as APIs grow.

SOAP vs REST

Feature SOAP REST

Type Protocol Architectural style

Data Format XML only Usually JSON (can also use XML, HTML, etc.)

Speed Slower Faster

Message Size Large Small

Security Built-in (WS-Security) Typically uses HTTPS and other mechanisms

Complexity More complex Simpler

Best Use Banking, enterprise Web apps, mobile apps, public APIs

1. Voice over Internet Protocol (VoIP)
VoIP (Voice over Internet Protocol) is a technology that allows people to make voice calls over the Internet instead of traditional telephone lines.

Instead of sending voice as analog electrical signals through telephone cables, VoIP converts your voice into digital data packets and sends them through the Internet.

How VoIP Works
Step 1: Speak

You speak into your phone or computer microphone.

↓

Step 2: Voice Conversion

Your voice (analog signal) is converted into digital data.

↓

Step 3: Packetization

The digital data is divided into small packets.

↓

Step 4: Internet Transmission

The packets travel through the Internet.

↓

Step 5: Reconstruction

The receiver's device combines the packets.

↓

Step 6: Digital to Analog

The digital packets are converted back into sound.

↓

Step 7: Listener hears your voice.

Equipment Required

High-speed Internet

Computer, smartphone or VoIP phone

Microphone

Speaker

Evolution of VoIP
1995

VocalTec introduced the first VoIP software called InternetPhone.

Problems:

Poor sound quality

Frequent call drops

No video

2003

Skype became popular.

Improvements:

Better voice quality

Video calling

Cheap international calls

COVID-19 Pandemic

Remote work increased.

VoIP applications became essential.

Examples:

Zoom

Teams

Google Meet

Advantages of VoIP

1. Low Cost

2. Free International Calls

3. Extra Features

Video calls

Call forwarding

Voicemail

Conference calls

4. Portable

5. Easy Installation

Disadvantages of VoIP

1. Needs Internet

2. Power Failure

3. Delay (Latency)

What is a Signal?
A signal is a function of one or more variables hat represents or carries information about a physical phenomenon. In communication and electronics, signals are used to transfer information from one device or system to another.

Examples of Signals

Human voice and sound waves

Voltage in electrical circuits

Temperature measured by a thermostat

Types of Signals

The file classifies signals into five main types.

1. Analog Signal

Continuous in time.

Can take any value within a range.

Usually represented by smooth sine waves.

More affected by noise.

Examples:

Sound waves

Temperature

Audio signals

Television signals

2. Digital Signal

Discrete (not continuous).

Represented using binary values (0 and 1).

More resistant to noise.

Widely used in computers and telecommunications.

Examples:

Computer data

Digital audio

Internet communication

3. Real and Complex Signals

Real Signal

Contains only real-number values.

Complex Signal

Contains real and imaginary parts.

4. Deterministic and Random Signals

Deterministic Signal

Can be described exactly by a mathematical equation.

Future values are predictable.

Random Signal

Cannot be predicted exactly.

Requires statistical methods for analysis.

Example

Deterministic: Sine wave

Random: Noise

5. Periodic and Non-Periodic Signals

Periodic Signal

Repeats after a fixed interval (period).

Example:

AC voltage

Sine wave

Non-Periodic (Aperiodic) Signal

Does not repeat.

Example:

Speech signal

Music signal

Signal Parameters

Important characteristics of a signal are:

Amplitude: Maximum strength or height of the signal.

Frequency: Number of cycles per second (measured in Hertz).

Phase: Indicates the position of the waveform relative to a reference.

Wavelength: Physical distance covered by one complete cycle.

Digital Signal Processing (DSP)
Digital Signal Processing is the processing of signals in digital form.

Steps:

Convert an analog signal to digital using an ADC (Analog-to-Digital Converter).

Process the digital signal using digital circuits or computers.

Convert it back to analog using a DAC (Digital-to-Analog Converter) if needed.

Basic Signals
The file describes four fundamental signals:

1. Unit Step Signal u(t)

Used to represent switching operations.

2. Unit Impulse Signal δ(t)

Used in system analysis.

3. Unit Ramp Signal r(t)

Increases linearly with time.

Used to model steadily increasing quantities.

4. Unit Parabolic Signal

Amplitude increases with the square of time

Applications of Signals

Signals are widely used in:

Communication systems

Telephone and wireless communication

Audio systems

Audio and video signal processing

Functional Signals

These include:

Sinc Function

Oscillates with decreasing amplitude.

Important in Fourier analysis and communication systems.

Signum Function

Indicates whether a value is positive, negative, or zero.

Continuous-Time Complex Exponential

If the exponent is positive → exponential growth.

If negative → exponential decay.

Used to model many physical and communication processes.

Geometric Signals

Two common geometric signals are:

Rectangular pulse

Triangular pulse

Both are symmetric and commonly used in signal analysis

What is a Digital Signal?
A digital signal represents information using binary digits (0 and 1).

Instead of continuously changing values, it has only two states.

____ _____

|____|

Characteristics

Discrete values

Binary (0 and 1)

High accuracy

Easy processing

Applications

Communication

Audio

Video

Storage

Advantages

Less noise

High accuracy

Easy compression

Secure encryption

Easy storage

Disadvantages

Requires higher bandwidth

Complex hardware

Analog-to-Digital conversion required

Quantization errors possible

SSD = Solid State Drive

It stores data using flash memory chips instead of rotating magnetic disks.

HDD vs SSD

HDD

Uses rotating disks.

SSD

Uses flash memory chips.

No noise

Disadvantages

Analog Signal
What is an Analog Signal?

An analog signal is a continuous signal whose value changes smoothly over time.

Example:

Human voice

Temperature

Graph

Characteristics

Continuous

Infinite values

Time varying

Natural signals

Disadvantages

Relationship Between Analog, Digital and VoIP
Suppose you make a WhatsApp call.

Step 1

You speak.

Voice is an analog signal.

↓

Step 2

Your phone converts it into a digital signal (0s and 1s).

↓

The digital data is sent over the Internet using VoIP.

↓

RADAR (Radio Detection and Ranging)
RADAR (Radio Detection and Ranging) is a technology that uses radio waves to detect the position, distance, speed, and direction of objects such as aircraft, ships, vehicles

Working of RADAR

Step 1

The transmitter generates radio waves.

↓

Step 2

The antenna sends them into space.

↓

Step 3

The waves hit an object.

↓

Step 4

The object reflects the waves.

↓

Step 5

The receiver receives the reflected signal.

↓

Step 6

The processor calculates:

Distance

Direction

Speed

Position

using the time delay and signal characteristics

Components of RADAR

Transmitter

Antenna

Receiver

Processor

Types of RADAR
1. Pulse Radar

Sends short pulses.

Measures distance.

2. Continuous Wave (CW) Radar

Sends continuous waves.

Measures speed using the Doppler effect.

3. Doppler Radar

Measures speed of moving objects.

Used in weather forecasting.

4. Synthetic Aperture Radar (SAR)

Produces high-resolution images of the Earth's surface.

Applications of RADAR

RADAR is used in:

Air traffic control

Weather forecasting

Military surveillance

Missile guidance

2. SONAR (Sound Navigation and Ranging)
SONAR (Sound Navigation and Ranging) is a technology that uses ultrasonic sound waves to detect underwater objects, measure depth, and determine distance. It is mainly used by ships and submarines.

Working of SONAR

Step 1

The transmitter emits ultrasonic waves.

↓

Step 2

The waves travel through water.

↓

Step 3

They strike an underwater object.

↓

Step 4

The waves are reflected back as echoes.

↓

Step 5

The receiver receives the echoes.

↓

Step 6

The time taken is measured to calculate the object's distance.

Components of SONAR

1. Transmitter (Emitter)

Produces ultrasonic waves.

2. Receiver

Receives reflected echoes.

3. Ultrasonic Waves

Sound waves with frequency greater than 20 kHz.

Applications (Uses) of SONAR

SONAR is used for:

Underwater communication

Measuring sea depth

Detecting submarines

Detecting underwater rocks and icebergs

Military surveillance

Echolocation in Bats

Bats also use ultrasonic waves.

They:

Produce ultrasonic sound.

Receive reflected echoes.

Determine the location of insects and obstacles.

This process is called echolocation.

Bandwidth
Bandwidth is the maximum amount of data that can be transmitted over a network connection in a given amount of time. It is usually measured in bits per second (bps), Kbps, Mbps, or Gbps. It represents the data-carrying capacity of a communication channel, not the actual speed of data transfer

Higher bandwidth allows more data to pass every second, but generally costs more.

Importance of Bandwidth

Bandwidth is important because it:

Determines how quickly web pages load.

Improves streaming quality.

Allows faster downloads and uploads.

Supports multiple users simultaneously.

Reduces network congestion.

Improves overall network performance.

Uses (Applications) of Bandwidth

Bandwidth is used for:

Increasing network capacity.

Internet Service Providers (ISPs) managing network traffic.

Bandwidth throttling to reduce congestion.

Connecting multiple devices simultaneously.

Communication systems and signal transmission.

Audio and video streaming.

Measuring Bandwidth
Bandwidth is commonly measured in:

Mbps (Megabits per second)

Gbps (Gigabits per second)

Two types:

Symmetrical Bandwidth

Upload speed = Download speed

Used in business networks

Asymmetrical Bandwidth

Download speed > Upload speed

Common in home broadband

Factors Affecting Bandwidth

Bandwidth performance is affected by

Network congestion

Packet loss

Delay (Latency)

Jitter

Bottleneck links

Ways to Increase Bandwidth

Upgrade Internet plan.

Use a better router.

Use Ethernet cable instead of Wi-Fi.

Upgrade networking hardware.

6. Network Address Translation (NAT)
Network Address Translation (NAT) is a technique that allows instances in private subnets to access the internet without being directly accessible from the internet.

In AWS, this is done using a NAT Gateway.

Why is NAT Needed?

Private subnet instances:

Cannot receive public internet traffic.

Cannot directly connect to the internet.

May still need internet access to:

Download software updates

Install packages

Working of NAT Gateway

Imagine an EC2 instance inside a private subnet.

Step 1

The EC2 instance sends a request to the internet.

↓

Step 2

The request reaches the NAT Gateway.

↓

Step 3

The NAT Gateway replaces the private IP with its public IP.

↓

Step 4

The request goes to the internet.

↓

Step 5

The internet sends the response back.

↓

Step 6

The NAT Gateway forwards the response to the private EC2 instance.

✔ Outbound traffic is allowed.

✖ Unsolicited inbound traffic is blocked.

Characteristics

Located in a Public Subnet

Used by Private Subnets

Requires an Internet Gateway

Uses

Download updates

Install software

Access external APIs

Definition

A Security Group is a stateful virtual firewall attached to AWS resources such as EC2 instances.

It controls:

Incoming traffic (Inbound)

Outgoing traffic (Outbound)

8. Classless Inter-Domain Routing (CIDR)
CIDR is a notation used to define the range of IP addresses assigned to a network or subnet.

Link Aggregation Control Protocol (LACP)
LACP (Link Aggregation Control Protocol) is an IEEE standard protocol used to combine multiple physical Ethernet links into a single logical link

This increases:

Bandwidth

Reliability

Redundancy

Why is LACP Needed?

Suppose one cable provides 1 Gbps.

Instead of buying one expensive 4 Gbps cable, you can combine:

1 Gbps

1 Gbps

1 Gbps

1 Gbps

↓

One Logical Link (4 Gbps)

Advantages:

Higher speed

Backup links

Better traffic distribution

Features of LACP

Automatically negotiates link aggregation.

Supports up to 16 Ethernet ports.

Maximum 8 active links.

Remaining links stay in standby.

LACP Modes
1. Active Mode

Actively sends LACP Data Units (LACPDUs).

Initiates link aggregation.

2. Passive Mode

Waits for another device.

Responds to received LACPDUs.

LACP in OSI Model

Layer 1 (Physical Layer)

Combines multiple physical cables.

Layer 2 (Data Link Layer)

Creates one logical Ethernet connection.

Handles MAC addresses.

Manages Ethernet frames.

Layer 3 (Network Layer)

Uses hashing based on:

Source IP

Destination IP

Timers

Fast Mode

Every 1 second

Slow Mode (Default)

Every 30 seconds

Load Balancing

Traffic can be distributed using:

Per packet

Per flow

Per socket

Failover

If one cable fails:

Traffic automatically moves to the remaining links.

No network downtime.

Applications of LACP

Enterprise LANs

Data Centers

Server connections

Storage networks

Advantages

Higher bandwidth

Redundancy

Automatic failover

Load balancing

Limitations

Requires compatible devices

Complex configuration

Limited scalability

Error Control in Data Link Layer
Error Control is a mechanism used by the Data Link Layer to ensure that transmitted frames are received correctly.

It performs:

Error Detection

Error Correction

Reliable Delivery

Two Parts of Error Control

1. Error Detection

Detects whether an error has occurred.

Examples:

CRC

Checksum

Parity

2. Error Correction

Corrects the detected errors.

Usually done by:

Retransmission

Forward Error Correction

Automatic Repeat Request (ARQ)
ARQ is the main error-control mechanism.

Working:

Sender sends frame

↓

Receiver checks it

↓

If correct → ACK

↓

If incorrect → Retransmit

Types of ARQ

Stop-and-Wait ARQ

Working:

Send one frame

↓

Wait for ACK

↓

Receive ACK

↓

Send next frame

If ACK is lost:

Retransmit.

Advantages

Simple

Reliable

Disadvantages

Slow

Poor bandwidth utilization

Sliding Window ARQ

Allows multiple frames before waiting for ACK.

Much faster than Stop-and-Wait.

Two types:

Go-Back-N

Selective Repeat

Go-Back-N ARQ

Suppose sender sends:

1

2

3

4

5

Frame 3 is lost.

Receiver accepts:

1

2

Rejects:

3

4

5

Sender retransmits:

3

4

5

Advantage

Simple

Disadvantage

Retransmits many correct frames.

Selective Repeat ARQ

Again:

1

2

3

4

5

Frame 3 is lost.

Receiver stores:

4

5

Only frame

3

is retransmitted.

Advantage

Efficient

Disadvantage

Needs more memory.

Error Detection
Definition

Error Detection is the process of determining whether transmitted data has been corrupted during communication.

Types of Errors

Single-Bit Error

Only one bit changes.

Example

101101

↓

101001

Burst Error

Several consecutive bits change.

Example

1011010110

↓

1000111110

Error Detection Methods

1. Simple Parity Check

Adds one parity bit.

Even Parity

Total number of 1's becomes even.

Odd Parity

Total number of 1's becomes odd.

Advantages

Easy

Detects single-bit errors

Disadvantages

Cannot detect even-numbered errors

3. Checksum

Working

Sender:

Divide data

Add segments

Take 1's complement

Send checksum

Receiver:

Add all segments

Result = all 1's

No error.

Otherwise:

Error detected.

4. Cyclic Redundancy Check (CRC)

Most reliable detection method.

Working:

Step 1

Append zeros.

↓

Step 2

Perform Modulo-2 division.

↓

Step 3

Remainder = CRC.

↓

Step 4

Append CRC to data.

Receiver performs the same division.

Remainder = 0 → No error

Non-zero remainder → Error

Error Correction
Error Correction is the process of detecting and fixing errors so the receiver obtains the correct data.

Types of Error Correction

1. Forward Error Correction (FEC)

Sender adds extra bits.

Receiver:

Detects

Corrects

No retransmission needed.

Used in:

Satellite communication

2. Backward Error Correction

Receiver detects error.

↓

Requests retransmission.

↓

Sender sends again.

Uses:

Stop-and-Wait

Go-Back-N

Selective Repeat

Applications of Error Correction

Used in:

Wi-Fi

4G/5G Networks

Satellite Communication

Deep Space Communication

HDDs

SSDs

Flow Control
Flow Control is a Data Link Layer mechanism that controls how fast the sender sends data so that the receiver is not overloaded.

Why is Flow Control Needed?

Without flow control:

Receiver buffer becomes full.

Packets are dropped.

Data is lost.

Network becomes inefficient.

With flow control:

No buffer overflow

Reliable communication

Better throughput

Stable network

Flow Control Example

Suppose

Sender speed = 100 Mbps

Receiver can process = 20 Mbps

Without flow control:

Sender --->>>>>>>>>>>>> Receiver

Receiver buffer fills

↓

Packets dropped

With flow control:

Sender ---> Receiver

Receiver processes

Sender sends next

Everything works smoothly.

Types of Flow Control
There are 2 approaches

1. Feedback-Based Flow Control

Receiver tells sender how much data it can accept.

Example:

Sender ---> Frame 1

Receiver ---> ACK

Sender ---> Frame 2

The sender adjusts its speed according to receiver feedback.

Examples:

Stop-and-Wait

Sliding Window

TCP

2. Rate-Based Flow Control

The sender sends data at a fixed rate.

Receiver does not constantly send feedback.

Used in

Video streaming

Voice calls

Examples

Leaky Bucket

Stop-and-Wait Flow Control
This is the simplest flow control technique.

Working

Step 1

Sender sends one frame.

Step 2

Receiver receives it.

Step 3

Receiver sends ACK.

Step 4

Sender sends next frame.

Only one frame is in transit.

Advantages

Very simple

Reliable

Disadvantages

Very slow.

Sender wastes time waiting for ACK.

Sliding Window Flow Control
Instead of sending one frame,

Sender sends multiple frames before waiting.

Much faster than Stop-and-Wait.

Advantages

Better bandwidth utilization

Higher throughput

Disadvantages

More memory

More complex

Stop-and-Wait ARQ
ARQ stands for

Automatic Repeat request

Working

Sender

Frame 0

↓

Receiver

Received

↓

Receiver sends

ACK 1

↓

Sender sends

Frame 1

Problems in Stop-and-Wait

1. Lost Data

2. Lost ACK

Receiver receives frame.

ACK gets lost.

Sender waits.

Timeout occurs.

Frame sent again.

Receiver ignores duplicate using sequence number.

3. Delayed ACK

Go-Back-N ARQ (GBN)
This is an improved Sliding Window protocol.

Instead of one frame,

Sender sends multiple frames.

Receiver sends cumulative ACK.

Advantages

Faster than Stop-and-Wait

High throughput

Good for reliable links

Example

Sender sends

0

1

2

3

4

Suppose Frame 2 is lost.

Receiver receives

0 ✓

1 ✓

2 ✗

3 discarded

4 discarded

Receiver keeps asking for Frame 2.

Sender retransmits

2

3

4

Even though 3 and 4 were already sent.

Disadvantages

If one frame is lost,

Many correct frames are retransmitted.

Bandwidth is wasted.

4. Selective Repeat ARQ
Selective Repeat solves the main problem of GBN.

Instead of retransmitting many frames,

It retransmits only the lost one.

Example

Sender sends

0

1

2

3

4

Frame 2 is lost.

Receiver gets

0 ✓

1 ✓

2 ✗

3 ✓

4 ✓

Unlike GBN,

Receiver stores Frames 3 and 4.

Sender retransmits

Only Frame 2

Receiver now delivers all frames in order.

Disadvantages

More memory required

Complex implementation

Piggybacking
Piggybacking is a technique where the receiver attaches the ACK to its own outgoing data frame instead of sending a separate ACK.

Without Piggybacking

A ---> DATA ---> B

B ---> ACK ---> A

B ---> DATA ---> A

A ---> ACK ---> B

Four transmissions.

With Piggybacking

A ---> DATA ---> B

B ---> DATA + ACK ---> A

A ---> DATA + ACK ---> B

Only two transmissions.

Advantages

Fewer packets

Less overhead

Better bandwidth utilization

Disadvantages

If the receiver has no data to send, it cannot wait forever to attach the ACK.

After a short delay, it sends a separate ACK to avoid unnecessary retransmission

Network Layer
The Network Layer is the 3rd layer of the OSI Model.

Its main job is to:

Give every device a logical address (IP Address).

Find the best path to the destination.

Forward packets through routers.

Deliver packets across multiple networks.

Suppose:

You are in Delhi.

Your friend is in Mumbai.

The Data Link Layer can only send data within one local network.

The Network Layer is responsible for taking your packet from Delhi to Mumbai through many routers.

Laptop

│

Switch

│

Router

│

Internet

│

Router

│

Switch

│

Friend's Laptop

Responsibilities of the Network Layer
A) Logical Addressing

Every device gets an IP address.

Unlike MAC addresses (which identify hardware), IP addresses identify devices across networks.

B) Packetization

Transport Layer sends segments.

Network Layer adds an IP header.

Now it becomes a Packet.

C) Routing

Routing means

Finding the best path from source to destination.

D) Forwarding

Routing chooses the path.

Forwarding actually sends the packet through the correct interface.

Think of:

Routing = Deciding the road.

Forwarding = Driving on the road.

E) Host-to-Host Delivery

Network Layer ensures

Host A

↓

Host B

even if they are thousands of kilometers apart.

F) Fragmentation

Different networks allow different packet sizes.

Example

Wi-Fi supports

1500 Bytes

Another network supports

576 Bytes

The Network Layer breaks the packet into smaller pieces.

Original Packet

1500 Bytes

↓

576

576

348

At the destination they are reassembled.

H) NAT (Network Address Translation)

Private IP addresses cannot directly access the Internet.

Router converts

Private IP

192.168.1.10

↓

Public IP

49.207.x.x

This is called NAT.

How the Network Layer Works
Step 1

Application creates data.

↓

Step 2

Transport Layer creates segment.

↓

Step 3

Network Layer adds IP Header.

↓

Packet formed.

↓

Router checks destination IP.

↓

Chooses best path.

↓

Packet travels through routers.

↓

Destination receives packet.

Protocols Used

Most important protocol.

Advantages

7. Limitations

Routing vs Flooding

Routing                 Flooding

Uses routing table      No routing table

Less traffic            High traffic

No duplicate packets    Duplicate packets present

Less reliable           More reliable

Usually finds best path    Sends packets on all paths, so destination receives via the shortest available path
