IPv4 vs IPv6
Feature	IPv4	IPv6
Address Size	32 bits	128 bits
Notation	Decimal (192.168.1.1)	Hexadecimal (2001:db8::1)
Address Space	~4.3 billion	Vastly larger (2¹²⁸)
NAT	Commonly required	Generally not required
Security	IPsec optional	IPsec built into the design
Header	Variable (20–60 bytes)	Fixed (40 bytes)
Fragmentation	Sender and routers	Sender only
Broadcast	Supported	Not supported (uses multicast/anycast)
Address Classes	Yes	No (prefix-based addressing)

What is a Host?
A host is any device connected to a network.

Examples:
Computer
Laptop
Mobile phone
What is a Subnet?
A subnet is a smaller network created from a larger network.

For example:

Original Network

192.168.1.0/24

can be divided into

192.168.1.0/25
192.168.1.128/25

Now there are 2 smaller networks (subnets) instead of one large network.

Why do we do Subnetting?

Without subnetting

Company
│
├── HR
├── Sales
├── Accounts
├── IT

Everyone is in one network.

Problems

Too much broadcast traffic
Slow network
Wastage of IP addresses
Poor security

After subnetting

HR → Subnet 1

Sales → Subnet 2

Accounts → Subnet 3

IT → Subnet 4

Benefits

Better performance
Better security
Less broadcast traffic

Formula to Calculate Number of Hosts
The most important formula is
Hosts=2*h−2 
where
•	h = Host bits 
We subtract 2 because:
•	One IP is the Network Address 
•	One IP is the Broadcast Address 
These cannot be assigned to devices.

How to Find Host Bits
IPv4 has 32 bits.
Host bits
= 32 − Prefix
Example
/24
Network bits = 24
Host bits
32−24=8
Hosts
2^8−2
=256−2
=254
So a /24 network supports 254 devices.

Quick Host Table (Very Important)
CIDR	Subnet Mask	Host Bits	Usable Hosts
/24	255.255.255.0	8	254
/25	255.255.255.128	7	126
/26	255.255.255.192	6	62
/27	255.255.255.224	5	30
/28	255.255.255.240	4	14
/29	255.255.255.248	3	6
/30	255.255.255.252	2	2

Formula for Number of Subnets
If subnetting from a larger network,
Subnets=2s 
where
•	s = Borrowed bits 
________________________________________
Example
Original network
/24
New subnet
/26
Borrowed bits
26−24=2
Subnets
2²=4
Answer
4 subnets

Finding the Network Address
Formula
Network Address

=

IP Address AND Subnet Mask
Example
IP
192.168.1.10
Mask
255.255.255.0
Result
192.168.1.0
This is the Network Address.
________________________________________
9. Finding the Broadcast Address
The broadcast address is the last IP in the subnet.
Example
Network

192.168.1.0/24
Range
192.168.1.0

↓

192.168.1.255
Broadcast
192.168.1.255

192.168.1.0/24

First Host

192.168.1.254

What is VLSM?
VLSM (Variable Length Subnet Mask) allows using different subnet masks within the same network based on actual host requirements.
Instead of giving every department the same-sized subnet, VLSM assigns subnet sizes according to need, reducing IP address wastage
Example
Company Network
192.168.1.0/24
Departments
Department	Hosts Needed
Sales	120
Development	50
Accounts	26
Management	5
________________________________________
Using FLSM
Every department gets
/24
Each receives
254 hosts
Even Management needs only 5 hosts, so most addresses are wasted.
________________________________________
Using VLSM
Allocate the smallest subnet that fits each department:
Department	Needed	Allocated
Sales	120	/25 (126 hosts)
Development	50	/26 (62 hosts)
Accounts	26	/27 (30 hosts)
Management	5	/29 (6 hosts)
This saves many IP addresses.
13. Steps to Solve VLSM Problems
1.	Write the required number of hosts. 
2.	Arrange requirements from largest to smallest. 
3.	Choose the smallest subnet that can accommodate each requirement. 
4.	Allocate the largest subnet first.
14. Advantages of VLSM
•	Efficient use of IP addresses. 
•	Less IP wastage. 
•	Better scalability. 
•	Improved routing. 
•	Smaller broadcast domains. 
•	Commonly used in modern networks with routing protocols like OSPF, EIGRP, and BGP. 
________________________________________
15. Disadvantages of VLSM
•	More complex to design. 
•	Requires careful planning. 
•	Easier to make configuration mistakes. 
•	Older devices or protocols may not support it.
Supernetting
What is Supernetting?
Supernetting is the opposite of subnetting.
•	Subnetting: Divide one large network into many smaller networks. 
•	Supernetting: Combine many smaller networks into one larger network. 
It is also called Route Aggregation or CIDR Aggregation.
Why is Supernetting Needed?
Suppose a router has
Network A
Network B
Network C
Network D
Each requires one routing table entry.
Routing table
Network	Interface
200.1.0.0/24	A
200.1.1.0/24	B
200.1.2.0/24	C
200.1.3.0/24	D
Instead, we can write
200.1.0.0/22
Only one routing entry is needed.
This makes routing faster.
Advantages of Supernetting
•	Smaller routing tables 
•	Faster routing 
•	Fewer routing updates 
•	Better utilization of IP addresses 
•	Easier network management 
________________________________________
Conditions for Supernetting
There are three conditions.
Condition 1
Networks must be contiguous (continuous).
Correct
192.168.0.0
192.168.1.0
192.168.2.0
192.168.3.0
Wrong
192.168.0.0
192.168.2.0
192.168.5.0
There are gaps.
________________________________________
Condition 2
Every subnet should have the same size.
Example
All should be
/24
or
/25
Not mixed.
Condition 3
The first network should be divisible by the total block size.
Suppose
4 networks
Each network has
256 addresses
Total
4 × 256 =1024
The first network must begin exactly at the boundary of 1024 addresses.

Routers perform routing.

•	Works at Layer 3

•	Connects different networks 
•	Forwards packets

↓

Destination IP is added.
↓

Router checks routing table.
↓
Step 4
Router forwards packet.
↓
Step 5
Packet reaches destination.
________________________________________

Every router crossed
=
One hop
Example
PC

↓

Router1

↓

Router2

↓

Router3

↓

Server
Hop count = 3
Lower hop count generally means a shorter path.

There are three major types.

Distance Vector Routing
Routers send the entire routing table to neighbors periodically.
Algorithm
Bellman-Ford
Protocols
•	RIP 
Advantages

Disadvantages
Link State Routing
Routers share information only when there is a change.
Algorithm
Dijkstra
Protocols

•	IS-IS 
Advantages
Fast.
Uses less bandwidth.
3. Bellman-Ford Algorithm
Purpose
Find the shortest path from one source to every other node.
Unlike Dijkstra,
it works with negative edge weights.
________________________________________
Main Idea
Repeatedly improve the shortest distance by relaxing edges.
________________________________________
Relaxation
Suppose
A ----5---- B

Distance(B)=10
But
Distance(A)=2
New distance
2+5=7
Since
7<10

update
Distance(B)=7
This process is called Relaxation.
Why Repeat V−1 Times?
If graph has
V vertices
Longest simple path
V−1 edges
Therefore
Repeat
V−1
times.
________________________________________
Detecting Negative Cycle
After
V−1
iterations
perform one extra iteration.
If any distance still decreases
↓
Negative cycle exists.
Return
-1
Advantages
•	Works with negative weights 
•	Detects negative cycles 
________________________________________
Disadvantages
•	Slow 
•	Not suitable for huge graphs
4. Dijkstra Algorithm
Purpose
Find the shortest path.
Works only if
All edge weights are non-negative.
Main Idea
Always choose the nearest unvisited vertex.
Once chosen,
its distance is considered final.
________________________________________
Working
Suppose
A

/ \
4 8

B C
Initially
A=0

B=∞

C=∞
Visit A
Update
B=4

C=8
Next
Visit B
Update C
4+3=7

7<8
Now
C=7
Shortest path found.
________________________________________
Why Priority Queue?
Priority Queue always removes the vertex having the smallest distance.
This makes the algorithm very fast.
Why Doesn't Dijkstra Work for Negative Edges?
Suppose
A → B = 4

A → C = 5

C → B = -10
Dijkstra first finalizes
B = 4
Later
A→C→B

5 + (-10) = -5
Actual shortest distance is -5, but Dijkstra has already finalized 4 and will not update it. Hence, it gives the wrong answer.
Bellman-Ford does not make this assumption, so it correctly handles negative edges.  67

Static Routing
**Static Routing** is a routing method in which the network administrator manually enters the routes into the router's routing table.
It is called **Non-Adaptive Routing** because the routes **do not change automatically**.

### How it works

Suppose there are three routers:

```
PC1 ---- R1 ---- R2 ---- R3 ---- PC2
```

The administrator manually tells:

- R1 → To reach PC2, send packets to R2.
- R2 → Send packets to R3.
- R3 → Deliver to PC2.

Even if one cable breaks, routers **cannot find another path automatically**.

## Characteristics

- Manual configuration
- No routing protocols used
- No routing algorithms
- Routes remain fixed until changed manually

## Advantages

- Very secure because no routing updates are exchanged.
- No bandwidth is wasted exchanging routing information.
- Low CPU and memory usage.
- Cheaper routers can be used.

## Disadvantages

- Difficult to manage large networks.
- Every new route must be configured manually.
- No automatic recovery if a link fails.

# Dynamic Routing

Dynamic Routing automatically learns and updates routes whenever the network changes.
It is called **Adaptive Routing** because it adapts to topology changes.
Routers communicate using **routing protocols**.

Example protocols:
- RIP
- OSPF
- EIGRP
- IS-IS

## Advantages

- Automatic updates
- Best path selection
- Supports large networks
- Automatic recovery after failures

## Disadvantages

- More CPU usage
- More memory usage
- Uses bandwidth for routing updates
- More complex configuration

# Unicast Communication

## Definition

**Unicast** means
> One sender → One receiver

```
Computer A  --------->  Computer B
```

Only one destination receives the data.

---

### Examples

- HTTP
- HTTPS
- FTP
- SSH
- TCP

---

# Types of Routing Protocols

There are three major routing protocols.

## 1. Distance Vector Routing

Each router only knows:
- Its neighbors
- Distance to destination

Examples:

- RIP

---

## 2. Link State Routing

Each router knows

**The complete network topology**

Example:

- OSPF
- IS-IS

---

## 3. Path Vector Routing

Each router stores the **entire path** to reach a destination.

Example:

- BGP

---

# 4. Link State Routing (LSR)

## Definition

Every router builds a complete map of the network.

Instead of only knowing neighbors, every router knows:

- All routers
- All links
- Cost of every link

Then it calculates the shortest path using **Dijkstra's Algorithm**.

## Working

### Step 1

Each router discovers its neighbors.

↓

### Step 2

Creates a **Link State Packet (LSP)**

Contains:

- Router ID
- Neighbor information
- Link cost

↓

### Step 3

Flooding

Every router sends its LSP to all routers.

Now every router has the same network map.

↓

### Step 4

Stores everything inside the

**Link State Database (LSDB)**

↓

### Step 5

Runs

**Shortest Path First (SPF)**

using

**Dijkstra Algorithm**

↓

### Step 6

Builds the routing table.

# Components of Link State Routing

### Link State Packet (LSP)

Contains

- Router identity
- Link cost
- Connected neighbors

---

### Link State Database (LSDB)

Database containing information from every router.

---

### Dijkstra Algorithm

Calculates the shortest path.

---

### Routing Table

Stores the best routes.

---

# Characteristics

- Large memory required
- More CPU usage
- Fast convergence
- Very reliable
- Authentication supported

# Link State Protocols

## OSPF

Open Shortest Path First

Most popular link-state protocol.

---

## IS-IS

Intermediate System to Intermediate System

Mostly used by Internet Service Providers.

---

# 5. OSPF (Open Shortest Path First)

## Definition

OSPF is a **Link-State Interior Gateway Protocol (IGP)** used within a single Autonomous System (AS).

It uses **Dijkstra's Shortest Path First (SPF)** algorithm to find the best route.

---

## Features

- Link-state protocol
- Protocol number **89**
- Administrative Distance = **110**
- Classless (supports VLSM and CIDR)
- Fast convergence
- Triggered updates (updates only when changes occur)

### Router Types

Internal Router → One area

ABR → Connects multiple areas

ASBR → Connects OSPF to another routing protocol

DR → Reduces unnecessary communication on shared networks

BDR → Backup of DR

## OSPF Message Types

1. Hello → Discover neighbors
2. DBD → Exchange database summary
3. LSR → Request missing information
4. LSU → Send complete routing information
5. LSAck → Acknowledge receipt

## OSPF Network Types

OSPF Network Types
Point-to-Point: Direct link between two routers.
Broadcast Multi-Access: (e.g., Ethernet) DR/BDR election required.
Point-to-Multipoint: Treats each connection as point-to-point.
NBMA (Non-Broadcast Multi-Access): (e.g., Frame Relay) Manual neighbor configuration needed..

## OSPF Timers

- Hello Timer: Interval between Hello packets (default: 10 sec).
- Dead Timer: Time to declare a neighbor down if Hellos are missing (default: 40 sec).

## Advantages

- Fast
- Scalable
- Loop-free
- Supports IPv4 & IPv6
- Supports load balancing

## Disadvantages

- Complex configuration
- High CPU usage

# TCP (Transmission Control Protocol)

## Definition

TCP is a **connection-oriented**, **reliable** transport-layer protocol.

It ensures:

- No data loss
- Correct order
- Error checking
- Flow control

## TCP Features

- Reliable
- Connection-oriented
- Ordered delivery
- Error detection
- Retransmission

# Three-Way Handshake (Connection Establishment)

Before sending data:

```
Client              Server

SYN  ------------>

      <----------- SYN + ACK

ACK  ------------>
```

Connection established.

---

 Four-Way Handshake (Connection Termination)

```
FIN -------->

     <------- ACK

     <------- FIN

ACK -------->
```

Connection closed.

---

# TCP Working

1. Segmenting → Breaks data into segments.
2. Routing via IP → IP delivers the segments.
3. Reassembly → Receiver arranges them in order.
4. ACK → Receiver acknowledges received data.
5. Retransmission → Lost segments are resent.
6. Flow & Error Control → Prevents overload and detects corruption.

## Applications

- HTTP/HTTPS
- Email (SMTP, IMAP, POP3)
- FTP/SFTP

# HTTP (HyperText Transfer Protocol)

## Definition

HTTP is an **application-layer protocol** that allows communication between a web browser and a web server.

---

## Working

```
Browser

↓

DNS Lookup

↓

HTTP Request

↓

Server

↓

HTTP Response

↓

Browser displays webpage
```

---

# HTTP Request Contains

- URL
- Method (GET, POST, PUT, DELETE)
- Headers

# HTTP Versions

## HTTP/1.1

- Persistent connections
- Better than HTTP/1.0

---

## HTTP/2

- Multiplexing
- Header Compression
- Server Push

---

## HTTP/3

Uses **QUIC** instead of TCP.

Advantages:

- Faster connection setup
- Better performance on unstable networks
- Improved security

# Cookies

Cookies are **small pieces of data** stored in the browser by websites.

Uses:

- Login sessions
- User preferences
- Shopping carts

---

# Advantages

- Simple
- Low memory usage
- Widely supported
- Fast with persistent connections

---

# Disadvantages

- No encryption (HTTP). Use **HTTPS** for security.
- Less secure
- Not ideal for sensitive data

## What is HTTP Status Code?

****HTTP Status codes**** are three-digit numbers that servers use to tell your browser what happened with the request you sent. There are different types of status codes:

1. [Informational](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/computer-networks/http-status-codes-informational-responses/) ****(1xx)****: These codes just give you information (e.g., 100 Continue means the request is still being processed).
2. [Successful](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/computer-networks/http-status-codes-successful-responses/)****(2xx)****: These codes tell you everything went fine (e.g., 200 OK means the request was successful).
3. [Redirection](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/computer-networks/http-status-codes-redirection-responses/)****(3xx)****: These codes tell the browser to take additional action (e.g., 301 Moved Permanently means the requested page has moved to a new address).
4. [Client Error](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/html/http-status-codes-client-error-responses/) ****(4xx)****: These codes indicate that there was a problem with your request (e.g., 404 Not Found means the page doesn’t exist).
5. [Server Error](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/computer-networks/http-status-codes-server-error-responses/) ****(5xx)****: These codes tell you that something went wrong on the server side (e.g., 500 Internal Server Error means the server had an issue processing the request).

# DoS (Denial of Service)

## Definition

A **DoS attack** is a cyberattack in which **one computer** overwhelms a server or network with excessive requests, making it unavailable to legitimate users.

## Goal

Prevent legitimate users from accessing the service.

---

## Common Types

- Ping of Death
- SYN Flood
- Buffer Overflow

## Effects

- Website unavailable
- Slow network
- Connection failures

## Prevention

- Firewalls
- IDS/IPS
- CDN
- Rate limiting

# DDoS (Distributed Denial of Service)

## Definition

A **DDoS attack** is similar to a DoS attack, but it is launched from **many compromised devices (a botnet)** instead of a single machine.
Because requests come from many locations, DDoS attacks are much harder to stop and trace.

## Types of DDoS Attacks

- **Volumetric attacks:** Flood bandwidth with massive traffic.
- **Protocol attacks:** Exploit transport/network protocols (e.g., SYN flood).
- **Application-layer attacks (Layer 7):** Overwhelm web applications with HTTP requests.
- **Fragmentation attacks:** Send malformed fragmented packets that cannot be reassembled.

## Characteristics

- Manual configuration
- No routing protocols used
- No routing algorithms
- Routes remain fixed until changed manually

## Advantages

- Very secure because no routing updates are exchanged.
- No bandwidth is wasted exchanging routing information.
- Low CPU and memory usage.
- Cheaper routers can be used.

## Disadvantages

- Difficult to manage large networks.
- Every new route must be configured manually.
- No automatic recovery if a link fails.

# Dynamic Routing

Dynamic Routing automatically learns and updates routes whenever the network changes.
It is called **Adaptive Routing** because it adapts to topology changes.
Routers communicate using **routing protocols**.

Example protocols:
- RIP
- OSPF
- EIGRP
- IS-IS

## Advantages

- Automatic updates
- Best path selection
- Supports large networks
- Automatic recovery after failures

## Disadvantages

- More CPU usage
- More memory usage
- Uses bandwidth for routing updates
- More complex configuration

# Unicast Communication

## Definition

**Unicast** means
> One sender → One receiver

```
Computer A  --------->  Computer B
```

Only one destination receives the data.

---

### Examples

- HTTP
- HTTPS
- FTP
- SSH
- TCP

---

# Types of Routing Protocols

There are three major routing protocols.

## 1. Distance Vector Routing

Each router only knows:
- Its neighbors
- Distance to destination

Examples:

- RIP

---

## 2. Link State Routing

Each router knows

**The complete network topology**

Example:

- OSPF
- IS-IS

---

## 3. Path Vector Routing

Each router stores the **entire path** to reach a destination.

Example:

- BGP

---

Each router discovers its neighbors.

Contains:

- Router ID
- Neighbor information
- Link cost

↓

### Step 3

Flooding

Every router sends its LSP to all routers.

Now every router has the same network map.

↓

### Step 4

Stores everything inside the

**Link State Database (LSDB)**

↓

### Step 5

Runs

**Shortest Path First (SPF)**

using

**Dijkstra Algorithm**

↓

### Step 6

Builds the routing table.

# Components of Link State Routing

### Link State Packet (LSP)

Contains

- Router identity
- Link cost
- Connected neighbors

---

### Link State Database (LSDB)

Database containing information from every router.

---

### Dijkstra Algorithm

Calculates the shortest path.

---

### Routing Table

Stores the best routes.

---

# Characteristics

- Large memory required
- More CPU usage
- Fast convergence
- Very reliable
- Authentication supported

Intermediate System to Intermediate System

Mostly used by Internet Service Providers.

# TCP (Transmission Control Protocol)

## Definition

TCP is a **connection-oriented**, **reliable** transport-layer protocol.

It ensures:

- No data loss
- Correct order
- Error checking
- Flow control

## TCP Features

- Reliable
- Connection-oriented
- Ordered delivery
- Error detection
- Retransmission

# Three-Way Handshake (Connection Establishment)

Before sending data:

```
Client              Server

SYN  ------------>

      <----------- SYN + ACK

ACK  ------------>
```

Connection established.

---

# Four-Way Handshake (Connection Termination)

```
FIN -------->

     <------- ACK

     <------- FIN

ACK -------->
```

Connection closed.

---

# TCP Working

1. Segmenting → Breaks data into segments.
2. Routing via IP → IP delivers the segments.
3. Reassembly → Receiver arranges them in order.
4. ACK → Receiver acknowledges received data.
5. Retransmission → Lost segments are resent.
6. Flow & Error Control → Prevents overload and detects corruption.

## Applications

- HTTP/HTTPS
- Email (SMTP, IMAP, POP3)
- FTP/SFTP

1. [Informational](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/computer-networks/http-status-codes-informational-responses/) ****(1xx)****: These codes just give you information (e.g., 100 Continue means the request is still being processed).

3. [Redirection](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/computer-networks/http-status-codes-redirection-responses/)****(3xx)****: These codes tell the browser to take additional action (e.g., 301 Moved Permanently means the requested page has moved to a new address).
4. [Client Error](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/html/http-status-codes-client-error-responses/) ****(4xx)****: These codes indicate that there was a problem with your request (e.g., 404 Not Found means the page doesn’t exist).
5. [Server Error](https://www.geeksforgeeks.org/computer-networks/http-status-codes-server-error-responses/) ****(5xx)****: These codes tell you that something went wrong on the server side (e.g., 500 Internal Server Error means the server had an issue processing the request).

# Why is Firewall Needed?

Suppose your company has:

- Employee data
- Customer information
- Bank records

If there is no firewall:

```
Internet
      |
 Hackers
      |
 Company Computers
```

Hackers can directly attack the computers.

With a firewall:

```
Internet
      |
 Hackers
      |
  Firewall
      |
 Company Computers
```

The firewall checks every request before allowing it.

---

# Firewall Actions

A firewall can take three actions.

## 1. Accept

Packet is allowed.

```
Packet ---> Firewall ---> Allowed
```

Example:

Employee opening Google.

---

## 2. Reject

Packet is blocked.

The firewall also informs the sender.

```
Packet ---> Firewall ---> Rejected
```

The sender knows access was denied.

---

## 3. Drop

Packet is silently discarded.

```
Packet ---> Firewall ---> X
```

No reply is sent.

Hackers cannot even know whether the system exists.

This is considered more secure.

---

# Importance of Firewall

### 1. Prevent Unauthorized Access

Only authorized users can enter the network.

Example:

Only company employees can access internal servers.

---

### 2. Block Malware

Firewall blocks

- viruses
- worms
- trojans
- phishing traffic

before they reach the computer.

---

### 3. Protect Sensitive Data

Prevents

- customer information
- passwords
- banking details

from being stolen.

---

### 4. Control Internet Usage

Organizations can block

- Facebook
- YouTube
- Gaming websites

during office hours.


### 5. Detect Insider Threats

Even employees inside the company can misuse data.

Firewall can detect

- unusual uploads
- suspicious downloads
- unauthorized connections

---

# Working of Firewall

### Step 1

Every packet enters the firewall.

```
Internet

↓

Firewall

↓

Computer
```

---

### Step 2

Firewall checks rules.

Example rules:

Allow:

- HTTP
- HTTPS
- DNS

Block:

- Telnet
- Unknown ports

---

### Step 3

Decision

If safe

↓

Allow

If dangerous

↓

Reject or Drop

---

### Step 4

Log Generation

Firewall stores logs like

```
Time

Source IP

Destination IP

Port

Allowed/Blocked
```

Network administrators use these logs to investigate attacks.

---

### Step 5

Default Policy
Sometimes no rule matches.
Firewall then uses a default action.
Possible default actions:
Accept
Reject
Drop
Best practice:
**Drop** or **Reject**
Never Accept by default.

# Types of Firewalls

## 1. Packet Filtering Firewall

Checks only

- Source IP
- Destination IP
- Port Number

## 2. Stateful Inspection Firewall

Keeps track of existing connections.

Example:

If you requested a webpage,

its reply is automatically allowed.

Much more secure.

---

## 3. Proxy Firewall

Acts as an intermediary.

Instead of

```
You → Website
```

communication becomes

```
You

↓

Proxy Firewall

↓

Website
```

The website never directly communicates with your computer.

---

## 4. Circuit-Level Gateway

Checks whether TCP connections are genuine.

Doesn't inspect actual data.

---

## 5. Web Application Firewall (WAF)

Protects websites.

Blocks attacks like

- SQL Injection
- Cross Site Scripting (XSS)
- HTTP Flood

---

## 6. Next Generation Firewall (NGFW)

Most advanced firewall.

Includes

- Antivirus
- Intrusion Prevention
- Deep Packet Inspection
- Malware Detection
- Application Control

---

# Firewall Types by Installation

## Network Firewall

Protects the entire network.

```
Internet

↓

Firewall

↓

All Computers
```

---

## Host-Based Firewall

Installed on a single computer.

Example:

Windows Defender Firewall.

# How DDoS Works

Step 1

Attacker infects thousands of computers.

↓

Step 2

These become bots.

↓

Step 3

Attacker sends command.

↓

All bots send requests together.

↓

Target server becomes overloaded.

↓

Legitimate users cannot access it.

# Botnet

Those infected computers together are called a **Botnet**.

# Common DDoS Attacks

## SYN Flood

Abuses TCP handshake.

Attacker sends

```
SYN

SYN

SYN

SYN
```

but never completes the handshake.

The server waits for replies until its resources are exhausted.

---

## HTTP Flood

Thousands or millions of HTTP GET/POST requests are sent to a web server.

Because they look like legitimate requests, they are difficult to distinguish from real users.

---

## DNS Amplification

The attacker sends a small DNS query with the victim's spoofed IP address.

The DNS server replies with a much larger response to the victim.

A tiny request generates a huge amount of traffic toward the target.

---

# DDoS Mitigation

- Firewalls
- Web Application Firewall (WAF)
- CDN
- Rate Limiting
- AI Detection
- Traffic Monitoring

# TCP Three-Way Handshake

## Why do we need it?

TCP is reliable.

Before data transfer,

both computers must agree that they are ready.

This is called the Three-Way Handshake.

## Step 1

Client sends

```
SYN
Sequence =1000
```

Meaning:

"I want to communicate."

---

## Step 2

Server replies

```
SYN + ACK

Seq =5000

Ack =1001
```

Meaning

"I received your request.

Here is my sequence number."

---

## Step 3

Client replies

```
ACK

Seq =1001

Ack =5001
```

Meaning

"I received your sequence number."

Connection established.

# HTTP Flood Attack

## Definition

An HTTP Flood Attack is a Layer 7 DDoS attack where attackers send huge numbers of valid-looking HTTP GET or POST requests to exhaust a web server's resources.

The requests appear legitimate, making them difficult to detect.

---

# How it Works

Thousands of bots repeatedly send requests such as:

```
GET /index.html

GET /login

GET /images/logo.png
```

The server processes each request until CPU, memory, or application resources are exhausted.

Legitimate users then experience slow responses or complete downtime.

---

# Types

## HTTP GET Flood

Repeated requests for pages, images, videos, PDFs, etc.

**Impact:** High bandwidth usage and server overload.

---

## HTTP POST Flood

Repeated submission of forms or API requests.

**Impact:** High CPU and database usage because each POST often triggers processing and database operations.

---

## Slowloris

The attacker opens many HTTP connections but sends requests very slowly, never completing them.

The server keeps waiting, tying up connection slots.

---

## Recursive HTTP Flood

The attacker crawls many URLs (e.g., `/home` → `/products` → `/contact`) to force continuous page generation and database work.

---

## Randomized URL Flood

The attacker changes URL parameters on every request, such as:

```
/index.php?id=1

/index.php?id=2

/index.php?id=3
```

This bypasses caching because every URL appears unique.

---

# Prevention

- Rate Limiting
- CAPTCHA
- Web Application Firewall (WAF)
- CDN
- Load Balancing

# Botnet

## Definition

A **Botnet** is a collection of malware-infected computers or devices that are secretly controlled by an attacker (called the **Botmaster**) through a **Command and Control (C&C)** system.

Each infected device is called a **Bot** or **Zombie**.

---

# How Botnets Work

### Step 1: Find Vulnerable Systems

The attacker looks for devices with:

- Outdated software
- Weak/default passwords
- Poor security settings
- Users who click unsafe links or download untrusted software

---

### Step 2: Malware Infection

The attacker infects devices using:

- Phishing emails
- Fake software updates
- Cracked software
- Malicious websites (drive-by downloads)

The malware installs silently.

---

### Step 3: Connect to the C&C Server

The infected device contacts the **Command and Control (C&C)** server.

It sends information such as:

- IP address
- Operating system
- Device details

Then it waits for commands.

---

### Step 4: Communication

Botnets commonly communicate using:

- **IRC** (older botnets)
- **HTTP/HTTPS** (to blend in with normal web traffic)
- **Peer-to-Peer (P2P)** (bots communicate directly without a central server)

---

### Step 5: Execute Commands

The botmaster instructs the bots to perform tasks such as:

- Sending spam emails
- Launching DDoS attacks
- Redirecting users to phishing websites
- Stealing data

---

### Step 6: Expansion

The botnet spreads by infecting more vulnerable devices.
Each new infected device becomes another bot, increasing the botnet's size and power.

## Types of Botnets

Botnets can be classified based on the communication channel used between the bots and the Command and Control (C&C) server.

## Types of Botnets

Botnets can be classified based on the communication channel used between the bots and the Command and Control (C&C) server.

# 1. IRC Botnet

This botnet uses Internet Relay Chat (IRC) servers as the Command and Control (C&C) channel through which the botmaster sends instructions to infected devices.

- Uses centralized communication structure.
- Bots connect to an IRC server to receive instructions.
- Commands are transmitted as normal chat messages.

### 2. Peer-to-Peer (P2P) Botnet

This operates using a decentralized network structure where each infected device communicates directly with other bots instead of relying on a central server.

- Does not depend on a central Command and Control server.
- Each bot acts as both client and server.
- Bots share commands with each other across the network.

### 3. HTTP/HTTPS Botnet

Bots periodically connect to specific URLs to receive instructions, making the traffic appear similar to normal web browsing activity.

- Uses web protocols for communication.
- Bots connect to web servers at regular intervals.
- Communication blends with normal internet traffic.

# Botnet Attacks

Botnets are commonly used for:

- **Phishing:** Sending fake emails or messages to steal credentials
- **DDoS Attacks:** Flooding websites with traffic
- **Spamming:** Sending large volumes of unwanted emails
- **Data Theft:** Stealing passwords, financial data, or confidential information

# Botnet Prevention

- Keep operating systems and software updated
- Use strong, unique passwords
- Enable Two-Factor Authentication (2FA)
- Avoid suspicious emails, links, and attachments
- Install trusted antivirus or endpoint security software

# Introduction to Phishing

## What is Phishing?

**Phishing** is a cyberattack in which an attacker **pretends to be a trusted person or organization** to trick people into revealing confidential information.
## Example

Suppose you receive this email:

> Dear Customer,
> 
> Your SBI account has been blocked.
> 
> Click here immediately to verify your account.

You click the link.

The website looks exactly like SBI.

You enter

- Username
- Password
- OTP

Instead of going to SBI,

the information goes directly to the hacker.

This is **Phishing**.

# What Information Do Attackers Steal?

They try to steal:

- Usernames
- Passwords
- Bank account details
- ATM PIN
- Credit card numbers
- Debit card numbers

# Methods Used to Carry Out Phishing

There are many ways hackers trick users.

---

# 1. Unknown Attachments

Example

You receive

```
Salary_Report.pdf.exe
```

It looks like a PDF.
Actually,
it's malware.
When opened,
it installs malicious software.
Possible results:

- Virus installation
- Keylogger
- Password theft
- Ransomware

---

# 2. Free Public Wi-Fi

Example

Airport Wi-Fi
Coffee shop Wi-Fi
Railway station Wi-Fi
These networks are often unsecured.
Attackers connected to the same Wi-Fi may perform:

- Packet Sniffing
- Man-in-the-Middle (MITM) attacks
- Session Hijacking

Never enter banking passwords or sensitive information on public Wi-Fi unless you're using a trusted VPN.

---

# 3. Social Media

Example
Someone sends you a friend request.
They chat with you for weeks.
Then they ask
Can you send me your OTP?
Since you trust them,
you may share it.
This is called **Social Engineering**.

Attackers also use **OSINT (Open Source Intelligence)** by collecting publicly available information from your profiles to make attacks more convincing.

---

# 4. Fake Links

Example
Real website

```
https://amazon.in
```

Fake website

```
https://amaz0n-login.com
```

Notice:
The letter **o** has been replaced with **0** (zero).
Many people don't notice.

# Types of Phishing

---

# 1. Email Phishing

Most common type.

Hackers send fake emails pretending to be:

- Bank
- Google
- Microsoft
- Amazon
- PayPal

Example

> Your Gmail storage is full.

> Click here.

The link leads to a fake login page.

---

# 2. Spear Phishing

Targeted phishing.
Instead of attacking everyone,
the hacker attacks one specific person.
Before sending the email
the hacker researches the victim.

Example
Employee:
Rahul Sharma

# 3. Whaling

Whaling targets **high-ranking executives**.

Examples

- CEO
- CFO
- Director
- Managing Director

These attacks usually involve:

- Money transfer
- Confidential documents
- Company secrets

Example

Fake email:

> Transfer ₹50 lakh immediately.

Appears to come from the CEO.

---

# 4. Smishing

SMS + Phishing = Smishing

Example

> Your package is waiting.

> Click here.

The link steals passwords.

---

# 5. Vishing

Voice + Phishing = Vishing

Example

Someone calls:

> Hello,

> I'm calling from SBI.

> Please tell me the OTP.

Banks **never** ask for OTP over the phone.

---

# 6. Clone Phishing

The hacker copies a genuine email.

Then changes:

- Link
- Attachment

Everything else remains the same.

# Preventing Phishing

---

## Download only from official sources

Never install software from unknown websites.

---

## Never share confidential information

Never share:

- OTP

## Check URLs

Always check

## Don't reply to suspicious emails

Instead,

contact the company using their official website or customer support.

---

## Use Anti-Phishing Software

Examples

- Malwarebytes
- Kaspersky
- Webroot
- Microsoft Defender SmartScreen

These tools warn you about known phishing sites.

---

## Keep Software Updated

Updates fix security vulnerabilities.

---

## Enable Firewall

Firewalls block many malicious connections before they reach your device.

# How to Identify a Fake Website

---

## 1. Check HTTPS

Secure website

```
https://
```

Not secure

```
http://
```

**Important:** HTTPS means the connection is encrypted, but **it does not guarantee the website itself is trustworthy**. Attackers can also obtain HTTPS certificates. So always verify the domain name as well.

---

## 2. Check Domain Name Carefully

Example

Real

```
facebook.com
```

Fake

```
faceb00k.com
```

## 3. Look at Website Design

Fake websites often have:

- Spelling mistakes
- Low-quality images

## 4. Anti-Phishing Tools

Examples

- PhishTank
- Kaspersky
- Malwarebytes
- Webroot

They maintain databases of phishing websites and block access.


# Two-Factor Authentication (2FA)
Normally,
login requires
Password
Only one layer of security.
If the hacker steals the password,
they can log in.
2FA adds another layer.

Example

Password

OTP

Now,

even if the hacker knows the password,

they still need the OTP.

---

# Why is 2FA Needed?

Without 2FA

```
Password

↓

Access Granted
```

With 2FA

```
Password

↓

OTP

↓

Access Granted
```

The attacker must compromise **both** factors

# Authentication Factors

Authentication verifies your identity using one or more factors.

---

## 1. Knowledge Factor

Something you know.

Examples

- Password
- PIN
- Security question

---

## 2. Possession Factor

Something you have.

Examples

- Phone
- OTP
- Security key
- Smart card

---

## 3. Biometric (Inherence) Factor

Something you are.

Examples

- Fingerprint
- Face ID
- Iris scan
- Voice recognition

---

## 4. Location Factor

Where you are.

Example

Only allow login from:

- Office network
- India
- Specific GPS location

---

## 5. Time Factor

When you log in.

Example

Employees can log in only between **9 AM and 6 PM**.

Outside that time, access is denied.

---

# How 2FA Works

### Step 1

Enter username and password.

↓

Server checks credentials.

---

### Step 2

If correct,

the server asks for a second factor.

Example

OTP.

---

### Step 3

The server generates a temporary code.

Example

```
483726
```

Usually valid for **30–60 seconds**.

---

### Step 4

The user enters the OTP or uses a biometric or security key.

---

### Step 5

The server verifies the second factor.

If it matches,

access is granted.

---

# Types of Second Factors

---

## 1. Possession-Based

Examples

- SMS OTP
- Email OTP
- Google Authenticator
- Microsoft Authenticator
- Authy
- Hardware OTP token

---

## 2. Biometric-Based

Examples

- Fingerprint
- Face ID
- Retina scan
- Iris scan

---

## 3. Physical Security Tokens

Examples

- YubiKey
- Smart Cards
- FIDO2 Security Keys

These are among the strongest forms of 2FA because they require a physical device.

---

# Is 2FA Completely Secure?

No.

It is **much** more secure than passwords alone, but no system is perfect.

Possible risks include:

- **SIM-swapping attacks** against SMS-based OTPs
- Theft of your phone or security key
- Sophisticated phishing sites that capture both your password and OTP in real time

For the highest security, security keys (like FIDO2/YubiKey) and passkeys are generally stronger than SMS OTPs.

# Malware

**Malware (Malicious Software)** = Any software intentionally created to **damage**, **steal data**, **spy**, or **gain unauthorized access** to a computer, mobile device, or network.

Think of malware as a **criminal hiding inside software**.

### Main Goals of Malware

- Steal passwords, bank details, and personal information.
- Damage or delete files.
- Slow down or crash computers.
- Spy on users.
### Why is malware dangerous?

- It keeps evolving.
- New versions appear regularly.
- Antivirus software cannot always detect new malware immediately.
- It is used in ransomware attacks, cyber espionage, and Advanced Persistent Threats (APTs).
# Types of Malware

## 1. Botnet

A **Botnet** is **not a single virus**.

It is a **network of infected computers (bots or zombies)** controlled by a hacker without the owners' knowledge.

### How it works

```
Attacker
    │
    ▼
Command & Control Server
    │
 ┌──┴─────┬───────┐
 ▼        ▼       ▼
Bot 1    Bot 2   Bot 3
(Infected Computers)
```

The hacker sends one command.

All infected computers perform it together.

### Uses

- DDoS attacks
- Sending spam emails
- Cryptocurrency mining
- Stealing information
### Example

**Mirai Botnet (2016)**

It infected IoT devices like CCTV cameras and routers to launch a massive DDoS attack.

---

## 2. Spyware

Spyware secretly watches everything you do.

It records

- Passwords
- Banking information
- Browsing history
- Emails
- Screenshots
- Keystrokes

without your permission.

### Example

**Pegasus**

One of the world's most advanced spyware programs.

It secretly monitored phones of journalists and government officials.

---

## 3. Adware

Adware displays unwanted advertisements.

Examples

- Random pop-up ads
- Browser ads
- Automatic redirects

Some adware also tracks browsing habits to show targeted advertisements.

---

## 4. Ransomware

Ransomware encrypts your files and asks for money (ransom).

### How it works

```
Computer
      │
      ▼
Files Encrypted
      │
      ▼
"Pay $500 to recover your files"
```

### Example

**WannaCry (2017)**

Affected over 200,000 computers worldwide.

Hospitals, businesses, and government offices were badly affected.

---

## 5. Trojan Horse

A Trojan pretends to be useful software.

Example

You download

```
Free Game
```

But it secretly installs malware.

Unlike worms, Trojans **do not spread by themselves.**

---

## 6. Worm

A worm spreads automatically.

No user action is needed.

### Example

```
Computer A
      │
      ▼
Computer B
      │
      ▼
Computer C
```

One infected computer infects many others through a network.

### Example

**Stuxnet**

It attacked industrial systems and damaged Iranian nuclear centrifuges.

---

# Signs Your Device is Infected

## 1. Slow Computer

Programs take longer to open.

Reason:  
Malware uses CPU and RAM.

---

## 2. Browser Redirects

You search Google.

Instead of opening Google,

it automatically opens another website.

---

## 3. Fake Virus Warnings

You may see messages like

```
Your computer has 567 viruses!

Click here to clean now!
```

These are usually fake.

---

## 4. Startup/Shutdown Problems

Symptoms

- Slow startup
- Automatic restart
- Windows doesn't boot properly

---

## 5. Too Many Pop-up Ads

Even when no browser is open,

ads continue appearing.

Usually caused by adware.

# Real World Malware Examples

## WannaCry

Type:  
Ransomware

Year:  
2017

Impact:  
Encrypted files worldwide.

---

## Stuxnet

Type:  
Worm

Target:  
Iran's nuclear facilities

Impact:  
Destroyed centrifuges.

---

## Emotet

Type:  
Trojan

Initially stole banking information.

Later spread ransomware and other malware.

---

## Pegasus

Type:  
Spyware

Used for surveillance of important people.

---

# Protection Against Malware

## 1. Avoid Phishing Emails

Never click unknown links.

Verify sender first.

---

## 2. Visit Trusted Websites

Avoid pirated software.

Download software only from official websites.

---

## 3. Update Software

Install security patches.

They fix known vulnerabilities.

---

## 4. Scan USB Drives

Unknown USB devices may contain malware.

Always scan before opening.

---

## 5. Install Antivirus

Examples

- Malwarebytes
- Bitdefender
- Windows Defender

---

## 6. Avoid Pop-ups

Never click

```
Congratulations!

You won an iPhone.
```

Simply close them.

---

## 7. Install Only Necessary Apps

Avoid downloading software from unknown sources.

---

# Steps to Remove Malware

### Step 1

Install Malwarebytes.

---

### Step 2

Update malware database.

---

### Step 3

Run Full Scan.

---

### Step 4

Review detected threats.

---

### Step 5

Move threats to Quarantine.

Quarantine means

```
Malware
   │
   ▼
Isolated

Cannot damage your computer.
```

---

### Step 6

Delete malware.

---

### Step 7

Restart computer.

---

### Step 8

Scan again.

If nothing is detected,

the system is probably clean.

---

| ----------------------- | ------------------------------------ |

| SUPERAntiSpyware        | Removes spyware and Trojans          |

# Advanced Persistent Threat (APT)
APT is **not just malware**.

It is a **long-term, carefully planned cyber attack** where attackers enter a network, stay hidden for months or even years, and continuously steal sensitive information without being detected.

# Why is it called APT?

## Advanced

Uses sophisticated techniques like:

- Zero-day exploits
- Social engineering
- Custom malware

---

## Persistent

The attacker stays hidden for a very long time.

---

## Threat

It is a serious cyber attack against valuable targets.

# Characteristics of APT

## 1. Advanced Techniques

Uses

- Spear phishing
- Zero-day exploits
- Custom malware

instead of simple viruses.

---

## 2. Persistence

The attacker remains inside the network for months or years.

---

## 3. Targeted

APT attacks focus on valuable organizations like:

- Banks
- Governments
- Defense
- Hospitals
- Large companies

They do **not** usually target random home users.

---

## 4. Data Exfiltration

The main objective is to quietly steal confidential information.

---

# Working of an APT

## Step 1. Gain Initial Access

Methods:

- Spear phishing emails
- Weak passwords
- Software vulnerabilities

---

## Step 2. Expand Access

The attacker moves through the network and infects more systems.

---

## Step 3. Gain Administrator Rights

They steal passwords or exploit vulnerabilities to obtain full control.

---

## Step 4. Move Freely

The attacker can access many computers inside the network without being noticed.

---

## Step 5. Harvest Data

Sensitive data is collected and secretly transferred to the attacker's systems.

# Detecting an APT

Signs include:

- Unusual file access
- Strange network traffic
- Unknown programs
- Login attempts from unusual locations
- Slow systems
# Protection Against APT

- Use strong, unique passwords.
- Enable Two-Factor Authentication (2FA).
- Keep software updated.
- Install antivirus software.
- Use firewalls.
- Back up important data regularly.
# Famous APT Attacks

|Attack|Target|Year|
|---|---|---|
|Stuxnet|Iran's nuclear facilities|2010|
|APT1|141+ companies|2006–2013|
|Operation Aurora|Google, Adobe|2009|
|DarkHotel|Business executives|2007–present|
|Operation Shady RAT|70+ organizations|2006–2011|
|APT28 (Fancy Bear)|Governments & military|2007–present|
|Operation Night Dragon|Energy companies|2009–2011|
|APT33|Aerospace & defense|2013–present|
# Malware vs APT (Important Exam Difference)

|Malware|APT|
|---|---|
|Malicious software|A long-term cyberattack campaign|
|May infect anyone|Targets specific organizations|
|Often detected quickly|Stays hidden for months or years|
|Can damage or steal data|Mainly steals valuable information over time|
|Examples: Worm, Trojan, Ransomware|Examples: APT1, APT28, DarkHotel|
# Botnet

## What is a Botnet?

A **Botnet** is a group of computers, phones, servers, or IoT devices that have been infected with malware and are secretly controlled by a hacker.

- Each infected device is called a **Bot** or **Zombie**.
- The hacker controlling them is called the **Bot Herder**.
- The hacker can make thousands or even millions of infected devices perform the same task simultaneously.
## How Does a Botnet Work?

### Step 1: Infection

The attacker infects devices using malware through:

- Phishing emails
- Fake software
- Malicious websites
- Fake updates
- Exploiting software vulnerabilities

Example:

You download a cracked game.

Hidden malware gets installed.

Your PC becomes infected.

---

### Step 2: Connect to Command & Control (C2) Server

After infection,

the malware secretly contacts the hacker's **Command and Control (C2) Server**.

Think of C2 as the headquarters.

```
Hacker
   |
   |
C2 Server
   |
----------------------------
|     |      |      |      |
PC1  PC2   Phone   Laptop IoT
```

Now your PC waits for commands.

---

### Step 3: Receive Commands

The hacker can tell every infected computer to:

- Attack websites
- Send spam emails
- Mine cryptocurrency
- Spread malware
- Steal data

All bots work together.

---

### Step 4: Self Propagation

Some botnets automatically search for other vulnerable devices.

Example

One infected computer scans nearby computers.

If another computer has weak security,

it infects that computer too.

Botnet keeps growing.

---

### Step 5: Staying Hidden

Botnets try to avoid detection by

- Hiding processes
- Changing filenames
- Encrypting communication
- Reinfecting computers after removal

This makes them difficult to remove.

---

# Uses of Botnets

## 1. DDoS Attack

This is the most common use.

Thousands of infected computers send requests to one server.

Example

100,000 bots visit one website simultaneously.

The server becomes overloaded.

The website crashes.

Example:

Mirai Botnet

---

## 2. Data Theft

Botnets steal

- Passwords
- Credit card details
- Login credentials
- Personal information

This data is sold on the dark web.

---

## 3. Sending Spam

Botnets send millions of spam emails.

Examples

- Fake lottery
- Fake Amazon order
- Fake bank email
- Phishing emails

---

## 4. Spreading Malware

One infected computer infects many others.

The malware spreads rapidly.

---

## 5. Cryptocurrency Mining

Instead of using their own computers,

hackers secretly use yours.

Your CPU mines cryptocurrency.

Result:

- Slow PC
- High electricity usage
- Overheating

---

## 6. Click Fraud

Botnets repeatedly click online advertisements.

Hackers earn advertising money.

Businesses lose money.

---

## 7. Cyber Espionage

Some governments and organizations use botnets to

- Spy
- Steal confidential documents
- Collect military information

---

# Evolution of Botnets

## 2000 — Mafiaboy Attack

A teenager launched massive DDoS attacks.

Targets included:

- Yahoo
- CNN
- eBay

This showed how dangerous botnets could become.

---

## 2016 — Mirai Botnet

One of the most famous botnets.

Instead of computers,

it infected IoT devices.

Examples

- CCTV cameras
- Smart TVs
- Routers
- Baby monitors

Millions of IoT devices attacked Dyn DNS.

Many websites became unavailable including:

- Netflix
- PayPal
- Amazon

---

## 2017 Google Cloud Attack

Attack size:

2.54 Tbps

One of the largest DDoS attacks ever.

---

## 2021 Azure Attack

Microsoft Azure experienced

3.47 Tbps DDoS attack.

Successfully defended.

---

# How Hackers Control Botnets

## 1. Centralized Model

```
          Hacker
             |
        C2 Server
       /   |   \
    Bot Bot Bot
```

Advantages

- Easy to manage

Disadvantages

- If C2 server is shut down,  
    whole botnet dies.

---

## 2. Peer-to-Peer (P2P)

```
Bot ---- Bot ---- Bot
 |         |        |
Bot ---- Bot ---- Bot
```

No central server.

Every bot communicates with others.

Harder to destroy.

More common today.

---

# Signs Your Computer is Part of a Botnet

- Computer becomes slow
- Internet usage increases
- High CPU usage
- Battery drains quickly
- Unknown programs appear
- Random pop-ups
- Strange emails sent from your account
- Frequent crashes
- Overheating

---

# Preventing Botnets

- Install antivirus
- Keep software updated
- Use strong passwords
- Enable firewall
- Don't download pirated software
- Don't click unknown links
- Enable MFA
- Monitor network traffic
# Spyware

## What is Spyware?

Spyware is malware that secretly monitors your activities and sends your personal information to an attacker without your permission.
It works silently.
Usually,
you don't even know it's installed.

# How Spyware Enters a Computer

### 1. Phishing

Fake email

Example

```
Your bank account has been blocked.

Click here.
```

You click.

Spyware installs.

---

### 2. Spoofing

Fake emails pretending to be

- Amazon
- PayPal
- Google
- Microsoft

---

### 3. Free Software

Many cracked software packages include spyware.

Example

Free Video Editor

↓

Installs spyware in the background.

# Types of Spyware

## Keylogger

Records every key you type.

Can steal

- Passwords
- ATM PINs
- Emails

---

## Adware

Shows unwanted advertisements.

Tracks browsing habits.

---

## Trojan

Pretends to be useful software.

Actually steals information.

---

## Tracking Cookies

Monitor browsing habits.

Usually used for advertising.

---

## System Monitor

Monitors everything

- Files
- Applications
- Browsing
- Chats

---

## Data Harvester

Collects personal information.

Example

- Phone number
- Credit card
- Email
## RAT (Remote Access Trojan)

Very dangerous.

Attacker gets complete remote control.

Can

- Watch screen
- Open camera
- Access files
- Delete files

---

# What Spyware Does

- Steals passwords
- Captures keystrokes
- Records browsing
- Takes screenshots
- Opens webcam
- Gives remote access
- Steals banking information

---

# How Spyware Attacks

Usually through

- Phishing emails
- Malicious downloads
- Drive-by downloads
- Software vulnerabilities
- Social engineering

---

# Problems Caused by Spyware

- Identity theft
- Financial loss
- Privacy violation
- Slow computer
- Browser changes
- Unauthorized access

---

# Signs of Spyware

- Slow PC
- Strange ads
- Pop-ups
- Browser homepage changes
- Unknown software installed
- High network usage

---

# Removing Spyware

1. Scan using antivirus.
2. Update Windows.
3. Remove suspicious software.
4. Clear browser cache.
5. Reset browser settings.

---

# Preventing Spyware

- Install antivirus
- Avoid pirated software
- Download only from trusted websites
- Keep Windows updated
- Don't click suspicious links
- Read installation prompts carefully
- Use strong browser security settings
# Ransomware

## What is Ransomware?

Ransomware is malware that encrypts your files and demands money (a ransom) to restore access.

Imagine someone locks all your documents in a safe and says:

> "Pay ₹50,000, and I'll give you the key."

That's exactly how ransomware works.

---

## How It Infects a Computer

Common infection methods include:

- Phishing emails with malicious attachments
- Fake software or updates
- Malicious pop-ups
- Drive-by downloads from compromised websites
- Exposed or weak Remote Desktop Protocol (RDP)

---

## How Ransomware Works

1. **Infection:** Malware enters the system.
2. **Execution:** It searches for important files.
3. **Encryption:** Files are encrypted with a secret key.
4. **Ransom Note:** A message demands payment, often in cryptocurrency.
5. **Possible Decryption:** Even if the ransom is paid, there is no guarantee the attacker will provide a working decryption key.

---

## Types of Ransomware

- **Crypto Ransomware:** Encrypts files.
- **Locker Ransomware:** Locks the entire device.
- **Scareware:** Displays fake infection warnings and demands payment.
- **Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS):** Criminals rent ransomware to other attackers.
- **Doxware (Extortionware):** Threatens to publish stolen data if payment is not made.

---

## Effects on Businesses

- Financial losses
- Operational downtime
- Permanent data loss
- Reputation damage
- Legal and regulatory penalties

---

## Famous Ransomware Attacks

- **AIDS Trojan (1989):** One of the first ransomware attacks.
- **CryptoLocker (2013):** Popularized Bitcoin ransom payments.
- **WannaCry (2017):** Infected over 230,000 computers in 150+ countries.
- **Petya / NotPetya (2016–2017):** Encrypted entire disks and caused massive global disruption.
- **LockBit (2020–2024):** One of the largest Ransomware-as-a-Service operations.

---

## Signs of a Ransomware Attack

- Files suddenly become inaccessible.
- File extensions change unexpectedly.
- The system becomes unusually slow.
- A ransom note appears.
- Antivirus or firewall alerts report suspicious activity.

---

## Prevention

- Keep regular offline or immutable backups.
- Update operating systems and applications promptly.
- Use reputable antivirus/anti-malware software.
- Enable Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA).
- Train users to recognize phishing attempts.
- Limit user privileges and segment networks.

---

# Trojan Horse

## What is a Trojan Horse?

A **Trojan Horse** is malware that disguises itself as a legitimate or useful program to trick users into installing it. Unlike viruses or worms, a Trojan **does not replicate itself**; it relies on user action to run.

The name comes from the ancient Greek story of the Trojan Horse, where soldiers hid inside a wooden horse to enter the city.

---

## How a Trojan Works

1. The attacker disguises malware as a useful file or application.
2. The user downloads and runs it.
3. The Trojan installs silently.
4. The attacker can then:
    - Steal data
    - Delete or modify files
    - Install additional malware
    - Gain remote access
    - Turn the computer into part of a botnet

---

## Common Infection Methods

- Malicious email attachments
- Fake software downloads
- Social engineering
- Malicious advertisements and pop-ups

---

## Types of Trojans

- **Backdoor Trojan:** Gives remote access.
- **Banker Trojan:** Steals banking credentials.
- **Downloader Trojan:** Downloads additional malware.
- **Dropper Trojan:** Installs other malware while avoiding detection.
- **GameThief Trojan:** Steals gaming accounts.
- **Ransom Trojan:** Encrypts files and demands payment.

---

## Famous Examples

- **Zeus (Zbot):** Stole banking credentials.
- **Tiny Banker:** Targeted online banking users.
- **Rakhni:** Installed ransomware or cryptocurrency miners.

---

## Prevention

- Download software only from trusted sources.
- Avoid suspicious email attachments.
- Keep antivirus software updated.
- Be cautious of pop-up advertisements.
- Keep your operating system and applications updated.

---

# Computer Worm

## What is a Computer Worm?

A **computer worm** is self-replicating malware that spreads automatically from one computer to another **without any user action**.

Unlike a Trojan, a worm does not need the user to execute it after the initial infection. Unlike a virus, it does not need to attach itself to another file.

## How a Worm Works

1. Finds a vulnerability (e.g., outdated software or open network ports).
2. Infects one computer.
3. Makes copies of itself.
4. Scans the network for other vulnerable systems.
5. Infects them automatically.
6. Repeats the process, spreading rapidly.

---

## Types of Worms

- **Email Worms:** Spread through infected email attachments (e.g., ILOVEYOU).
- **Network Worms:** Exploit network vulnerabilities (e.g., Conficker).
- **File-Sharing Worms:** Spread via shared folders or peer-to-peer networks.
- **Instant Messaging Worms:** Spread through messaging apps with malicious links.
- **Internet Worms:** Exploit vulnerable web servers (e.g., Code Red).

---

## Effects of Worms

- Consume bandwidth and system resources.
- Slow down networks.
- Damage files.
- Steal information.
- Install additional malware.
- Cause widespread outages.

---

## Prevention

- Keep operating systems and applications updated.
- Use strong passwords.
- Enable firewalls.
- Install reputable antivirus software.
- Be cautious with email attachments and links.
- Avoid suspicious websites and downloads.

# Antivirus

## What is Antivirus?

An **Antivirus** is a software program that protects your computer from viruses and other malicious software (malware).

Its main jobs are to:

- Detect viruses
- Prevent viruses from entering the computer
- Remove viruses
- Protect the computer continuously

Think of antivirus as a **security guard** for your computer.

# Why Do We Need Antivirus?

Without antivirus,

a virus can

- Delete files
- Steal passwords
- Encrypt documents (Ransomware)
- Slow down the computer
- Spy on you
- Control your PC remotely

Antivirus protects against all these threats.

# Types of Antivirus Software

There are different ways an antivirus protects your computer.

---

## 1. On-Demand Scan

This scan only happens when **you tell it to scan**.

### Example

You download a movie.

You think something looks suspicious.

You right-click

```
Scan with Windows Defender
```

The antivirus checks only that file.

This is an **On-Demand Scan**.

### Features

- Manual
- User starts scan
- Good for checking individual files

---

## 2. Real-Time Protection

This is the most important feature.

It works continuously in the background.

You don't need to start it.

Whenever

- USB is inserted
- File is downloaded
- Program is opened
- Website is visited

the antivirus immediately scans it.

### Example

You insert a pendrive.

Before Windows even opens it,

the antivirus scans it.

If malware is found,

it blocks it.

### Advantages

- Automatic
- Fast
- Stops infection before damage

---

## 3. Smart Scan

Instead of scanning the entire computer,

it scans only the places where malware is most likely to exist.

Example

- Downloads folder
- Desktop
- Running processes
- Startup programs

This makes scanning much faster.

---

## 4. Spyware Scanner

Specifically looks for spyware.

It checks whether software is secretly

- Tracking browsing
- Recording passwords
- Sending data to hackers

If found,

it removes it.

---

## 5. Machine Learning (ML) Antivirus

Traditional antivirus only recognizes known viruses.

Machine Learning Antivirus can recognize **new malware**.

It learns patterns from millions of malware samples.

### Example

Suppose a new virus has never been seen before.

Machine learning notices that the program

- Encrypts files
- Deletes backups
- Connects to suspicious servers

It predicts

"This looks dangerous."

and blocks it.

---

## 6. Database Antivirus

This is the traditional antivirus.

It compares files against a huge virus database.

Every known virus has a unique fingerprint called a **signature**.

If the signature matches,

the file is infected.

---

## 7. Malware Remover

Sometimes malware is already inside your PC.

This software specializes in removing existing malware instead of preventing infections.

Example

Malwarebytes

---

## 8. Email Antivirus

Scans emails.

Checks

- Attachments
- Links
- Scripts

before they reach your inbox.

Example

If someone sends

```
invoice.pdf.exe
```

the antivirus blocks it.

---

## 9. Heuristic Antivirus

Looks for suspicious code rather than exact virus signatures.

Example

Suppose a program suddenly tries to

- Delete system files
- Encrypt thousands of documents
- Disable Task Manager

Even though it's a new virus,

the antivirus recognizes these suspicious behaviors.

# How Antivirus Works

Let's understand the complete process.

---

## Step 1

A file enters your computer.

Example

```
game.exe
```

---

## Step 2

The antivirus immediately scans it.

It compares the file with millions of virus signatures.

```
File

↓

Virus Database

↓

Match?
```

---

## Step 3

If a match is found

```
Virus Detected
```

---

## Step 4

If no signature matches,

the antivirus performs heuristic analysis.

It checks

- File structure
- Program behavior
- Memory access
- Registry modifications

---

## Step 5

If the behavior is dangerous,

the antivirus blocks the program.

---

## Step 6

If malware is confirmed,

the antivirus

- Deletes it
- Quarantines it
- Repairs infected files

---

## Step 7

The antivirus updates its virus database regularly.

New viruses appear every day.

Without updates,

the antivirus cannot recognize new threats.

---

# Detection Techniques Used by Antivirus

## 1. Signature-Based Detection

Compares the file with known virus signatures.

### Advantages

- Very accurate
- Very fast

### Disadvantages

Cannot detect brand-new viruses.

---

## 2. Heuristic Detection

Looks for suspicious code patterns.

Example

A program tries to

- Delete Windows files
- Encrypt documents

Even though it's unknown,

the antivirus blocks it.

---

## 3. Behavior-Based Detection

Instead of looking at code,

it watches what the program actually does.

Example

A program suddenly

- Opens thousands of files
- Encrypts them
- Deletes backups

This resembles ransomware.

The antivirus stops it immediately.

---

## 4. Machine Learning Detection

Uses Artificial Intelligence.

Learns from millions of malware samples.

Can recognize new malware.

---

# What Happens After Detection?

There are three possibilities.

## 1. Delete

The infected file is permanently removed.

---

## 2. Quarantine

The file is isolated.

It cannot run.

It stays in a safe folder.

If it turns out to be a false alarm,

you can restore it.

---

## 3. Repair

Some antivirus software removes only the malicious code while keeping the original file.

---

# Scheduling Scans

Antivirus allows automatic scanning.

Example

```
Daily at 2 AM

Weekly

Monthly
```

No manual effort needed.

---

# Why Antivirus Needs Administrator Permission

The antivirus must scan

- Windows files
- Registry
- Memory
- Startup programs
- Running applications

Without administrator privileges,

it cannot inspect these protected areas.

---

# Other Ways to Protect Your Computer

Antivirus alone is not enough.

### Firewall

Blocks unauthorized network access.

### Avoid Suspicious Links

Never click unknown emails or websites.

### SIEM (Security Information and Event Management)

Used mostly in organizations.

It collects logs from many systems, detects suspicious activity, and alerts security teams.

---

# Advantages of Antivirus

## 1. Detects Malware

Finds viruses before they damage the system.

---

## 2. Removes Malware

Deletes infected files safely.

---

## 3. Real-Time Protection

Stops malware before it starts.

---

## 4. Blocks Dangerous Websites

Warns before opening phishing or malicious websites.

---

## 5. Automatic Updates

Downloads the latest virus definitions.

---

## 6. Protects Personal Data

Keeps passwords, banking details, and documents safe.

---

## 7. Performance Tools

Some antivirus programs also include

- Junk file cleanup
- Startup optimization
- Performance tuning

---

# Disadvantages of Antivirus

## 1. Uses System Resources

Consumes CPU, RAM, and disk activity.

Older computers may become slower.

---

## 2. False Positives

Sometimes safe software is mistakenly identified as malware.

---

## 3. Paid Features

Many advanced protections require a subscription.

---

## 4. Not 100% Effective

A brand-new (zero-day) attack may bypass traditional signature-based detection until updates are released.

# Anti-Malware

## What is Anti-Malware?

**Anti-Malware** is software designed to **detect, prevent, block, and remove all kinds of malware** from a computer.
Think of it as a **security team** that protects your entire computer from every type of cyber attack.
# What is Malware?

The word **Malware** comes from

```
Malicious + Software = Malware
```

Malware is any software intentionally created to harm a computer.

# Why Do We Need Anti-Malware?

Suppose you download a cracked game.

Hidden inside it are

- Trojan
- Spyware
- Ransomware
- Keylogger

Without anti-malware,

all of these could infect your PC.

With anti-malware,

the threats are detected and blocked before they can cause damage.

---

# What Does Anti-Malware Do?

Anti-malware performs four major tasks:

1. Detects malware
2. Blocks malware
3. Removes malware
4. Protects the system continuously

---

# Uses of Anti-Malware

## 1. Block Malware Infiltration

### Meaning

It prevents malware from entering your computer.

### How?

It scans every external source such as:

- USB drives
- Pen drives
- CDs/DVDs
- SD cards
- Downloaded files
- Email attachments

### Example

You insert a USB drive.

```
USB

↓

Anti-Malware Scan

↓

Virus Found

↓

USB Blocked
```

The malware never reaches your computer.

---

### It also scans Internet Downloads

Suppose you download

```
Free Photoshop Crack.exe
```

The anti-malware notices

- Trojan
- Spyware
- Keylogger

It blocks the download immediately.

---

### Email Protection

Suppose you receive

```
Invoice.pdf.exe
```

It looks like a PDF.

Actually,

it's malware.

The anti-malware blocks it before you open it.

---

# 2. Detect Ongoing Intrusions

Sometimes malware is already inside your computer.

Anti-malware continuously watches for suspicious activities.

This is called **Heuristic Monitoring**.

---

## Example 1

Suppose your PC is idle.

Suddenly

```
CPU Usage

2%

↓

95%
```

The antivirus notices something unusual.

It investigates.

A hidden crypto miner is found.

---

## Example 2

Thousands of files begin changing rapidly.

```
Photos

↓

Encrypted

↓

Documents

↓

Encrypted

↓

Videos

↓

Encrypted
```

The anti-malware recognizes this behavior as ransomware and immediately blocks the process.

---

## Memory Analysis

Some malware hides only in RAM (memory).

It never creates files on the hard disk.

Anti-malware scans memory to detect:

- Hidden processes
- Malicious scripts
- Injected code

---

## Network Monitoring (Beaconing Detection)

Many malware programs secretly communicate with hackers.

Example

```
Your Computer

↓

Unknown Server

↓

Sending Passwords
```

This secret communication is called **Beaconing**.

Anti-malware detects unusual network traffic and blocks it.

---

# 3. Prevent Remote Hacking

Hackers often try to access computers remotely.

Anti-malware prevents this in several ways.

---

## Intrusion Prevention System (IPS)

It watches incoming network traffic.

If someone tries to

- Guess passwords
- Exploit vulnerabilities
- Access restricted ports

the IPS blocks them.

Example

```
Hacker

↓

Port 3389 (Remote Desktop)

↓

Blocked
```

---

## Firewall Protection

A firewall acts like a gatekeeper.

```
Internet

↓

Firewall

↓

Computer
```

It checks every incoming and outgoing connection.

Safe traffic is allowed.

Suspicious traffic is blocked.

---

## VPN Protection

Public Wi-Fi is unsafe.

A VPN encrypts your internet traffic.

Example

Without VPN:

```
Laptop

↓

Public Wi-Fi

↓

Anyone Can Spy
```

With VPN:

```
Laptop

↓

Encrypted Tunnel

↓

Internet
```

Hackers cannot easily read your data.

---

## Encryption

Even if malware steals a file,

encryption makes it unreadable without the decryption key.

---

# 4. Clean Malware Infections

Suppose malware has already infected your PC.

The anti-malware removes it.

---

## Boot-Time Scan

Some malware loads before Windows starts.

Normal antivirus cannot remove it while Windows is running.

So,

the anti-malware scans **before Windows boots**.

```
Power ON

↓

Anti-Malware Scan

↓

Windows Starts
```

This allows hidden malware (such as rootkits) to be removed before it becomes active.

---

## Quarantine

Instead of deleting files immediately,

anti-malware often moves them to a secure area called **Quarantine**.

```
Infected File

↓

Quarantine

↓

Cannot Run
```

If it's a false alarm,

the user can restore the file.

---

## Rollback

Some advanced anti-malware solutions keep restore points.

Example

Yesterday

```
Healthy System
```

Today

```
Ransomware Attack
```

The software rolls the system back to yesterday's clean state, reducing data loss.

---

# 5. System Immunization

Anti-malware also prevents future attacks.

---

## Automatic Updates

Hackers create new malware every day.

The anti-malware regularly downloads:

- New malware signatures
- New detection rules
- Improved scanning engines

This keeps protection current.

---

## Patch Vulnerabilities

Old software often has security holes.

Anti-malware may recommend or automatically apply security updates to close these vulnerabilities.

---

# How Anti-Malware Works

Anti-malware uses several techniques together.

---

# 1. Signature-Based Detection

This is the oldest and fastest detection method.

Every malware has a unique **signature** (digital fingerprint).

The anti-malware compares every file against its signature database.

### Process

```
Downloaded File

↓

Compare With Database

↓

Signature Match?

↓

Yes

↓

Malware Detected
```

### Advantages

- Fast
- Accurate
- Few false positives

### Disadvantages

Cannot detect brand-new malware until its signature is added to the database.

---

# 2. Heuristic Analysis

Hackers constantly create new malware.

These threats won't have signatures yet.

Heuristic analysis examines a program's structure and behavior to identify suspicious characteristics.

### What Does It Check?

- File extension
- Hidden code
- Encryption routines
- Registry modifications
- Password-stealing functions
- Attempts to disable security software
- Unexpected network connections

### Example

A program suddenly tries to:

- Encrypt all files
- Disable Windows Defender
- Modify startup settings

Even if it's never been seen before,

the anti-malware recognizes the suspicious behavior and blocks it.

### Advantages

- Detects unknown malware
- Helps stop zero-day threats

### Disadvantages

- May occasionally flag legitimate software (false positives)

---

# 3. Sandboxing

Sometimes a file is suspicious but not clearly malicious.

Instead of running it on your real computer,

the anti-malware runs it in a **Sandbox**.

A sandbox is an isolated virtual environment.

```
Unknown Program

↓

Sandbox

↓

Observe Behavior
```

The software monitors whether the program:

- Creates hidden files
- Modifies the registry
- Contacts suspicious servers
- Disables antivirus
- Encrypts files

If it behaves maliciously,

it is blocked before it ever runs on your actual system.

### Advantage

Safely detects unknown malware without risking your computer.

---

# 4. Cloud-Based Lookups

Modern anti-malware connects to cloud servers maintained by the security vendor.

When an unknown file appears,

the software sends its hash (a unique identifier) or reputation information to the cloud.

```
Unknown File

↓

Vendor Cloud Database

↓

Known Safe?

Known Malware?

Unknown?
```

Millions of users contribute threat intelligence, allowing new threats to be recognized quickly.

### Advantages

- Faster updates
- Global threat intelligence
- Detects newly discovered malware quickly

---

# 5. Real-Time Protection

This is the most important feature.

The anti-malware continuously monitors:

- Files
- USB devices
- Downloads
- Running programs
- Memory
- Network traffic

Example:

You download a file.

Before it opens,

the anti-malware scans it automatically.

If malware is found,

it is blocked immediately.

---

# 6. Rootkit Detection

Rootkits are among the most dangerous malware because they hide deep inside the operating system.

They often evade normal scans.

Anti-malware uses advanced techniques such as:

- Memory analysis
- Boot-time scanning
- Behavioral monitoring
- Kernel integrity checks

to detect and remove rootkits.

---

# 7. Automatic Updates

New malware appears every day.

If the anti-malware isn't updated,

it won't recognize new threats.

Automatic updates provide:

- New malware signatures
- Improved detection engines
- Better heuristic rules
- Latest cloud threat intelligence

---

# Complete Anti-Malware Working Flow

```
File Arrives
      │
      ▼
Real-Time Scan Starts
      │
      ▼
Signature Detection
      │
 ┌────┴─────┐
 │          │
Match     No Match
 │          │
 ▼          ▼
Block   Heuristic Analysis
              │
              ▼
        Still Suspicious?
         ┌────┴─────┐
         │          │
        Yes        No
         │          │
         ▼          ▼
     Run in Sandbox  Allow Execution
         │
         ▼
Malicious Behavior?
    ┌────┴─────┐
    │          │
   Yes        No
    │          │
    ▼          ▼
Quarantine/Delete  Allow Program
```

---

# Advantages of Anti-Malware

- Protects against all types of malware.
- Detects both known and unknown threats.
- Provides continuous real-time protection.
- Blocks malicious websites, downloads, and email attachments.
- Detects hidden threats like rootkits.
- Frequently updates to defend against emerging attacks.

---

# Disadvantages of Anti-Malware

- Uses CPU, RAM, and disk resources.
- Some advanced features require a paid subscription.
- Can occasionally generate false positives.
- No security solution can guarantee 100% protection, especially against sophisticated zero-day attacks.

# Antivirus vs Antimalware

Although these terms are often used interchangeably today, there is a slight difference.

| Feature             | Antivirus                                            | Antimalware                                                                             |
| ------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Main Purpose**    | Protects mainly against traditional viruses          | Protects against all types of malware                                                   |
| **Threats Covered** | Viruses, some worms and Trojans                      | Viruses, worms, Trojans, spyware, ransomware, rootkits, adware, etc.                    |
| **Detection**       | Mainly signature-based with some heuristic detection | Signature-based, behavior analysis, sandboxing, machine learning, and traffic filtering |
| **Updates**         | Regular updates                                      | Typically more frequent updates to address emerging threats                             |
| **Best For**        | Personal computers and common threats                | Modern environments facing advanced and evolving attacks                                |
| **Examples**        | Windows Defender, Avast, AVG, Quick Heal             | Malwarebytes, Spybot Search & Destroy                                                   |

# Browser Sandboxing 

Browser sandboxing is one of the **most important security features** in modern web browsers. It prevents malicious websites, ads, and downloaded files from damaging your computer by **isolating**them from the rest of the operating system.
# What is Browser Sandboxing?

Think of a **sandbox** like a child's sandbox.

- A child can play inside the sandbox.
- The sand stays inside.
- It cannot spread throughout the house.

A browser sandbox works in exactly the same way.

Whenever you visit a website, the browser places that website inside a **restricted environment (sandbox)** where it can run but **cannot freely access your computer**.

Without sandboxing:

```
Website
   │
   ▼
Operating System
   │
   ▼
Files, Passwords, Camera, Documents
```

With sandboxing:

```
Website
   │
   ▼
┌──────────────────────────┐
│       SANDBOX            │
│ Limited permissions      │
│ Limited memory           │
│ No direct file access    │
└──────────────────────────┘
```

Everything the website does remains inside this "box."

---

# Why is Browser Sandboxing Important?

Every website runs code.

For example:

- JavaScript
- HTML
- CSS
- WebAssembly

Normally this code should only display webpages.

However, hackers may insert malicious code into websites.

Without sandboxing that code could:

- Install malware
- Delete files
- Read passwords
- Spy through webcam
- Encrypt files (ransomware)

Sandboxing blocks these actions.

# Why is Browser Sandboxing Needed?

The Internet contains many dangers.

---

## 1. Malware

Malware means **Malicious Software**.

Examples:

- Virus
- Worm
- Trojan
- Spyware
- Ransomware

Some websites automatically attempt to download malware.

Sandboxing prevents it from affecting your computer.

---

## 2. Zero-Day Exploits

A Zero-Day Vulnerability is a software flaw that developers **don't know about yet**.

Since no patch exists:

- Antivirus may miss it.
- Operating system isn't updated.
