> **Database Description (DBD) packets**

# Simple example of DBD

Imagine R1 has:

```
LSA 1
LSA 2
LSA 3
LSA 4
```

R2 has:

```
LSA 1
LSA 2
LSA 3
LSA 5
```

They exchange DBD information.

R1 discovers:

> "R2 has LSA 5, which I don't have."

R2 discovers:

> "R1 has LSA 4, which I don't have."

They now know which information is missing.

That's why the **Exchange** state is important.

# LSDB — Link-State Database

Before going further, understand **LSDB**.

Each OSPF router maintains a database describing the topology of its OSPF area.

The LSDB contains information that allows routers to build this topology picture.

The goal is for routers in the same OSPF area to have synchronized LSDB information.

---

# 17. State 6 — Loading

Now the routers know what information they're missing.

So they request it.

They use:

> **LSR — Link-State Request**

For example:

```
R1:
"I am missing LSA 5."

R1 ─── LSR ───→ R2
```

R2 responds with:

> **LSU — Link-State Update**

```
R2 ─── LSU ───→ R1
```

The LSU contains the requested LSA information.

---

# 18. LSR, LSU and LSA

These three are easy to confuse.

### LSA

**Link-State Advertisement**

The actual link-state information.

Think:

> **Information about the network topology**

### LSR

**Link-State Request**

Router says:

> "I need this LSA."

### LSU

**Link-State Update**

Router says:

> "Here is the requested LSA."

# State 7 — Full

Finally, the routers' LSDBs are synchronized.

```
R1 ═══════════ R2
       FULL
```

This means:

> **The OSPF adjacency is fully established and the databases are synchronized.**

Now both routers have the information they need to calculate paths.

# SPF calculation

Once the topology information is available, OSPF can run its:

> **Shortest Path First (SPF)** algorithm.

The algorithm is commonly associated with **Dijkstra's algorithm**.

Imagine:

```
       10
R1 ───────── R2
│            │
│ 5          │ 2
│            │
R3 ───────── R4
       1
```

OSPF knows the topology and link costs.

It calculates the best paths based on the OSPF metric/cost.

So:

```
LSDB
 ↓
Network topology
 ↓
SPF calculation
 ↓
Best paths
 ↓
Routing table
```

---

# 22. Complete OSPF state journey

Let's put everything together.

```
             OSPF Neighbor Formation
                       │
                       ▼
                    DOWN
                       │
                Receive Hello
                       │
                       ▼
                     INIT
                       │
             See yourself in
             neighbor's Hello
                       │
                       ▼
                    2-WAY
                       │
             DR/BDR election
             where applicable
                       │
                       ▼
                   EXSTART
                       │
             Master/Slave chosen
                       │
                       ▼
                   EXCHANGE
                       │
              Exchange DBDs
                       │
                       ▼
                   LOADING
                       │
            LSR → request LSAs
            LSU → receive LSAs
                       │
                       ▼
                     FULL
                       │
             LSDB synchronized
                       │
                       ▼
                 SPF calculation
```

### Neighbor relationship

Starts when routers discover each other using Hello packets.
### Full adjacency

After database synchronization

What can prevent adjacency?
For two routers to successfully form an adjacency, important OSPF parameters need to be compatible.

Examples include:

### Area ID

If one router is in:

```
Area 0
```

and another is configured for:

```
Area 1
```

on that link, they won't form the expected adjacency.

### Hello/Dead timers

If the timers don't match appropriately, neighbor formation can fail.

### Authentication

If authentication is configured, the routers need compatible authentication settings.

### MTU

An MTU mismatch can cause problems during the database exchange stage.

These are common things to check when an OSPF neighbor is stuck in a state.
# What is IS-IS?

**IS-IS = Intermediate System to Intermediate System**

It is also a **link-state IGP**.

It was originally designed for **OSI networking**, rather than specifically for IP.

Later, IS-IS was extended to support IP routing.

This is one of the most important differences.

Think of it this way:

```
OSPF
Designed primarily for IP
        ↓
IP-based operation

IS-IS
Originally designed for OSI
        ↓
Uses CLNS/Layer 2 mechanisms
        ↓
Can carry IP routing information
```

Today, IS-IS is particularly popular in **large service-provider/ISP networks**.

---

# 3. The biggest difference: Where they operate

This is probably the most important point from your table.

## OSPF

OSPF is carried inside IP.

Conceptually:

```
┌────────────────────┐
│      OSPF          │
├────────────────────┤
│        IP          │
├────────────────────┤
│      Ethernet      │
└────────────────────┘
```

Therefore, OSPF routers communicate using IP-based mechanisms.

---

## IS-IS

IS-IS operates directly over the data-link layer rather than being carried inside IP.

Conceptually:

```
┌────────────────────┐
│      IS-IS         │
├────────────────────┤
│    Data Link       │
├────────────────────┤
│     Ethernet       │
└────────────────────┘
```

This means IS-IS does **not require IP connectivity between neighboring routers for the IS-IS protocol itself**.

That's a major advantage in some provider environments.

---

# 4. Why does IS-IS not need IP connectivity?

Suppose:

```
R1 ───────── R2
```

With OSPF, OSPF communication is carried using IP.

So IP addressing/configuration is involved in establishing OSPF communication.

With IS-IS:

```
R1 ─── Layer 2 ─── R2
       ↑
      IS-IS
```

IS-IS messages are sent using Layer-2 mechanisms associated with CLNS.

Therefore:

> **IS-IS can establish its routing relationship without relying on IP as the transport for the routing protocol.**


## Administrative Distance
Your table says:

|Protocol|Administrative Distance|
|---|---|
|OSPF|**110**|
|IS-IS|**115**|

Administrative Distance (AD) is used by routers to determine how trustworthy a route source is **when routes to the same destination are learned from different routing protocols**.

Lower AD is preferred.
# DR/BDR vs DIS

This is a very important exam question.

## OSPF

On broadcast networks, OSPF elects:

```
DR
+
BDR
```

### DR = Designated Router

The main router for the shared broadcast network.

### BDR = Backup Designated Router

Backup for the DR.

```
        R1
         \
          \
R2 ───── Network ───── R3
          /
         /
        R4

        ↓

       DR
       BDR
```

---

## IS-IS

IS-IS uses:

> **DIS = Designated Intermediate System**

Instead of DR + BDR.

```
IS-IS

Single DIS
```

There isn't an OSPF-style BDR election.

# IS-IS uses Levels

Instead of OSPF's area structure, IS-IS primarily uses:

### Level 1

Responsible for routing **within an area**.

### Level 2

Responsible for routing **between areas**.

### Level 1/Level 2

Can perform both functions.

Think:

```
IS-IS

Area A                 Area B
┌─────────┐           ┌─────────┐
│ Level 1 │───────────│ Level 1 │
└─────────┘   L2      └─────────┘
       \               /
        └── Level 2 ──┘
```
# Router identification

## OSPF

OSPF uses a:

> **Router ID**

Example:

```
Router ID = 1.1.1.1
```

The Router ID uniquely identifies the OSPF router.

---

## IS-IS

IS-IS uses a:

> **System ID**

Example:

```
System ID = 0000.0000.0001
```

The System ID identifies the IS-IS router.

# Virtual Links

### OSPF

OSPF supports:

> **Virtual Links**

A virtual link can be used to logically connect an area to the OSPF backbone under certain network designs.
### IS-IS

IS-IS doesn't use OSPF-style virtual links.
# Security difference

Your source says IS-IS is less exposed because it operates at Layer 2.

The basic idea is reasonable, but don't interpret it as:

> "IS-IS is completely secure."

It isn't.

IS-IS still needs appropriate security mechanisms and protection.
# Flexibility

Your source says:

> IS-IS is more flexible, especially in provider networks.

Why?

One major reason is that IS-IS was designed around a flexible link-state database and can carry information beyond basic IP routing.

This has made IS-IS particularly attractive in large service-provider environments.

OSPF is also highly capable and widely deployed.

# Why IS-IS is popular with ISPs

Imagine a huge provider network:

```
             ISP Backbone

        R1 ───── R2 ───── R3
        │        │        │
        │        │        │
        R4 ───── R5 ───── R6
        │                 │
        R7 ────────────── R8
```

There could be hundreds or thousands of routers.

IS-IS is popular in such environments because of characteristics such as:

- Strong hierarchical design using L1/L2
- Efficient link-state operation
- Layer-2 protocol transport
- Flexible TLV-based information encoding
# TLVs — an important IS-IS concept

One reason IS-IS is considered flexible is its extensive use of:

> **TLV = Type-Length-Value**

Think of it as a flexible information container.

```
Type | Length | Value
```

For example:

```
Type = X
Length = 4
Value = some information
```

New capabilities can be represented using additional TLVs without redesigning the entire basic protocol structure.

This has helped IS-IS evolve over time.
# EIGRP — Enhanced Interior Gateway Routing Protocol

## What is EIGRP?

**EIGRP (Enhanced Interior Gateway Routing Protocol)** is an **Interior Gateway Protocol (IGP)** used to exchange routing information **inside an Autonomous System (AS)**.

It is commonly described as an **advanced distance-vector protocol**
> **EIGRP is an IGP designed for routing within an organization/AS and uses DUAL to quickly calculate loop-free routes.**

It operates at **Layer 3** and uses **IP protocol number 88**.

---

# 2. Where does EIGRP fit?

Think about routing protocols like this:

```
                 Routing Protocols
                       │
          ┌────────────┴────────────┐
          │                         │
        IGP                        EGP
   Inside an AS                Between ASes
          │                         │
   ┌──────┼──────┐                 BGP
   │      │      │
 OSPF   EIGRP   IS-IS
```

So:

```
EIGRP → Inside an AS
OSPF  → Inside an AS
IS-IS → Inside an AS
BGP   → Between ASes
```

---

# 3. Administrative Distance in EIGRP

**Administrative Distance (AD)** tells a router how trustworthy a route source is.

Generally:

> **Lower AD = more preferred**

EIGRP has different AD values depending on the type of route:

|EIGRP Route|AD|
|---|---|
|Summary|5|
|Internal|90|
|External|170|

### Internal route

A route learned through normal EIGRP operation.

```
EIGRP internal → AD 90
```

### External route

A route redistributed into EIGRP from another routing source.

```
Other protocol
      ↓
 Redistribution
      ↓
EIGRP external → AD 170
```

### Summary route

An EIGRP summary route has:

```
AD = 5
```

The important exam numbers are:

> **5, 90, 170**

---

# 4. How EIGRP discovers neighbors

Before two routers can exchange routing information, they need to become **EIGRP neighbors**.

Imagine:

```
R1 ───────── R2
```

R1 sends **Hello** messages.

R2 receives them and responds appropriately.

Eventually:

```
R1 ↔ R2
   Neighbor
```

Once the relationship is established, the routers can exchange routing information.

---

# 5. EIGRP message types

Your notes list several important EIGRP messages.

## 1. Hello

Used for:

- Neighbor discovery
- Maintaining neighbor relationships

Typical default values in the notes:

```
Hello = 5 seconds
```
# Update

An **Update** message carries routing information.

There are two important ideas:

### Full update

Used when a neighbor relationship is initially established.

The router provides the routing information needed to synchronize the neighbor.

### Partial update

When something changes:

```
Network X
    ↓
Path changes
    ↓
EIGRP sends update
    ↓
Only relevant changed information
```

This is more efficient than repeatedly sending the entire routing table.

---

# 7. Query

Suppose:

```
R1 ─── R2 ─── Network X
```

R2 loses its route to Network X.

R2 may ask neighboring EIGRP routers:

> "Do you have another route to Network X?"

That is the purpose of a **Query**.

```
R2
 │
 ├──── Query ────> R1
 │
 └──── Query ────> R3
```

---

# 8. Reply

The neighbors respond to the Query with a **Reply**.

```
R2 ─── Query ───> R1

R1 ─── Reply ───> R2
```

The reply tells EIGRP whether an alternate path exists.

---

# 9. Acknowledgement

Some EIGRP messages require reliable delivery.

An acknowledgment confirms receipt.

Your notes describe an acknowledgment as a Hello packet with no routing data.

So:

```
Update → needs acknowledgment
Query  → needs acknowledgment
Reply  → needs acknowledgment
```

Whereas:

```
Hello → doesn't require acknowledgment
ACK   → itself doesn't require acknowledgment
```

---

# 10. Composite Metric

This is one of the most important EIGRP concepts.

EIGRP uses a **composite metric** to determine which path is preferable.

The metric can consider:

- Bandwidth
- Load
- Delay
- Reliability
- MTU
# Why EIGRP is fast

The key word is:

> **DUAL**

DUAL = **Diffusing Update Algorithm**

It helps EIGRP calculate loop-free paths and react quickly to topology changes.

Suppose:

```
        R2
       /  \
      /    \
     R1     R3
      \    /
       \  /
        R4
```

Suppose R1 normally uses:

```
R1 → R2 → destination
```

If R2 fails, EIGRP can potentially use an alternate route through R4/R3 without rebuilding everything from scratch.

This contributes to EIGRP's fast convergence.

---

# 12. EIGRP's big advantage: unequal-cost load balancing

This is an important feature.

Many routing protocols traditionally prefer equal-cost paths.

EIGRP can support:

> **Unequal-cost load balancing**

For example:

```
Path A = metric 100
Path B = metric 150
```

EIGRP can potentially use both paths under appropriate conditions/configuration.

That makes EIGRP useful when multiple paths have different capacities/costs.

---

# 13. EIGRP neighbor requirements

For two routers to form an EIGRP neighbor relationship, important parameters must agree.

Your notes mention:

### 1. Same AS number

```
R1 → AS 100
R2 → AS 100
```

Works.

```
R1 → AS 100
R2 → AS 200
```

They won't form the intended EIGRP adjacency.

### 2. Matching K values

The metric-related K values need to match.

### 3. Authentication

If authentication is configured, the routers must use compatible authentication settings.

Your notes specifically mention MD5.

### 4. Address/subnet considerations

The routers must be appropriately addressed and able to communicate over the relevant interfaces.
# BGP

Now we move from **inside an AS** to **between ASes**.

## What is BGP?

**BGP = Border Gateway Protocol**

BGP is the primary routing protocol used for exchanging reachability information between **Autonomous Systems** on the Internet.

It is classified as a:

> **Path-vector routing protocol**

This is different from:

```
OSPF  → Link-state
EIGRP → Advanced distance-vector
IS-IS → Link-state
BGP   → Path-vector
```

---

# 16. What is an Autonomous System?

An **Autonomous System (AS)** is a collection of networks under a common administrative control that presents a common routing policy to the Internet.

# Why do we need BGP?

Imagine the Internet consisted of:

```
AS 100
   │
AS 200
   │
AS 300
   │
AS 400
```

Each AS has its own internal network.

BGP tells the ASes:

> "I can reach these networks, and here is the path/policy information associated with reaching them."

So BGP essentially helps build an inter-AS view of reachability.

---

# 18. BGP is a path-vector protocol

This is extremely important.

Suppose:

```
AS 100 → AS 200 → AS 300
```

A BGP route may carry information such as:

```
Destination: Network X

AS_PATH:
100 200 300
```

The AS path provides information about which autonomous systems the route advertisement has traversed.

This also helps prevent routing loops.

If an AS sees its own AS number already in the AS_PATH, it can reject that route.

---

# 19. BGP runs over TCP

This is another major exam point.

```
BGP
 ↓
TCP
 ↓
IP
 ↓
Ethernet
```

BGP uses:

> **TCP port 179**

Compare:

```
BGP  → TCP
OSPF → IP protocol 89
EIGRP → IP protocol 88
```

So don't say OSPF uses TCP or UDP.

---

# 20. eBGP vs iBGP

There are two major forms.

## eBGP

**External BGP**

Used between different ASes.

```
AS 100                 AS 200

R1 ───── eBGP ───── R2
```

R1 belongs to AS 100.

R2 belongs to AS 200.

---

## iBGP

**Internal BGP**

Used between BGP routers within the same AS.

```
          AS 100
      ┌────────────┐
      │            │
     R1 ── iBGP ── R2
      │            │
      └────────────┘
```

So:

```
eBGP → different ASes
iBGP → same AS
```

---

# 21. How BGP establishes a relationship

BGP routers establish a TCP connection.

Conceptually:

```
R1                          R2
│                           │
│──── TCP connection ──────>│
│                           │
│──── BGP OPEN ────────────>│
│<─── BGP OPEN ─────────────│
│                           │
│──── UPDATE ──────────────>│
│<──── UPDATE ──────────────│
```

BGP then exchanges reachability information.

BGP also periodically uses **KEEPALIVE** messages to maintain the session.

---

# 22. BGP path attributes

BGP doesn't simply say:

> "This path has the fewest hops, so use it."

Instead, BGP uses **attributes and policies**.

Important ones include:

### Weight

Cisco-specific attribute.

```
Higher Weight → preferred
```

It is local to the router.

---

### Local Preference

Used to influence the preferred **outbound path from an AS**.

```
Higher Local Preference → preferred
```

Unlike Weight, Local Preference is intended to be shared within the AS.

### AS_PATH

Shows the sequence of ASes through which the route has passed.
### NEXT_HOP

Tells the router the next-hop address used to reach the advertised destination.

---

# 23. Why BGP is slower than OSPF

BGP is designed for a completely different purpose.

OSPF wants:

> "Find the shortest path inside my network quickly."

BGP wants:

> "Choose a route according to reachability, policies, attributes and AS-level paths."

Therefore:

```
OSPF
Fast convergence
       ↓
Internal network

BGP
Policy-driven
       ↓
Internet-scale routing
```

BGP deliberately prioritizes **stability, scalability and policy control** rather than simply achieving the fastest possible convergence.

---

# 24. BGP vs OSPF

The key differences:

|Feature|BGP|OSPF|
|---|---|---|
|Type|Path-vector|Link-state|
|Scope|Inter-domain/AS|Intra-domain|
|Main purpose|Between ASes|Inside an AS|
|Transport|TCP|IP|
|TCP port|179|N/A|
|Algorithm|Path-vector decision process|SPF/Dijkstra|
|Convergence|Generally slower|Generally faster|
|Policy|Very strong|More limited|
|Internet routing|Yes|No, not as the Internet's inter-domain protocol|

---

# 25. Now MPLS

This is a completely different concept.

**MPLS = Multiprotocol Label Switching**

Instead of forwarding packets purely by repeatedly examining the destination IP address, MPLS uses **labels**.

Think of it like a railway system.

An IP packet is like:

> "I need to go to City X."

MPLS gives it a label:

```
LABEL 42
```

The routers in the MPLS core can then make forwarding decisions based primarily on that label.

---

# 26. Why MPLS?

Traditional IP forwarding:

```
Packet
  ↓
Look at destination IP
  ↓
Routing table lookup
  ↓
Determine next hop
  ↓
Forward
```

MPLS:

```
Packet
  ↓
Look at MPLS label
  ↓
Label lookup
  ↓
Swap label
  ↓
Forward
```

Historically this was associated with faster forwarding, although modern hardware can perform IP forwarding extremely quickly too.

Today, MPLS's major value is not simply "speed."

It is especially useful for:

- Traffic engineering
- VPN services
- QoS
- Provider networks
- Predictable forwarding paths
# Why is MPLS called Layer 2.5?

MPLS doesn't fit neatly into the traditional Layer 2/Layer 3 model.

Its label sits between the Layer 2 and Layer 3 headers.

# MPLS label stack

MPLS can use multiple labels.

For example:

```
┌───────────────┐
│ Label 100     │
├───────────────┤
│ Label 250     │
├───────────────┤
│ IP Packet     │
└───────────────┘
```

This is called a:

> **Label stack**

Multiple labels are useful for hierarchical forwarding and services such as MPLS VPNs.

---

# 30. Important MPLS routers

There are three important terms.

## CE

**Customer Edge**

Customer-side router.

```
Customer
   │
  CE
   │
   PE
```

---

## PE

**Provider Edge**

Router at the edge of the service-provider MPLS network.

```
Customer
   │
  CE
   │
  PE
   │
MPLS Core
```

The PE is where traffic enters or leaves the provider's MPLS domain.

---

## LSR

**Label Switch Router**

A router in the MPLS network that forwards packets using labels.

---

# 31. Ingress, intermediate and egress

Think about an MPLS network like this:

```
Customer
   │
  CE
   │
   ▼
Ingress PE
   │
   ▼
LSR
   │
   ▼
LSR
   │
   ▼
Egress PE
   │
   ▼
  CE
   │
Customer
```

Now let's follow a packet.

---

# 32. Step 1 — Ingress: PUSH

The customer sends an IP packet:

```
CE
 │
 │ IP packet
 ▼
PE
```

The ingress PE determines the appropriate MPLS forwarding treatment.

It adds a label.

This operation is:

> **PUSH**

```
Before:

[IP Packet]

After:

[MPLS Label][IP Packet]
```

---

# 33. Step 2 — Core: SWAP

Now the packet enters the MPLS core.

Suppose:

```
Label = 100
```

The next LSR might have a forwarding instruction:

```
Incoming label 100
        ↓
Outgoing label 250
```

So it replaces:

```
100 → 250
```

This is called:

> **SWAP**

```
LSR
 │
 │ 100
 ▼
[SWAP]
 │
 │ 250
 ▼
Next LSR
```

The core can therefore forward based on labels rather than doing a normal IP routing lookup for every MPLS transit hop.

---

# 34. Step 3 — Egress: POP

Eventually the packet reaches the egress side.

The MPLS label is removed.

This is:

> **POP**

```
[MPLS Label][IP Packet]
          ↓
       POP
          ↓
     [IP Packet]
```

The IP packet can then be forwarded toward the destination customer.

---

# 35. PUSH, SWAP, POP

This is probably the easiest MPLS concept to memorize:

```
Ingress → PUSH
Core    → SWAP
Egress  → POP
```

Remember:

> **Push = add**
> 
> **Swap = replace**
> 
> **Pop = remove**

---

# 36. What is LDP?

Your notes mention:

> **LDP = Label Distribution Protocol**

LDP can distribute label bindings between MPLS routers.

Conceptually:

```
Router R1
   │
   │ Label information
   ▼
Router R2
```

This allows routers to establish label mappings used for MPLS forwarding.

Important distinction:

```
Routing protocol
      ↓
Determines reachability/topology

LDP
      ↓
Distributes labels associated with routes
```

MPLS can also be used with other control-plane mechanisms, such as RSVP-TE or Segment Routing, depending on the design.

---

# 37. MPLS and VPN

This is another extremely important concept.

Suppose one ISP provides service to two customers:

```
Customer A
    │
   PE
    │
 ┌──┴──────────┐
 │ MPLS Core   │
 └──┬──────────┘
    │
   PE
    │
Customer B
```

MPLS can keep different customers' traffic logically separated.

But:

> **MPLS itself does NOT encrypt the traffic.**

This distinction is extremely important.

---

# 38. MPLS vs encryption

Think:

```
MPLS
  ↓
Traffic isolation / forwarding

Encryption
  ↓
Confidentiality
```

MPLS can provide logical separation, but if you need encryption against someone who can observe the traffic, you need an encryption mechanism such as an appropriate VPN/IPsec design.

So:

> **MPLS VPN ≠ automatically encrypted VPN**

---

# 39. Why providers like MPLS

Imagine an ISP carrying traffic for:

```
Company A
Company B
Company C
Company D
```

The provider can use MPLS mechanisms to separate and manage their traffic.

It can support:

- Layer 3 VPNs
- Traffic engineering
- QoS
- Scalable provider services
- Predictable forwarding behavior

This is why MPLS became very important in carrier networks.

---

# 40. Advantages of MPLS

### 1. Traffic engineering

The provider can control how traffic travels through the network.

For example:

```
Normal path:
A → B → C → D

Traffic-engineered path:
A → E → F → D
```

This can help avoid congested links.

---

### 2. QoS

Different traffic can receive different treatment.

For example:

```
Voice → High priority
Video → Medium/high priority
File transfer → Normal priority
```

---

### 3. VPN support

MPLS is widely used to build provider-managed VPN services.

---

### 4. Scalability

It can support large provider networks and many customer VPNs.

---

# 41. Disadvantages of MPLS

### Expensive

Provider MPLS infrastructure is more expensive than a simple small IP network.

### Complex

MPLS involves:

- Labels
- LSRs
- PE/CE architecture
- Routing protocols
- Label distribution
- VPN concepts

So it requires specialized knowledge.
# What is Internet Protocol (IP)?

**IP = Internet Protocol.**

It is a **Network Layer (Layer 3) protocol** used to deliver data from one device/network to another.

The most important job of IP is:

> **Give devices logical addresses and deliver packets toward the correct destination.**
# Why is IP important?

Imagine millions of devices connected to the Internet.

How would your computer know where to send data?

That's where IP addressing comes in.

Every network interface participating in IP communication has an **IP address**.

An IP address has two conceptual roles:

### Identification

It identifies a particular network interface/device within the network context.

### Location

It tells routers approximately **where in the network topology the destination belongs**.

This second point is extremely important.

An IP address isn't simply a permanent "name" of a computer. It also provides information that routers can use to determine **which network to forward the packet toward**.

---

# 3. IP is connectionless

This is one of the most important characteristics of IP.

IP is a **connectionless protocol**.

That means IP does **not** first establish a dedicated connection between sender and receiver before sending every packet.
IP itself doesn't guarantee:

- delivery
- ordering
- duplicate prevention
- retransmission

Those responsibilities can be handled by higher-layer protocols such as **TCP**.

# IP works with ICMP

You previously looked at **ICMP**, so this connection is important.

**ICMP = Internet Control Message Protocol.**

ICMP is used to communicate things such as:

- destination unreachable
- time exceeded
- echo request
- echo reply
## IP Address

A logical address used by IP.

Example IPv4:

```
192.168.1.10
```

Example IPv6:

```
2001:db8::1
```

---

## Packet

A **packet** is a unit of data transmitted across an IP network.

An IP packet contains mainly:

```
+----------------------+
|      IP Header       |
+----------------------+
|       Payload        |
+----------------------+
```

The header contains control information.

The payload contains the data being carried.

---

## Router

A router connects different networks and forwards packets between them.

For example:

```
Network A
   |
   |
 Router
   |
   |
Network B
```

A router examines the **destination IP address** and consults its **routing table**.

It then decides:

> "Which interface should I send this packet out of?"

---

# 6. IPv4 vs IPv6

There are two major versions of IP.

## IPv4

IPv4 uses **32-bit addresses**.

Example:

```
192.168.1.10
```

IPv4 has approximately:

**4.3 billion possible addresses** (2³²).

Because the Internet grew enormously, IPv4 addresses became scarce.

---

## IPv6

IPv6 uses **128-bit addresses**.

Example:

```
2001:db8:abcd:1234::1
```

It provides an enormous address space:

**2¹²⁸ addresses.**

IPv6 was designed largely to solve the address-exhaustion problem and also introduces improvements in areas such as addressing, autoconfiguration, and packet handling

## TOP 20 Essential Protocols

These protocols and ports are fundamental for network communication, troubleshooting, and securing services.

| Port | Protocol | Common Service       | Use Case                     |
| ---- | -------- | -------------------- | ---------------------------- |
| 20   | TCP      | FTP (Data)           | File transfers (active mode) |
| 21   | TCP      | FTP (Control)        | File transfer control        |
| 22   | TCP      | SSH                  | Secure remote login, SFTP    |
| 23   | TCP      | Telnet               | Remote login (insecure)      |
| 25   | TCP      | SMTP                 | Sending email                |
| 53   | TCP/UDP  | DNS                  | Domain name resolution       |
| 67   | UDP      | DHCP (Server)        | Assigning IP addresses       |
| 68   | UDP      | DHCP (Client)        | Receiving IP addresses       |
| 80   | TCP      | HTTP                 | Web traffic                  |
| 110  | TCP      | POP3                 | Receiving email              |
| 123  | UDP      | NTP                  | Time synchronization         |
| 135  | TCP      | Microsoft RPC        | DCOM services on Windows     |
| 137  | UDP      | NetBIOS Name Service | Windows file sharing         |
| 138  | UDP      | NetBIOS Datagram     | Windows file sharing         |
| 139  | TCP      | NetBIOS Session      | Windows SMB traffic          |
| 143  | TCP      | IMAP                 | Email retrieval              |
| 161  | UDP      | SNMP                 | Network management           |
| 443  | TCP      | HTTPS                | Secure web traffic           |
| 445  | TCP      | Microsoft-DS (SMB)   | File and printer sharing     |
| 3389 | TCP      | RDP                  | Remote Desktop Protocol      |
# TCP and UDP

You'll see TCP and UDP repeatedly in the table.

These are **Transport Layer protocols (Layer 4)**.
Modern HTTPS can also run over **QUIC**, which uses UDP, so the simplified TCP-only picture isn't universally true today.

---

# 9. How IP addressing works

Suppose your computer wants to communicate with another computer.

Your computer has:

```
Source IP:
192.168.1.10
```

The destination is:

```
Destination IP:
192.168.5.20
```

The IP packet contains these addresses:

```
+--------------------------------+
| Source IP      192.168.1.10    |
| Destination IP 192.168.5.20    |
+--------------------------------+
|            Data                |
+--------------------------------+
```

The packet is then sent toward the destination.

---

# 10. Static vs Dynamic IP addresses

An IP address can be assigned in different ways.

## Static

The address is manually/configurationally assigned and generally remains the same.

Example:

```
Server
↓
192.168.1.100
```

## Dynamic

The address is assigned automatically, commonly using **DHCP**.

For example:

```
Computer
   |
   | DHCP
   ↓
DHCP Server
   |
   ↓
192.168.1.25
```

Your home router commonly acts as the DHCP server for devices on your local network.

---

# 11. Where does DNS fit?

Suppose you type:

```
google.com
```

Your computer doesn't directly put `"google.com"` into the IP packet as the destination.

It needs to discover an IP address associated with the domain.

DNS helps:

```
google.com
     ↓
    DNS
     ↓
IP address
     ↓
IP communication
```

# What happens when the destination is on another network?

This is where the router becomes important.

Suppose:

```
Your PC:
192.168.1.10

Destination:
192.168.5.20
```

Your PC determines that:

```
192.168.5.20
```

is not on its local subnet.

So it sends the frame to its **default gateway**.
**The IP destination remains the final destination IP while routers forward the packet hop by hop.**

At each local link, however, the Layer-2 frame is addressed to the **next hop's MAC address**.

---

# 14. What is a routing table?

Every router has a **routing table**.

It contains information about where different destination networks should be forward
Suppose a packet arrives:

```
Destination:
192.168.3.50
```

The router looks at the routing table and determines:

```
192.168.3.0/24
       ↓
Next hop: 10.0.0.3
```

It forwards the packet accordingly.
# What are routing protocols?

Routers need to learn about networks.

Routing protocols help routers exchange routing information.

Examples include:

### OSPF

An interior gateway routing protocol used within an organization/network.

### EIGRP

An advanced distance-vector routing protocol, traditionally associated with Cisco environments.

### BGP

Used to exchange routing information between **autonomous systems** and is fundamental to Internet routing.

You were recently studying **OSPF, EIGRP and BGP**, so this is where they connect to IP.

Think:

```
IP
 ↓
Packet needs a path
 ↓
Router
 ↓
Routing table
 ↓
Routing protocols help build/update routing information
```

---

# 16. What is NAT?

NAT = **Network Address Translation**.

It is commonly used in private networks.

Suppose your home has:

```
Laptop      192.168.1.10
Phone       192.168.1.11
PC          192.168.1.12
```

These are private IP addresses.

Your router may have one public IP:

```
Public IP:
203.x.x.x
```

NAT allows multiple internal devices to communicate with the Internet using that public address.

# How IP routing actually happens

Let's walk through a complete example.

Suppose you open a website.

Your computer eventually needs to send packets toward the server's IP address.

Imagine:

```
Your PC
192.168.1.10
     |
     ↓
Home Router
     |
     ↓
ISP Router
     |
     ↓
Internet Router
     |
     ↓
Destination Network
     |
     ↓
Web Server
```

### Step 1 — Application generates data

Your browser creates data.

```
Browser
   ↓
HTTPS/HTTP
```

### Step 2 — Transport layer processes it

Depending on the protocol:

```
TCP / UDP / QUIC
```

### Step 3 — IP encapsulates it

IP adds its header:

```
Source IP
Destination IP
Other IP information
```

Now we have an IP packet.

### Step 4 — Packet goes to the next hop

Your computer checks whether the destination is local.

If not, it sends the packet toward its default gateway.

### Step 5 — Router examines destination IP

The router looks at:

```
Destination IP
```

and performs a routing-table lookup.

### Step 6 — Router forwards the packet

It sends the packet to the appropriate next hop.

### Step 7 — More routers repeat the process

```
Router 1
   ↓
Router 2
   ↓
Router 3
   ↓
Router 4
```

Each router makes its own forwarding decision.

### Step 8 — Destination receives it

Eventually:

```
Destination Server
```

receives the packets.

Higher-layer protocols then process the contents.

---

# 20. Can different packets take different routes?

**Yes.**

This is an important property of packet-switched networks.

Suppose you send:

```
Packet 1
Packet 2
Packet 3
```

They may potentially travel through different paths:

```
Packet 1 → Router A → Router C → Server

Packet 2 → Router B → Router C → Server

Packet 3 → Router A → Router D → Server
```

IP itself does not promise that all packets will follow exactly the same path.

This is one reason TCP is important: TCP can detect missing/out-of-order data and provide reliable, ordered delivery to the application.

# Understanding the email example

The article's email example is trying to explain **encapsulation and decapsulation**.

Suppose you send an email.

The data moves down the protocol stack:

```
Email Application
       ↓
     SMTP
       ↓
      TCP
       ↓
       IP
       ↓
Ethernet/Wi-Fi
```

Each layer adds its own information.

Conceptually:

```
Application Data
       ↓
+----------------+
| TCP Header     |
| Application    |
| Data           |
+----------------+
       ↓
+----------------+
| IP Header      |
| TCP Header     |
| Application    |
| Data           |
+----------------+
       ↓
+----------------+
| Ethernet/Wi-Fi |
| Header         |
| IP Header      |
| TCP Header     |
| Application    |
| Data           |
+----------------+
```

This is called **encapsulation**.

---

# 22. What happens at the receiving server?

The reverse happens.

The receiving device removes/processes the headers at each layer.

This is called **decapsulation**.

# Generic Routing Encapsulation (GRE) Tunnel
GRE is a **tunneling protocol**.

Its main purpose is to take an existing packet and **encapsulate it inside another packet** so that it can travel across a network.

Think of GRE like putting a letter inside another envelope:

```
Original packet
      ↓
┌──────────────────────┐
│ GRE/Outer information│
│ ┌──────────────────┐ │
│ │ Original packet  │ │
│ └──────────────────┘ │
└──────────────────────┘
```

The outside packet is used to transport the original packet across the network.

---

# 2. Why do we need a GRE tunnel?

Imagine two company networks:

```
Office A                         Office B

PC1 ── R1 ── Internet ── R2 ── Server
```

R1 and R2 aren't directly connected.

Normally, R1 would route packets through the intermediate network.

But suppose the company wants the two routers to behave as though they have a **logical direct connection**.

GRE can create:

```
Office A                         Office B

PC1 ── R1 ================= R2 ── Server
             GRE Tunnel
```

There may actually be many physical routers between R1 and R2:

```
R1 → ISP Router → Router → Router → R2
```

But GRE makes it look logically like:

```
R1 ================= R2
       GRE Tunnel
```

That's why it's called a **tunnel**.
# What does "encapsulation" mean?

This is the most important concept.

Suppose PC1 wants to send:

```
Source IP:      10.20.1.10
Destination IP: 10.20.2.10
```

Normally the packet might look conceptually like:

```
┌─────────────────────────────┐
│ Original IP Header          │
│ Src: 10.20.1.10             │
│ Dst: 10.20.2.10             │
├─────────────────────────────┤
│ Original Data               │
└─────────────────────────────┘
```

R1 receives this packet.

Instead of simply forwarding it normally, GRE **wraps the original packet**.

Conceptually:

```
┌─────────────────────────────┐
│ Outer IP Header             │
│ Src: R1 tunnel endpoint     │
│ Dst: R2 tunnel endpoint     │
├─────────────────────────────┤
│ GRE Header                  │
├─────────────────────────────┤
│ Original IP Header          │
│ Src: 10.20.1.10             │
│ Dst: 10.20.2.10             │
├─────────────────────────────┤
│ Original Data               │
└─────────────────────────────┘
```

This is **GRE encapsulation**.

---

# 4. There are two sets of addresses

This is where beginners often get confused.

Suppose:

```
PC1 = 10.20.1.10
Server = 10.20.2.10
```

The original packet contains:

```
Original Source:
10.20.1.10

Original Destination:
10.20.2.10
```

But the GRE tunnel might have endpoints:

```
R1 = 10.40.20.1
R2 = 10.40.20.2
```

So after encapsulation:

```
OUTER IP
Source      = 10.40.20.1
Destination = 10.40.20.2

GRE HEADER

INNER IP
Source      = 10.20.1.10
Destination = 10.20.2.10
```

Think of it this way:

**Outer IP = "Where should this tunnel packet go?"**

**Inner IP = "Where does the original packet ultimately belong**

# GRE encapsulation process

Let's follow the packet.

## Step 1 — PC1 creates a packet

PC1 wants to communicate with the server.

```
PC1
10.20.1.10
      ↓
Server
10.20.2.10
```

The original IP packet is:

```
Source      = 10.20.1.10
Destination = 10.20.2.10
```

---

## Step 2 — Packet reaches R1

R1 is configured with a GRE tunnel toward R2.

R1 decides:

> "This traffic should be sent through my GRE tunnel."

---

## Step 3 — R1 adds GRE encapsulation

R1 adds a **GRE header** and an **outer IP header**.

Conceptually:

```
┌─────────────────────────────┐
│ Outer IP Header             │
│ 10.40.20.1 → 10.40.20.2    │
├─────────────────────────────┤
│ GRE Header                  │
├─────────────────────────────┤
│ Original IP Packet          │
│ 10.20.1.10 → 10.20.2.10    │
└─────────────────────────────┘
```

Now the packet can travel through the intermediate network.

---

# 6. What happens in the middle?

This is important.

Suppose the physical network is:

```
R1
 |
ISP Router
 |
Router A
 |
Router B
 |
R2
```

The intermediate routers don't need to understand the original communication.

They simply see the **outer IP packet**.

They essentially see:

```
Source      = 10.40.20.1
Destination = 10.40.20.2
```

So they route the packet toward:

```
10.40.20.2
```

which is the tunnel's remote endpoint.

Conceptually:

```
R1
 │
 ▼
Router A
 │
 ▼
Router B
 │
 ▼
Router C
 │
 ▼
R2
```

The GRE tunnel makes these physical hops behave like one logical connection between R1 and R2.

---

# 7. What happens at R2?

R2 receives the GRE packet.

It recognizes:

> "This packet is for my GRE tunnel."

R2 removes:

```
Outer IP Header
       +
GRE Header
```

This process is called **decapsulation**.

The original packet remains:

```
Source      = 10.20.1.10
Destination = 10.20.2.10
```

R2 then forwards it toward the server.

```
R2 ─────────→ Server
              10.20.2.10
```
# What is a tunnel interface?

When GRE is configured, routers create a **logical/virtual interface** called a tunnel interface.

For example:

```
R1
Tunnel0
IP: 10.40.20.1
```

and:

```
R2
Tunnel0
IP: 10.40.20.2
```

These aren't necessarily physical interfaces like:

```
GigabitEthernet0/0
GigabitEthernet0/1
```

They're logical interfaces representing the GRE tunnel.
# . Why give the tunnel interfaces IP addresses?

Because now routers can **route traffic through the tunnel like a normal IP link**.

For example:

```
R1 Tunnel0
10.40.20.1
      │
      │
      │
R2 Tunnel0
10.40.20.2
```

R1 can have a route like:

```
10.20.2.0/24 → Tunnel0
```

Meaning:

> "To reach the 10.20.2.0/24 network, send the traffic through the GRE tunnel."
# What does "supports multiprotocol traffic" mean?

GRE isn't limited to simply carrying IPv4 traffic.

It can encapsulate different types of network-layer traffic, including things such as:

- IPv4
- IPv6
- multicast traffic
# Is GRE secure?

### This is VERY important:

**GRE does NOT provide encryption.**

The article's wording saying GRE transports packets "securely" is misleading if it means **confidentiality**.

GRE provides:

> **Encapsulation and tunneling**

It does **not** inherently provide:

> **Encryption**

So if someone can observe the GRE traffic, the contents aren't automatically protected by GRE encryption.

---

# 15. GRE + IPsec

This is why GRE is often combined with **IPsec**.

Think:

```
GRE
 ↓
Creates the tunnel / carries traffic
```

and:

```
IPsec
 ↓
Provides encryption/security
```

Together:

```
Original Packet
      ↓
     GRE
      ↓
    IPsec
      ↓
Encrypted traffic
      ↓
   Internet
      ↓
     IPsec
      ↓
     GRE
      ↓
Original Packet
```

# . GRE overhead

GRE adds additional headers to the original packet.

Original:

```
[ IP Header ][ Data ]
```

After GRE:

```
[ Outer IP ][ GRE ][ Original IP ][ Data ]
```

Therefore the packet becomes larger.

This is called **overhead**.
# GRE vs normal routing

Without GRE:

```
PC1
 ↓
R1
 ↓
Router
 ↓
Router
 ↓
R2
 ↓
Server
```

R1 simply routes the original IP packet through the network.

With GRE:

```
PC1
 ↓
R1
 ↓
GRE Encapsulation
 ↓
=================
   GRE Tunnel
=================
 ↓
R2
 ↓
GRE Decapsulation
 ↓
Server
```

The underlying routers transport the **outer packet**, while R1 and R2 handle the GRE tunnel.

---

# 19. The two IP addresses you MUST understand

Suppose:

```
PC1 = 10.20.1.10
Server = 10.20.2.10

R1 tunnel = 10.40.20.1
R2 tunnel = 10.40.20.2
```

When PC1 sends data:

### Original/inner packet

```
10.20.1.10
     ↓
10.20.2.10
```

### GRE outer packet

```
10.40.20.1
     ↓
10.40.20.2
```

So:

**Inner IP addresses = original communication**

**Outer IP addresses = GRE tunnel endpoints**

# Transport Layer
The **Transport Layer is Layer 4 of the OSI model**. Its main job is to provide **process-to-process communication** between applications running on different devices.

Think of it as the layer that makes sure data reaches the **correct application**, not just the correct computer.
## 1. Main Functions

### 🔹 End-to-end communication

Provides communication between applications/processes on two different hosts.

### 🔹 Port numbers

IP addresses identify the **host**, while port numbers identify the **application/process**.

For example:

```
192.168.1.10:443
       │       │
       │       └── Port → HTTPS service
       └────────── IP → Host
```

### 🔹 Segmentation

The Transport Layer breaks large application data into smaller units.

```
Large Data
   ↓
Segment 1
Segment 2
Segment 3
```

TCP calls them **segments**, while UDP calls them **datagrams**.

### 🔹 Reliability

TCP can provide:

- acknowledgments
- sequencing
- retransmission
- error detection

UDP does not provide these reliability mechanisms.

### 🔹 Flow control

Prevents a fast sender from overwhelming a slow receiver.

### 🔹 Multiplexing/Demultiplexing

Allows many applications to use the network simultaneously using **port numbers**.

# How the Transport Layer Works

Suppose you have Chrome, Discord, and another application communicating simultaneously.

The Transport Layer uses different ports to distinguish their data:

```
Chrome   → Port 443
App      → Port 5000
Service  → Port 6000
```

The Network Layer gets the data to the correct **device**, while the Transport Layer gets it to the correct **application**.

Remember:

> **IP address → Which device?**  
> **Port number → Which application?**

The Transport Layer exists primarily at the **end systems**. Intermediate routers normally operate at the Network Layer rather than implementing the end-to-end transport functions.

---

# 3. TCP 3-Way Handshake

TCP establishes a connection before sending normal application data.

### Step 1 — SYN

Client → Server

```
SYN
```

Client says:

> "I want to establish a TCP connection."

### Step 2 — SYN-ACK

Server → Client

```
SYN + ACK
```

Server says:

> "I received your request, and I also want to establish the connection."

### Step 3 — ACK

Client → Server

```
ACK
```

Client confirms:

> "I received your response."

Now:

```
Connection ESTABLISHED
```

Simple memory trick:

**SYN → SYN-ACK → ACK**

---

# 4. TCP

**TCP = Transmission Control Protocol**

TCP is:

- **Connection-oriented**
- **Reliable**
- **Ordered**
- Uses acknowledgments
- Can retransmit lost data
- Provides flow control
- Generally has more overhead than UDP
# UDP

**UDP = User Datagram Protocol**

UDP is:

- **Connectionless**
- Lightweight
- Faster/simpler in many situations
- No built-in acknowledgment
- No built-in retransmission
- No guarantee of ordered delivery
- Uses an **8-byte header**

Think:

> **UDP = simplicity and low overhead**

Applications can choose UDP when they can tolerate some loss or handle reliability themselves.

---

# 6. SCTP

**SCTP = Stream Control Transmission Protocol**

SCTP is another Transport Layer protocol.

It provides reliable transport and has features such as:

- **Multi-streaming** — multiple independent streams within one association
- **Multi-homing** — an endpoint can use multiple IP addresses/interfaces for resilience

It is less commonly encountered than TCP and UDP in basic networking.

---

# 7. TCP vs UDP

| Feature        | TCP                     | UDP                      |
| -------------- | ----------------------- | ------------------------ |
| Connection     | Connection-oriented     | Connectionless           |
| Reliability    | Reliable                | Best-effort              |
| Ordering       | Yes                     | No guarantee             |
| ACKs           | Yes                     | No                       |
| Retransmission | Yes                     | No                       |
| Flow control   | Yes                     | No                       |
| Header         | 20–60 bytes             | 8 bytes                  |
| Overhead       | Higher                  | Lower                    |
| Speed/latency  | Generally more overhead | Generally lower overhead |
| Data unit      | Segment                 | Datagram                 |
